A Variational Analysis of Kernel Learning with Learnable Linear Transformations
Yang Li, Feng Ruan

Abstract
The classical kernel ridge regression problem aims to find the best fit for the output Y Y as a function of the input data X ∈ ℝ d X\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , with a fixed choice of regularization term imposed by a given choice of a reproducing kernel Hilbert space, such as a Sobolev space. Here we consider a generalization of the kernel ridge regression problem, by introducing an extra matrix parameter U U , which aims to detect the scale parameters and the feature variables in the data, and thereby improve the efficiency of kernel ridge regression. This naturally leads to a nonlinear variational problem to optimize the choice of U U . We study various foundational mathematical aspects of this variational problem, including its Euler-Lagrange equation, continuity and first variation, limiting behavior under degenerate or diverging transformations, and the structure of its local minimizers. Particular attention is given to two data-distribution settings, namely multi-scale and multi-index models, where the learned transformation U U encodes intrinsic scale parameters and the essential low-dimensional feature variables, respectively. Keywords: kernel learning; reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces; asymptotic analysis; variational analysis; partial compactification; Fourier analysis; operator-theoretic methods; variable selection; scale detection Mathematics Subject Classification: . 46E22, 49J99, 68T05. 1 Introduction 1.1 Kernel learning problem The classical version of kernel ridge regression [ 15 ] [ 3 , Chapter 7] [ 38 ] is the following problem. Let X X be a random variable valued in a Euclidean space V ≃ ℝ d V\simeq\mathbb{R}^{d} , and let Y Y be a random variable with 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]<+\infty . One aims to find the minimizer f f of the loss function 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y − f ⁡ ( X ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 , \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y-f(X)|^{2}]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}, where 0 < λ < 1 0<\lambda<1 is a fixed small parameter, and ℋ \mathcal{H} is a fixed a priori choice of reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) induced by a kernel function K K [ 1 ] . The idea is that we wish to find the optimal function f ⁡ ( X ) f(X) which fits Y Y , among a given nice class of functions. The expectation term measures the accuracy of approximation, while the regularization term λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 \frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2} imposes a certain amount of smoothness, which helps to achieve good generalization behavior for new test data. The choice of ℋ \mathcal{H} reflects an a priori guess on the function class for f f , and the effectiveness of this method depends crucially on selecting an appropriate kernel [ 36 ] . In classical kernel ridge regression this kernel K K is fixed in advance, which is problematic when the relevant structure of the data is not known beforehand. This limitation has motivated kernel learning , in which one searches over a parametrized family of kernels { K θ } \{K_{\theta}\} to better adapt to the data distribution [ 25 ] [ 18 , 30 , 41 , 12 , 21 ] (see Section below). This paper studies the special case of optimizing among the family of kernels K U ​ ( x , x ′ ) = K ⁡ ( U ​ x , U ​ x ′ ) , K_{U}(x,x^{\prime})=K(Ux,Ux^{\prime}), parametrized by a learnable linear transformation U ∈ End ⁡ ( V ) := { U : V → V ​ linear map } , {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{U\in\End(V):=\{U:V\to V\text{ linear map}\},}} from the variational calculus perspective. Equivalently, for each U ∈ End ⁡ ( V ) U\in\End(V) we consider I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y − f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 , I(f,U,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\big[|Y-f(UX)|^{2}\big]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}, minimized over f ∈ ℋ f\in\mathcal{H} , and we denote the minimal value by J ⁡ ( U , λ ) = min f ∈ ℋ ⁡ I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) . J(U,\lambda)=\min_{f\in\mathcal{H}}I(f,U,\lambda). Although kernel ridge regression is linear in f f for fixed U U , the dependence of J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) on U U is highly nonlinear. Consequently, optimizing over U U leads to a nonlinear variational problem whose structure is far from obvious. Our goal is to analyze this dependence of U U , and in particular, how to optimize the choice of U U , thereby shedding light on which U U are preferred by the objective J J , and how they extract latent structure in the data. A particularly nice case is when the RKHS ℋ \mathcal{H} is rotationally invariant. In this case, J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) depends only on the inner product Σ = U T ​ U \Sigma=U^{T}U , and the kernel ridge regression with parameter admits an equivalent formulation ( cf. Section below) . One minimizes the loss function ℐ ⁡ ( F , Σ , λ ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y − F ⁡ ( X ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ F ‖ ℋ Σ 2 , \mathcal{I}(F,\Sigma,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y-F(X)|^{2}]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}}^{2}, where the choice of the Hilbert norm varies with Σ \Sigma . Then J ⁡ ( U , λ ) = 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) := min F ∈ ℋ Σ ⁡ ℐ ⁡ ( F , Σ , λ ) . J(U,\lambda)=\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda):=\min_{F\in\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}}\mathcal{I}(F,\Sigma,\lambda). We denote S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} as the space of inner products on V V , which is an open subset of the space of positive semi-definite forms S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} , and we refer to the local minimizers Σ \Sigma of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} as vacua in analogy with physics, where the vacua sometimes refer to local energy minimizing configurations. These vacua correspond to representations favored by the model. One of our principal objectives is to understand the landscape of vacua. Remark 1 . Recent works in machine learning have further drawn connections between analogous kernel learning formulations and two-layer neural network models, see, e.g., [ 30 , 41 , 12 , 16 , 21 ] . While our analysis does not rely on this perspective, the variational framework developed here is compatible with such viewpoints. For readers interested in discussion of these connections—and for an account of how our results interact with this perspective—we refer the reader to the postscript of the companion paper “Gradient Flow in the Kernel Learning Problem” [ 28 ] . Readers interested in these aspects are strongly encouraged to consult those discussion immediately after reading this introduction. 1.2 Scale adaptation and variable selection We now present a sequence of examples highlighting two basic mechanisms in the kernel learning problem: scale adaptation and variable selection . To make the role of scale adaptation explicit, we begin by illustrating the intrinsic tension between approximation and regularization in a fixed RKHS, thereby motivating the need to learn an extra parameter U U . Example 1.1 . Let X ∼ N ⁡ ( 0 , σ 2 ) X\sim N(0,\sigma^{2}) be a normal variable with variance σ 2 \sigma^{2} , and Y = sin ⁡ ( σ − 1 ​ X ) Y=\sin(\sigma^{-1}X) . We first consider the classical kernel ridge regression problem, where ℋ \mathcal{H} is a fixed Sobolev space H s H_{s} with s > 1 2 s>\frac{1}{2} . If we want to achieve good approximation, then f ≈ sin ⁡ ( x / σ ) f\approx\sin(x/\sigma) for | x | ≲ σ |x|\lesssim\sigma . Now if σ \sigma is very large, then this implies that the L 2 L^{2} -norm of f f is very large, of the order σ 1 / 2 \sigma^{1/2} . For a fixed Sobolev space, ‖ f ‖ L 2 ≲ ‖ f ‖ ℋ \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}} , so ‖ f ‖ ℋ ≳ σ 1 / 2 \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\gtrsim\sigma^{1/2} is also very large. For the minimizer f f of the kernel ridge regression problem, 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y − f ⁡ ( X ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ≲ 1 , \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y-f(X)|^{2}]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\lesssim 1, so this can only happen when λ ≲ σ − 1 \lambda\lesssim\sigma^{-1} . If on the other hand σ \sigma is very small, then sin ⁡ ( x / σ ) \sin(x/\sigma) has very high Fourier frequency | ω | ∼ σ − 1 |\omega|\sim\sigma^{-1} , so if the minimizer f ≈ sin ⁡ ( x / σ ) f\approx\sin(x/\sigma) on | x | ≲ σ |x|\lesssim\sigma , we will have ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 ∼ ∫ ( 1 + | ω | 2 ) s ​ | f ^ | 2 ​ 𝑑 ω ≳ σ − 2 ​ s ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 2 ∼ σ 1 − 2 ​ s . \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\sim\int(1+|\omega|^{2})^{s}|\hat{f}|^{2}d\omega\gtrsim\sigma^{-2s}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\sim\sigma^{1-2s}. This would force λ ≲ σ 2 ​ s − 1 \lambda\lesssim\sigma^{2s-1} . The upshot is that as long as σ \sigma is too big or too small, then for fixed regularization parameter λ \lambda , it would be impossible to achieve good approximation. On the other hand, if we vary the choice of the scale parameter U U , or equivalently from the viewpoint of Σ = U T ​ U \Sigma=U^{T}U , we vary the choice of the Hilbert inner product , then for U ∼ σ − 1 U\sim\sigma^{-1} , we can simply take f = sin ⁡ ( x ) f=\sin(x) , which would give a good approximation, at no major cost to the regularization parameter λ \lambda . The upshot is that in order to break the approximation-regularization trade-off, we should aim to learn the appropriate scale parameter, or equivalently to learn the appropriate Hilbert norm . Such a phenomenon offers the first motivation of the kernel learning problem, which is the hope that the scale parameters are reflected in the global minima or vacua of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} . The previous example involves a single characteristic scale σ \sigma . A natural question is what happens when the data exhibits several distinct scales. In such cases, a fixed RKHS faces an even sharper tension: no single choice of norm can simultaneously resolve all scales, and any learned representation must implicitly decide which scales to emphasize. We record two models illustrating this multi-scale structure. Example 1.2 . (Two scale problem) Let V = ℝ V=\mathbb{R} , and X 0 ∈ { 0 , 1 , … ​ m } X_{0}\in\{0,1,\ldots m\} be a discrete variable, which is not directly observable , and we prescribe the probabilities ℙ ⁡ ( X 0 = i ) > 0 \mathbb{P}(X_{0}=i)>0 . We have m m distinct points a 1 , … ​ a m ∈ ℝ a_{1},\ldots a_{m}\in\mathbb{R} . Let X ∼ N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) X\sim N(0,1) conditional on X 0 = 0 X_{0}=0 , and X ∼ N ⁡ ( a i , σ 2 ) X\sim N(a_{i},\sigma^{2}) conditional on X 0 = i X_{0}=i for i = 1 , … ​ m i=1,\ldots m , where 0 < σ ≪ 1 0<\sigma\ll 1 . Let Y = sin ( X ) + ∑ 1 m sin ( X − a i σ ) e − | X − a i | 2 / σ 2 . Y=\sin(X)+\sum_{1}^{m}\sin(\frac{X-a_{i}}{\sigma})e^{-|X-a_{i}|^{2}/\sigma^{2}}. In this problem there are two inherent scales, of order 1 , σ 1,\sigma respectively. Example 1.3 . (Multi-scaled problem) Let V = ℝ V=\mathbb{R} , and X 0 ∈ { 1 , … ​ m } X_{0}\in\{1,\ldots m\} be a discrete variable, which is not directly accessible to the observer , and we prescribe the probabilities ℙ ⁡ ( X 0 = i ) > 0 \mathbb{P}(X_{0}=i)>0 . Let a 1 , … ​ a m ∈ ℝ a_{1},\ldots a_{m}\in\mathbb{R} be distinct points. Let X ∼ N ⁡ ( a i , σ i 2 ) X\sim N(a_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2}) conditional on X 0 = i X_{0}=i for i = 1 , … ​ m i=1,\ldots m , where 0 < σ 1 ≪ … ≪ σ m ≲ 1 0<\sigma_{1}\ll\ldots\ll\sigma_{m}\lesssim 1 have very different orders of magnitudes. Let Y = ∑ 1 m sin ( X − a i σ i ) e − | X − a i | 2 / σ i 2 . Y=\sum_{1}^{m}\sin(\frac{X-a_{i}}{\sigma_{i}})e^{-|X-a_{i}|^{2}/\sigma_{i}^{2}}. The task is to learn Y Y based on observations of ( X , Y ) (X,Y) , without observing X 0 X_{0} . In this problem we have m m very distinct inherent scales, of order σ 1 , … ​ σ m \sigma_{1},\ldots\sigma_{m} . For these multi-scale problems, the central question is to understand the mechanism of scale adaptation : in what sense do the minimizers U U of the kernel learning objective reflect the intrinsic scales of the data. Finally, we turn to the mechanism of variable selection . Example 1.4 . (Variable selection and multi-index problem) Suppose V = W ⊕ W ′ V=W\oplus W^{\prime} is high dimensional, while the subspace W W is low dimensional. We write X = ( X W , X W ′ ) X=(X_{W},X_{W^{\prime}}) , and assume that X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} is independent of both X W X_{W} and Y Y . Then the prediction follows a multi-index model [ 13 ] , namely, 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y | X ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y | X W ] . \mathbb{E}[Y|X]=\mathbb{E}[Y|X_{W}]. One then would hope to fit Y Y by f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) f(UX) , where the image of U ∈ End ⁡ ( V ) U\in\End(V) lands in W W , so that U U captures the essential degrees of freedom X W X_{W} for the prediction of Y Y . If so, one would also expect U U to be low rank. In light of these examples, it is natural to seek a complete description of the vacua Σ \Sigma (resp. U U ). Of particular interest are those vacua whose associated RKHS ℋ Σ \mathcal{H}_{\Sigma} are adapted to the intrinsic scales of the data, as in the multi-scale problem, and to its predictive feature variables, as in the multi-index problem. A fundamental challenge is that 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) (resp. J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) ) is in general non-convex in Σ \Sigma (resp. U U ), so that one is confronted with a rich landscape of stationary points rather than a single canonical minimizer. Thus one is faced with two main problems: • The static problem is to understand how 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) depends on Σ \Sigma , and in particular to understand the landscape of the vacua under a priori structural assumptions about the distribution of X , Y X,Y . As 𝒥 \mathcal{J} is non-convex, in general the vacua are not unique. The main contribution of this paper is to make a foundational study on this static problem. • The dynamical problem is to design a gradient flow on the space S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} of Σ \Sigma which converges to the vacua of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} . This depends on the choice of a Riemannian metric on S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} , and we will identify a canonical choice in the companion paper [ 28 ] with a number of good properties, such as subsequential convergence to stationary points, and monotone reduction of Gaussian noise effect. On the other hand, there are still many challenges ahead to make this into an efficient numerical optimization scheme. 1.3 Overview of this paper For the sake of exposition we focus on the rotationally invariant case, even though many results in this paper apply to more general RKHSs. This paper studies how 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) depends on Σ \Sigma . In particular, we are interested in the following questions: Question 1 . What is the limiting behavior of 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) when Σ \Sigma becomes degenerate, or when Σ \Sigma tends to infinity in the space of inner products? More formally speaking, we want to find a natural partial compactification , namely adding in certain limit points for Σ \Sigma , such that 𝒥 \mathcal{J} extends to a continuous functional. The degenerate Σ \Sigma turns out to be related to variable selection or dimension redction, whereas Σ → + ∞ \Sigma\to+\infty is related to scale detection. Question 2 . How do the vacua of 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) reflect the structural properties of the distribution of ( X , Y ) (X,Y) , such as the scale parameters and the essential degrees of freedom of how Y Y depends on X X ? When should there be several vacua of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} ? Remark 2 . The kernel learning problem is analogous to the 2-layer neural network: the function f f is analogous to the outer layer weights, and minimizing with respect to f f for fixed U U (resp. minimizing with respect to the outer layer weights for fixed inner layer weights) amounts to a linear regression problem; the parameter U U (or Σ \Sigma ) is analogous to the inner layer weights, and the minimization in U U or Σ \Sigma (resp. the inner layer weights) is a genuinely nonlinear problem. We can idealize the 2-layer neural network training as a two time-scale process, with a faster time scale which minimizes the loss function with respect to the outer layer weights while fixing the inner layer weights, and a slower time scale which minimizes with respect to the inner layer weights. The analogous problem is to minimize I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) first with respect to f f , so we obtain J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) (or equivalently 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) ), and then finding the local minimizers(=vacua) of J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) with respect to U U . To summarize, our main question is the analogue of describing the long time limit of the 2-layer neural network training process (with infinite sample data , so the difference between finite sample empirical expectation and the population level expectation can be ignored), and in particular about how the optimal inner layer weights reflect properties of the data distribution ( X , Y ) (X,Y) . This question is significantly different from, and complementary to, the statistical question of ‘how many finite samples does one need to achieve a certain training/test accuracy’. It is worth emphasizing that 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) is in general far from convex , and its vacua far from unique (as we shall prove in some examples). The main point of our results can be summarized in the slogan that ‘ the significant vacua Σ \Sigma of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} knows about the essential degrees of freedom, scale parameters and clustering behavior in X X ’. We now summarize the contents of the paper. Further conceptual discussions are given at the ends of several sections. 1. Section starts with a quick introduction of the kernel ridge regression problem where the RKHS is defined by a translation invariant kernel. Then we characterize the minimizer in terms of an integral equation derived from the Euler-Lagrange equation (Proposition ), and then prove a quantitative estimate on the modulus of continuity of 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) and the minimizer function (Proposition , Lemma ) using the coercivity estimate of the integral equation; this implies in particular the continuous extension of the functional 𝒥 \mathcal{J} to the space of positive semi-definite form S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} . We recall the representer formula for the minimizer of the kernel ridge regression problem when X X takes only finitely many values (Proposition ). Using the law of large numbers , the general distribution case can be approximated with arbitrarily high probability by the case of empirical distributions (Proposition ). 2. Section studies the limiting behavior of 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) when Σ \Sigma tends to infinity in the space of inner products, while remaining bounded on a (possibly empty) proper subspace W ⊂ V W\subset V . The main theorem is Theorem . Perhaps surprisingly, we discover that the answer is sensitive to whether the marginal distribution of X X on the quotient space V / W V/W contains any discrete part. If there is no discrete part, for instance if X X is a continuous variable, then lim 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] \lim\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}] , which is the global supremum of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} . If there is a nontrivial discrete part, then we find that the kernel ridge regression problem decouples to a number of dimensionally reduced problems in the asymptotic limit, whose minimizers become orthogonal in the RKHS asymptotically. The proof technique relies on relating the kernel ridge regression problem to an optimal interpolation problem , and uses a little Fourier analysis. 3. Section focuses on the rotationally invariant kernel case. The main result is the first variation formula of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} in Theorem , which generalizes [ 12 , Lemma 3.6] by relaxing the condition on the kernel function. We provide two arguments under slightly different conditions, one using integral equation techniques, the other using finite measure approximation via the law of large numbers. We then discuss when the first variation formula can be continuously extended to the boundary of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} (Proposition ), and introduce some formal definitions on the partial compactification (Section ). Notable examples of rotationally invariant kernels come from completely monotone functions according to Schoenberg’s theorem (Section ), including Gaussian kernels, Laplace kernels and Sobolev kernels. 4. The vacua are formally defined as the local minima of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} on the partial compactification. There are two kinds of limiting configurations: vacua on the boundary of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} represented by degenerate inner products, and vacua at infinity. The boundary vacua are intimately related to the problem of variable selection (Section ); e.g., Example where Y Y depends on certain components of X X . Applying the first variation formula to the boundary points Σ \Sigma , we derive some criterion to test the local minimizing property (Section ), and identify two opposite effects for and against the local minimizing property (Lemma , ). In Section we revisit the case where X X is a discrete variable taking a finite number of values, and extract the sub-leading effect when Σ → ∞ \Sigma\to\infty . A particular consequence is to exhibit examples for the vacua at infinity. 5. Section takes a more operator theoretic approach to the Euler-Lagrange equation, with an eye towards analyzing multi-scaled problems as in Examples , , where the probability distribution of X X contains several ‘clusters’. We prove various operator norm estimates (Proposition ) and their variants associated to clusters, to the effect that if two clusters are far separated spatially, or if their scale parameters are very different, then the operator products describing the interactions between the clusters have small norm. One can then compare the minimizer of the kernel ridge regression, versus the sum of the minimizers for the kernel ridge regression problems associated to the clusters. Theorems , and Corollary quantify their deviation, which is small when the clusters are far separated. In the opposite direction, if some component of X X concentrates near its conditional expectation value with respect to the inner product Σ \Sigma , then the minimizer f U f_{U} is well approximated by the minimizer for the kernel ridge regression problem associated to a simpler probability distribution of ( X , Y ) (X,Y) , depending on fewer degrees of freedom (Section ). 6. Section formulates the notion of ‘ scale detectors ’, which we consider to be the most important vacua. The task is to relate scale detectors to the information of ( X , Y ) (X,Y) . In the simplest case where X X is a continuous variable whose marginal distributions satisfy a priori bounds on the probability density, and if the global minimum of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} is strictly lower than the infimum of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} on the boundary of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} with a quantitative gap, then one can show that the global minimizing vacuum Σ \Sigma is a scale detector , and all scale detectors in the interior of S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} are uniformly equivalent to each other up to estimable constants (Theorem ). To exhibit a multi-scaled phenomenon we need to allow the probability density of X X to concentrate. Using the machinery developed in this paper, we construct a class of one-dimensional examples with multiple vacua (typical cases being Example ), which turn out to reflect the scale parameters in the distribution of X X (Theorem ). Remark 3 . The exposition in this paper is aimed primarily at a general mathematical readership familiar with analysis and linear algebra, and does not assume any prior background in machine learning. Some results in this paper are also reproduced from [ 12 ] , often in a slightly more general setting. Readers interested in machine learning may wish to consult Appendix , which interprets the main results through the lens of representation learning and discusses their possible implications for two-layer neural network models. 1.4 Related Work The general problem of learning a kernel from data was first discussed in [ 25 ] and has since been developed extensively; see, for example, [ 19 ] . Within this broad literature, a more recent and increasingly active line of work focuses on kernel learning through learnable linear transformations of the input, in which one introduces a matrix U U and considers kernels related to the form K ⁡ ( U ​ x , U ​ x ′ ) K(Ux,Ux^{\prime}) . This class of models has attracted increasing attention in recent years, in part because it admits a natural interpretation as a two-layer architecture: the linear transformation x ↦ U ​ x x\mapsto Ux plays the role of the inner (feature) layer, while the function f f corresponds to the outer layer, in the sense that, for fixed U U , optimization with respect to f f reduces to a linear regression problem. From this perspective, learning the transformation U U induces representation learning in kernel models and bears a close structural analogy to two-layer neural network architectures [ 30 , 16 , 12 , 21 , 41 , 28 ] Existing work within such kernel-based representation learning has led to a variety of formulations: some works prefer to impose explicit constraints on U U (e.g., low-rank), others extend to combinations of multiple transformations { U i } \{U_{i}\} . These studies typically focus on finite-sample generalization guarantees, statistical recovery of predictive subspaces, or the analysis of specific algorithmic procedures. The present paper is situated within this line of work, but adopts a different perspective: rather than analyzing finite-sample behavior or particular algorithms, we study the population-level variational landscape underlying these formulations. We next review representative works in this area and explain how our framework connects to—and differs from—these formulations in scope, perspective, and results. A foundational line of research by Fukumizu, Bach, and Jordan [ 17 , 18 ] introduced low-rank kernel learning under the hard constraint rank ⁡ ( U ) = r \mathrm{rank}(U)=r among others. They proved that, with a vanishing regularization parameter, the empirical global minimizer recovers the correct predictive subspace of dimension r r in the large-sample limit. More recently, Chen et al. [ 12 ] removed the rank constraint and studied the unconstrained kernel learning model, which is the finite sample analogue of the problem considered in the present paper. They established that the empirical global minimizer is statistically consistent in recovering both the predictive subspace and its intrinsic dimension, and demonstrated strong empirical performance relative to classical kernel ridge regression. Related theoretical developments have also shown improved generalization guarantees for global minimizers when an explicit low-rank constraint is imposed on U U [ 21 ] . Beyond these single-transformation models, Follain and Bach [ 16 ] propose a multiple kernel learning formulation to learn kernels of the form ∑ i η i ​ K ​ ( U i ​ x , U i ​ x ′ ) \sum_{i}\eta_{i}K(U_{i}x,U_{i}x^{\prime}) , which can be interpreted as learning multiple candidate representations than a single U U ; their main theoretical results provide high-probability generalization bounds for the empirical global minimizer associated with that kernel. In a parallel algorithmic direction, Radhakrishnan et al. [ 30 ] study kernel-based feature learning through the AGOP (Average Gradient Outer Product) scheme, which adopts the same parametrization f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) f(UX) considered in the present paper. AGOP can be formally viewed as an iterative procedure on the same kernel learning objective J J . It has been analyzed theoretically, leading to improved generalization error bounds over standard kernel methods [ 41 ] , and has also demonstrated strong empirical performance on benchmark datasets [ 30 ] . However, AGOP is not derived from an explicit variational principle, and its precise relationship to the optimization landscape of J J remains largely unexplored, presenting an interesting direction for future research [ 30 , 31 ] . Complementary to these existing developments, our work studies the kernel learning objective J J entirely at the population level and independently of any specific algorithmic procedure . We develop a variational analysis of the population functional J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) , establishing its continuity properties, first-variation formula, and limiting behavior under degenerate and diverging transformations U U for various choices of data distributions ( X , Y ) (X,Y) and RKHS ℋ \mathcal{H} . This analysis yields a detailed description of the landscape of J J , including both its global minimizers and its local minimizers (vacua), thereby revealing which representations are intrinsically preferred by this variational formulation in the infinite-sample limit . Particular attention is given to two data-distribution settings of special interest, namely multi-index and multi-scale models. In the former, the learned transformation U U recovers the essential predictive feature variables, while in the latter it captures intrinsic scale information in the underlying data distribution—a representation-learning phenomenon that, to the best of our knowledge, has not been studied in the existing literature. Together, these results demonstrate how the variational structure of the kernel learning problem can favor representations that capture multi-index and multi-scale structure in the data. At the same time, several natural variational questions remain open, including a more complete higher-order characterization of the variational landscape and extensions of the present theory to broader classes of data distributions and representation-learning phenomena (see the remarks throughout the paper). Finally, beyond its static variational implications, the framework developed here also provides a natural starting point for studying associated dynamical questions, such as gradient-flow dynamics [ 28 ] , whose formulation fundamentally relies on the first-variation analysis developed in this paper. The study of such dynamical questions, however, lies beyond the scope of the present work. 2 Kernel ridge regression problem 2.1 Translation invariant RKHS We now give a quick review of translation invariant reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS); more general references on RKHS can be found in [ 1 ] [ 15 ] . Let ℋ \mathcal{H} be a Hilbert space of complex valued functions on a d d -dimensional Euclidean space V ≃ ℝ d V\simeq\mathbb{R}^{d} , associated to a translation invariant kernel. The space ℋ \mathcal{H} is defined in terms of the Fourier transform by ℋ = { f : ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 = ∫ V ∗ | f ^ | 2 ​ ( ω ) k V ​ ( ω ) ​ 𝑑 ω < + ∞ } , \mathcal{H}=\{f:\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\int_{V^{*}}\frac{|\hat{f}|^{2}(\omega)}{k_{V}(\omega)}d\omega<+\infty\}, (1) where we require the function k V k_{V} to be strictly positive, and satisfies the L 1 L^{1} -integrability condition ∫ V ∗ k V ​ ( ω ) ​ 𝑑 ω = 1 . \int_{V^{*}}k_{V}(\omega)d\omega=1. (2) At this generality, we do not need to assume that k V k_{V} is smooth or rotationally invariant, although such assumptions are frequently used in practice. The point of working in this generality is that one is forced to think through what are the essential structures. Clearly the Hilbert space is preserved under the translation f ↦ f ( ⋅ + a ) f\mapsto f(\cdot+a) for any a ∈ V a\in V . Notation . Our convention for the Fourier transform pair is f ^ ​ ( ω ) = ∫ ℝ N f ⁡ ( x ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ x , ω ⟩ ​ 𝑑 x , f ⁡ ( x ) = ∫ ( ℝ N ) ∗ f ^ ​ ( ω ) ​ e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ x , ω ⟩ ​ 𝑑 ω . \hat{f}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(x)e^{-2\pi i\langle x,\omega\rangle}dx,\quad f(x)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{*}}\hat{f}(\omega)e^{2\pi i\langle x,\omega\rangle}d\omega. (3) Notice the Fourier variables naturally live in the dual space. The reason for the 2 ​ π ​ i 2\pi i choice is that it makes the least appearance of dimensional constants. The integrability condition on k V k_{V} ensures the following Sobolev embedding properties on the function space ℋ \mathcal{H} . Lemma 2.1 . (Sobolev embedding) For any f ∈ ℋ f\in\mathcal{H} , we have ‖ f ‖ L ∞ ≤ ‖ f ‖ ℋ \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}} , and f f is continuous, and | f | → 0 |f|\to 0 at infinity. Proof. We note by Cauchy-Schwarz that ‖ f ‖ L ∞ 2 ≤ ‖ f ^ ‖ L 1 2 ≤ ( ∫ V ∗ | f ^ | 2 k V ​ 𝑑 ω ) ​ ( ∫ V ∗ k V ​ 𝑑 ω ) ≤ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 . \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\leq\left\lVert\hat{f}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}^{2}\leq(\int_{V^{*}}\frac{|\hat{f}|^{2}}{k_{V}}d\omega)(\int_{V^{*}}k_{V}d\omega)\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}. (4) Since f ^ \hat{f} is L 1 L^{1} , the continuity of f f follows from dominated convergence. By the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma | f | → 0 |f|\to 0 at infinity. ∎ In particular, there is a continuous embedding of ℋ \mathcal{H} into the space of continuous functions C 0 C^{0} , which means that ℋ \mathcal{H} is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS). We define the kernel function K ⁡ ( x ) = ∫ V ∗ e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ x , ω ⟩ ​ k V ​ ( ω ) ​ 𝑑 ω , K(x)=\int_{V^{*}}e^{2\pi i\langle x,\omega\rangle}k_{V}(\omega)d\omega, (5) and let K ⁡ ( x , y ) = K ⁡ ( x − y ) K(x,y)=K(x-y) , so in particular K ⁡ ( x , y ) = K ⁡ ( y , x ) ¯ K(x,y)=\overline{K(y,x)} . The continuous functions K ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) K(x,\cdot) lie in ℋ \mathcal{H} , and have Fourier transform k V ​ ( ω ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ ω , x ⟩ k_{V}(\omega)e^{-2\pi i\langle\omega,x\rangle} . By Riemann-Lebesgue, K ⁡ ( x ) → 0 K(x)\to 0 as | x | → ∞ |x|\to\infty . These functions are known as reproducing kernels. Each f ∈ ℋ f\in\mathcal{H} can be represented as f ⁡ ( x ) = ( f , K ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) ) ℋ . f(x)=(f,K(x,\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}. (6) Lemma 2.2 . The functions in the unit ball of ℋ \mathcal{H} have uniform modulus of continuity. Proof. By the reproducing kernel property, | f ⁡ ( x ) − f ⁡ ( y ) | = | ( f , K ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) − K ⁡ ( y , ⋅ ) ) ℋ | ≤ ‖ f ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ K ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) − K ⁡ ( y , ⋅ ) ‖ ℋ , |f(x)-f(y)|=|(f,K(x,\cdot)-K(y,\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}|\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\left\lVert K(x,\cdot)-K(y,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}, and as | y − x | → 0 |y-x|\to 0 , ‖ K ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) − K ⁡ ( y , ⋅ ) ‖ ℋ 2 = K ⁡ ( x , x ) + K ⁡ ( y , y ) − 2 ​ Re ​ ( K ⁡ ( x , y ) ) = 2 − 2 ​ Re ​ K ​ ( x − y ) → 0 . \left\lVert K(x,\cdot)-K(y,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=K(x,x)+K(y,y)-2\text{Re}(K(x,y))=2-2\text{Re}K(x-y)\to 0. Hence the functions f f are equicontinuous. ∎ 2.2 Minimization problem Let X X be a random variable taking value in the Euclidean space V ≃ ℝ d V\simeq\mathbb{R}^{d} , and Y Y be a complex valued random variable with 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]<+\infty . Following [ 15 , section 6] , we consider the following minimization problem, which we will refer to as kernel ridge regression . The loss function is I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ | Y − f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 , I(f,U,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}|Y-f(UX)|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}, (7) depending on the parameters U ∈ End ⁡ ( V ) U\in\End(V) and λ ∈ ( 0 , + ∞ ) \lambda\in(0,+\infty) , and we seek the minimizer for J ⁡ ( U , λ ) = inf f ∈ ℋ I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) . J(U,\lambda)=\inf_{f\in\mathcal{H}}I(f,U,\lambda). Notice that due to the Sobolev embedding, every f ∈ ℋ f\in\mathcal{H} is a continuous function on V V , so I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) is always well defined. The existence of a unique minimizer follows from the Riesz representation theorem [ 15 , Proposition 7] . Lemma 2.3 . (Trivial upper bound) We always have J ⁡ ( U , λ ) ≤ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] J(U,\lambda)\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}] . If 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y | U ​ X ] \mathbb{E}[Y|UX] is nonzero, then the strict inequality holds. Proof. Plug in the test function f = 0 f=0 , then I ⁡ ( 0 , U , λ ) ≥ J ⁡ ( U , λ ) I(0,U,\lambda)\geq J(U,\lambda) . The equality is achieved only when f = 0 f=0 is the minimizer, which by the Euler-Lagrange equation implies that 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y | U ​ X ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[Y|UX]=0 . ∎ Lemma 2.4 . (Trivial lower bound) I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ Var ​ ( Y | U ​ X ) ] + 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y | U ​ X ] − f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 . I(f,U,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\text{\rm Var}(Y|UX)]+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|\mathbb{E}[Y|UX]-f(UX)|^{2}]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}. In particular J ⁡ ( U , λ ) ≥ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ Var ​ ( Y | U ​ X ) ] . J(U,\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\text{\rm Var}(Y|UX)]. 2.3 Euler-Lagrange equation Lemma 2.5 . (Euler-Lagrange) The minimizer f U f_{U} for I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) satisfies that 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( Y − f U ​ ( U ​ X ) ) ​ g ¯ ​ ( U ​ X ) ] = λ ​ ( f U , g ) ℋ , ∀ g ∈ ℋ . \mathbb{E}[(Y-f_{U}(UX))\bar{g}(UX)]=\lambda(f_{U},g)_{\mathcal{H}},\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{H}. We plug in the kernel function K ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) K(x,\cdot) as the test function g g in the Euler-Lagrange equation, to deduce the integral equation f U ​ ( x ) = ( f U , K ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) ) ℋ = 1 λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ( Y − f U ​ ( U ​ X ) ) ​ K ⁡ ( x , U ​ X ) ¯ ] . f_{U}(x)=(f_{U},K(x,\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}[(Y-f_{U}(UX))\overline{K(x,UX)}]. (8) By plugging the test function f U f_{U} into the Euler-Lagrange equation, we deduce 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ​ f U ​ ( U ​ X ) ¯ ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] + λ ​ ( f U , f U ) ℋ \mathbb{E}[Y\overline{f_{U}(UX)}]=\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)|^{2}]+\lambda(f_{U},f_{U})_{\mathcal{H}} whence J ⁡ ( U , λ ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ Y ​ f U ​ ( U ​ X ) ¯ ] = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ Y ¯ ​ f U ​ ( U ​ X ) ] . J(U,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[Y\overline{f_{U}(UX)}]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\bar{Y}f_{U}(UX)]. (9) Notation . We denote the (semi)-norm ‖ f ‖ 𝔼 2 = 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f ⁡ ( X ) | 2 ] \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{E}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[|f(X)|^{2}] for functions f f defined on the support of X X . Proposition 2.6 . The function f U ∘ U f_{U}\circ U is characterized as the unique finite norm solution to the integral equation F ⁡ ( x ) = 1 λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ( Y − F ⁡ ( X ) ) ​ K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( x − X ) ) ¯ ] . F(x)=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}[(Y-F(X))\overline{K(U(x-X))}]. Proof. For later application, we consider the more general integral equation f ⁡ ( x ) + 1 λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ f ⁡ ( X ) ​ K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( x − X ) ) ¯ ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ h ⁡ ( x , X ) ] , f(x)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}[f(X)\overline{K(U(x-X))}]=\mathbb{E}[h(x,X)], where 𝔼 ⁡ [ | h ⁡ ( X ′ , X ) | 2 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|h(X^{\prime},X)|^{2}]<+\infty , and X ′ X^{\prime} is an independent copy of X X . We claim that the equation has a unique finite norm solution, and it satisfies the coercivity estimate ‖ f ‖ 𝔼 2 ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | h ⁡ ( X ′ , X ) | 2 ] . \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{E}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[|h(X^{\prime},X)|^{2}]. (10) We notice that for any f f with ‖ f ‖ 𝔼 < + ∞ \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{E}}<+\infty , 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( X ) ​ K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( x − X ) ) ¯ ​ f ⁡ ( X ′ ) ¯ ] = ∫ V ∗ 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( X ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) , ω ⟩ ​ f ⁡ ( X ′ ) ¯ ] ​ k V ​ ( ω ) ​ 𝑑 ω = ∫ V ∗ | 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( X ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ U ​ X , ω ⟩ ] | 2 ​ k V ​ ( ω ) ​ d ω ≥ 0 , \begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[f(X)\overline{K(U(x-X))}\overline{f(X^{\prime})}]\\ =&\int_{V^{*}}\mathbb{E}[f(X)e^{-2\pi i\langle U(X-X^{\prime}),\omega\rangle}\overline{f(X^{\prime})}]k_{V}(\omega)d\omega\\ =&\int_{V^{*}}|\mathbb{E}[f(X)e^{-2\pi i\langle UX,\omega\rangle}]|^{2}k_{V}(\omega)d\omega\geq 0,\end{split} (11) and the linear functional g ↦ 𝔼 ⁡ [ h ⁡ ( X ′ , X ) ​ g ​ ( X ′ ) ] g\mapsto\mathbb{E}[h(X^{\prime},X)g(X^{\prime})] has norm bounded by 𝔼 ​ [ | h ⁡ ( X ′ , X ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 \mathbb{E}[|h(X^{\prime},X)|^{2}]^{1/2} with respect to the Hilbert inner product ‖ f ‖ 𝔼 2 ≤ ‖ f ‖ 𝔼 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( X ) ​ K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( x − X ) ) ¯ ​ f ⁡ ( X ′ ) ¯ ] , \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{E}}^{2}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{E}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[f(X)\overline{K(U(x-X))}\overline{f(X^{\prime})}], so the claim follows from Riesz representation theorem. In our setting, the integral equation on f U ∘ U f_{U}\circ U follows from Euler-Lagrange ( ). The coercivity estimate applied to the homogeneous equation implies uniqueness. ∎ The integral equation admits a natural interpretation. The quantity Y − F ⁡ ( X ) Y-F(X) represents the prediction residual. The value of F ⁡ ( x ) F(x) is obtained by averaging these residuals against the kernel profile centered at x x . 2.4 Quantitative continuity in parameter We now prove some quantitative continuity in U U for the minimal value J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) and minimizer f U f_{U} of I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) . Lemma 2.7 . For any U , U ~ U,\tilde{U} , there is a uniform bound 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) − f U ~ ​ ( U ~ ​ X ) | 2 ] ≤ 1 λ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X ′ − X ) ) − K ⁡ ( U ~ ​ ( X ′ − X ) ) | 2 ] , \mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)-f_{\tilde{U}}(\tilde{U}X)|^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|K(U(X^{\prime}-X))-K(\tilde{U}(X^{\prime}-X))|^{2}], (12) where X ′ X^{\prime} denotes an independent copy of X X . Proof. By the integral equation for f U ∘ U f_{U}\circ U and f U ~ ∘ U ~ f_{\tilde{U}}\circ\tilde{U} , the difference f ⁡ ( x ) = f U ~ ​ ( U ~ ​ x ) − f U ​ ( U ​ x ) f(x)=f_{\tilde{U}}(\tilde{U}x)-f_{U}(Ux) satisfies f ⁡ ( x ) + 1 λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ f ⁡ ( X ) ​ K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( x − X ) ) ¯ ] = 1 λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ( Y − f U ~ ​ ( U ~ ​ X ) ) ​ g ⁡ ( x − X ) ¯ ] , f(x)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}[f(X)\overline{K(U(x-X))}]=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}[(Y-f_{\tilde{U}}(\tilde{U}X))\overline{g(x-X)}], (13) for g ⁡ ( x ) = K ⁡ ( U ~ ​ x ) − K ⁡ ( U ​ x ) g(x)=K(\tilde{U}x)-K(Ux) . By applying the coercivity estimate ( ), ‖ f ‖ 𝔼 2 ≤ 1 λ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y − f U ~ ​ ( U ~ ​ X ) | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | g ⁡ ( X ′ − X ) | 2 ] ≤ 1 λ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | g ⁡ ( X ′ − X ) | 2 ] , \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{E}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Y-f_{\tilde{U}}(\tilde{U}X)|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|g(X^{\prime}-X)|^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|g(X^{\prime}-X)|^{2}], where the second inequality uses the trivial upper bound Lemma . ∎ Proposition 2.8 . (Quantitative continuity of J J ) For any U , U ~ U,\tilde{U} , there is a uniform bound | J ⁡ ( U , λ ) − J ⁡ ( U ~ , λ ) | ≤ 1 2 ​ λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X ′ − X ) ) − K ⁡ ( U ~ ​ ( X ′ − X ) ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 , |J(U,\lambda)-J(\tilde{U},\lambda)|\leq\frac{1}{2\lambda}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|K(U(X^{\prime}-X))-K(\tilde{U}(X^{\prime}-X))|^{2}]^{1/2}, where X ′ X^{\prime} denotes an independent copy of X X . Proof. Using the formula ( ), and Cauchy-Schwarz, | J ⁡ ( U , λ ) − J ⁡ ( U ~ , λ ) | ≤ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] 1 / 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) − f U ~ ​ ( U ~ ​ X ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 , |J(U,\lambda)-J(\tilde{U},\lambda)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)-f_{\tilde{U}}(\tilde{U}X)|^{2}]^{1/2}, so the Proposition follows from ( ). ∎ Corollary 2.9 . The minimizer f U f_{U} depends continuously on U U , in the Hilbert space ℋ \mathcal{H} -norm. Proof. Since I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) is quadratic in f f with minimizer f U f_{U} , we have I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) = 𝒬 ⁡ ( f − f U ) + J ⁡ ( U , λ ) , I(f,U,\lambda)=\mathcal{Q}(f-f_{U})+J(U,\lambda), where 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is the quadratic term, bounded below by λ 2 ​ ‖ f − f U ‖ ℋ 2 \frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f-f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2} . The same holds with U U replaced by any other U ~ \tilde{U} . In particular, when U ~ → U \tilde{U}\to U , J ⁡ ( U ~ , λ ) + λ 2 ​ ‖ f U − f U ~ ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ I ⁡ ( f U , U ~ , λ ) → I ⁡ ( f U , U , λ ) = J ⁡ ( U , λ ) , J(\tilde{U},\lambda)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f_{U}-f_{\tilde{U}}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leq I(f_{U},\tilde{U},\lambda)\to I(f_{U},U,\lambda)=J(U,\lambda), Hence lim sup U ~ → U λ 2 ​ ‖ f U − f U ~ ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ lim U ~ → U ( J ⁡ ( U , λ ) − J ⁡ ( U ~ , λ ) ) = 0 , \limsup_{\tilde{U}\to U}\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f_{U}-f_{\tilde{U}}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\lim_{\tilde{U}\to U}(J(U,\lambda)-J(\tilde{U},\lambda))=0, so ‖ f U − f U ~ ‖ ℋ → 0 \left\lVert f_{U}-f_{\tilde{U}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\to 0 . ∎ 2.5 Representer theorem for finite atomic measures In the simplest case X X takes value in a finite number of points { a 1 , … ​ a m } ⊂ V \{a_{1},\ldots a_{m}\}\subset V , with ℙ ⁡ ( X = a i ) = p i \mathbb{P}(X=a_{i})=p_{i} , and Y = y i Y=y_{i} conditional on X = a i X=a_{i} . Then the representer theorem [ 3 , Section 7.2] [ 15 , Proposition 8] gives an explicit formula for J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) in terms of the kernel functions. We write M = ( M i ​ j ) M=(M_{ij}) as the m × m m\times m inner product matrix with entries M i ​ j = K ⁡ ( U ​ a i , U ​ a j ) = K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( a i − a j ) ) M_{ij}=K(Ua_{i},Ua_{j})=K(U(a_{i}-a_{j})) and let M − 1 M^{-1} denote its inverse matrix whenever M M is invertible, which is the case when the points { U ​ a i } 1 m \{Ua_{i}\}_{1}^{m} are distinct from each other . We further write P = diag ​ ( p 1 , … ​ p m ) P=\text{diag}(p_{1},\ldots p_{m}) encoding the probability of the atoms at a i a_{i} . Proposition 2.10 . [ 15 , Proposition 8] (Representer theorem) Assume that M M is invertible. Then J ⁡ ( U , λ ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ 1 m p i ​ | y i | 2 − 1 2 ​ ∑ i , j = 1 m ( λ ​ M − 1 + P ) i ​ j − 1 ​ p i ​ y i ​ p j ​ y ¯ j . J(U,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1}^{m}p_{i}|y_{i}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}(\lambda M^{-1}+P)^{-1}_{ij}p_{i}y_{i}p_{j}\bar{y}_{j}. (14) Proof. We minimize the functional I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ 1 m p i ​ | y i − f ⁡ ( U ​ a i ) | 2 + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 . I(f,U,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1}^{m}p_{i}|y_{i}-f(Ua_{i})|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}. We first fix the m m numbers f ⁡ ( U ​ a i ) f(Ua_{i}) , and minimize ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2} . This is an optimal interpolation problem, and the solution is f = ∑ i , j = 1 m ( M − 1 ) j ​ i ​ f ​ ( U ​ a j ) ​ K ​ ( U ​ a i , ⋅ ) , ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 = ∑ i , j = 1 m ( M − 1 ) i ​ j ​ f ​ ( U ​ a i ) ​ f ⁡ ( U ​ a j ) ¯ . f=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}(M^{-1})_{ji}f(Ua_{j})K(Ua_{i},\cdot),\quad\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}(M^{-1})_{ij}f(Ua_{i})\overline{f(Ua_{j})}. In the second step, we minimize with respect to f ⁡ ( U ​ a i ) f(Ua_{i}) ; this is a finite dimensional linear regression problem. The minimizer satisfies f ⁡ ( U ​ a i ) = ∑ j = 1 m ( λ ​ M − 1 + P ) j ​ i − 1 ​ p j ​ y j , f(Ua_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\lambda M^{-1}+P)^{-1}_{ji}p_{j}y_{j}, (15) whence by ( ) J ⁡ ( U , λ ) = min f ⁡ I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ p i ​ | y i | 2 − 1 2 ​ ∑ ( λ ​ M − 1 + P ) i ​ j − 1 ​ p i ​ y i ​ p j ​ y ¯ j , J(U,\lambda)=\min_{f}I(f,U,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\sum p_{i}|y_{i}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum(\lambda M^{-1}+P)^{-1}_{ij}p_{i}y_{i}p_{j}\bar{y}_{j}, as required. ∎ 2.6 Law of large numbers Suppose we take m m i.i.d. observations of ( X , Y ) (X,Y) , then the empirical expectation is defined by 𝔼 m ​ [ F ⁡ ( X , Y ) ] = 1 m ​ ∑ 1 m F ⁡ ( X ( i ) , Y ( i ) ) . \mathbb{E}_{m}[F(X,Y)]=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{1}^{m}F(X_{(i)},Y_{(i)}). Suppose that 𝔼 ⁡ [ | F ⁡ ( X , Y ) | ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|F(X,Y)|]<+\infty , then the law of large numbers implies that almost surely lim m → + ∞ 𝔼 m ​ [ F ⁡ ( X , Y ) ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( X , Y ) ] . \lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{m}[F(X,Y)]=\mathbb{E}[F(X,Y)]. By replacing 𝔼 \mathbb{E} with the empirical expectation 𝔼 m \mathbb{E}_{m} , we can define the empirical version I m ​ ( f , U , λ ) I_{m}(f,U,\lambda) of I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) , whose minimizer is f U , m f_{U,m} , and the minimal value is J m ​ ( U , λ ) J_{m}(U,\lambda) . We first show the almost sure convergence of the empirical I m ​ ( f , U , λ ) I_{m}(f,U,\lambda) to I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) . Lemma 2.11 . Suppose 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]<+\infty . For every constant M < ∞ M<\infty , almost surely lim m → + ∞ sup f ∈ ℋ : ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ M sup U : | U | ≤ M | 𝔼 m [ | f ( U X ) − Y | 2 ] − 𝔼 [ | Y − f ( U X ) | 2 ] | = 0 . \lim_{m\to+\infty}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}:\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq M}\sup_{U:|U|\leq M}|\mathbb{E}_{m}[|f(UX)-Y|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[|Y-f(UX)|^{2}]|=0. As a consequence, I m ​ ( f , U , λ ) → I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I_{m}(f,U,\lambda)\to I(f,U,\lambda) converge uniformly in f ∈ { f : ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ M } f\in\{f:\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq M\} and U ∈ { U : | U | ≤ M } U\in\{U:|U|\leq M\} . Proof. Let ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 be a small number. For the random variable X X we can first find some large R R depending on ϵ \epsilon , such that lim m → ∞ 𝔼 m ​ [ 𝟙 | X | ≥ R ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝟙 | X | ≥ R ] ≤ ϵ 2 \lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{m}[\mathbbm{1}_{|X|\geq R}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{|X|\geq R}]\leq\epsilon^{2} . We then notice that the class of functions f ∘ U f\circ U with a given bound M M is equicontinuous and uniformly bounded by M 1 / 2 M^{1/2} , so by Arzela-Ascoli, there is a finite ϵ \epsilon -net g 1 , … ​ g N g_{1},\ldots g_{N} , such that any f ∘ U f\circ U in this class is ϵ \epsilon -close in C 0 C^{0} -norm to some g i g_{i} on the compact set { | x | ≤ R } \{|x|\leq R\} . By the law of large numbers applied to | Y − g i ​ ( X ) | 2 |Y-g_{i}(X)|^{2} , almost surely we have lim m → ∞ max 1 ≤ i ≤ N | 𝔼 m ​ | Y − g i ​ ( X ) | 2 − 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y − g i ​ ( X ) | 2 ] | = 0 . \lim_{m\to\infty}\max_{1\leq i\leq N}|\mathbb{E}_{m}|Y-g_{i}(X)|^{2}-\mathbb{E}[|Y-g_{i}(X)|^{2}]|=0. We note that f , g i f,g_{i} are all uniformly bounded in C 0 C^{0} norm by M 1 / 2 M^{1/2} . By separately estimating the contribution for | X | ≤ R |X|\leq R and | X | ≥ R |X|\geq R , we see that almost surely, | 𝔼 m ​ [ | f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) − Y | 2 ] − 𝔼 m ​ [ | g i ​ ( U ​ X ) − Y | 2 ] | ≤ C ​ ϵ ​ ( 𝔼 m ​ [ | Y | + | f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | + | g i ​ ( U ​ X ) | ] ) + C ⁡ ( 𝔼 m ​ [ ( | Y ​ | + | f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | + | ​ g i ​ ( U ​ X ) | ) ​ 𝟙 | X | ≥ R ] CLOSE ≤ C ​ ϵ + C ​ ϵ ​ 𝔼 m ​ [ | Y | ] + C ​ ( 𝔼 m ​ [ | Y | 2 + | f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 + | g i ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ) 1 / 2 ​ 𝔼 m ​ [ 𝟙 | X | ≥ R ] 1 / 2 ≤ C ​ ϵ + C ​ ϵ ​ ( 𝔼 m ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ) 1 / 2 , \begin{split}&|\mathbb{E}_{m}[|f(UX)-Y|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{m}[|g_{i}(UX)-Y|^{2}]|\\ &\leq C\epsilon(\mathbb{E}_{m}[|Y|+|f(UX)|+|g_{i}(UX)|])+C(\mathbb{E}_{m}[(|Y|+|f(UX)|+|g_{i}(UX)|)\mathbbm{1}_{|X|\geq R}]\\ &\leq C\epsilon+C\epsilon\mathbb{E}_{m}[|Y|]+C(\mathbb{E}_{m}[|Y|^{2}+|f(UX)|^{2}+|g_{i}(UX)|^{2}])^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{m}[\mathbbm{1}_{|X|\geq R}]^{1/2}\\ &\leq C\epsilon+C\epsilon(\mathbb{E}_{m}[|Y|^{2}])^{1/2},\end{split} where C < ∞ C<\infty depends on M M but not on f , g i f,g_{i} . A similar estimate holds with 𝔼 m \mathbb{E}_{m} replaced by 𝔼 \mathbb{E} . Since ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 is arbitrary, and 𝔼 m ​ [ | Y | 2 ] → 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ] < ∞ \mathbb{E}_{m}[|Y|^{2}]\to\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]<\infty , the lemma follows. ∎ We next show the almost sure convergence of the empirical minimizers. Proposition 2.12 . Suppose 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]<+\infty . For any compact subset 𝒰 \mathcal{U} of U U , the following event holds almost surely: as m → + ∞ m\to+\infty , the minimizer f U , m ∈ ℋ f_{U,m}\in\mathcal{H} (resp. J m ​ ( U , λ ) J_{m}(U,\lambda) ) converges uniformly in U U to f U ∈ ℋ f_{U}\in\mathcal{H} (resp. J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) ). Proof. By the law of large numbers, almost surely ‖ f U , m ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ λ − 1 ​ 𝔼 m ​ [ | Y | 2 ] → λ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] < + ∞ . \left\lVert f_{U,m}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\lambda^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{m}[|Y|^{2}]\to\lambda^{-1}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]<+\infty. Hence, for any δ > 0 \delta>0 , we can select M < ∞ M<\infty such that with probability at least ≥ 1 − δ \geq 1-\delta , M M serves as a uniform bound on ‖ f U , m ‖ ℋ 2 \left\lVert f_{U,m}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2} and | U | |U| in the compact set 𝒰 \mathcal{U} . By applying Lemma for this M M , we deduce that sup U ∈ 𝒰 | I m ​ ( f U , m , U , λ ) − I ⁡ ( f U , m , U , λ ) | → 0 ( m → ∞ ) \sup_{U\in\mathcal{U}}|I_{m}(f_{U,m},U,\lambda)-I(f_{U,m},U,\lambda)|\to 0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (m\to\infty) occurs with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta . Since δ > 0 \delta>0 is arbitrary, this means that it also occurs almost surely. Similarly, we can deduce that almost surely, sup U ∈ 𝒰 | I m ​ ( f U , U , λ ) − I ⁡ ( f U , U , λ ) | → 0 ( m → ∞ ) . \sup_{U\in\mathcal{U}}|I_{m}(f_{U},U,\lambda)-I(f_{U},U,\lambda)|\to 0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (m\to\infty). Since I m ​ ( f , U , λ ) I_{m}(f,U,\lambda) is quadratic in f f with minimizer f U , m f_{U,m} , we have I m ​ ( f , U , λ ) = 𝒬 ⁡ ( f − f U , m ) + J m ​ ( U , λ ) , I_{m}(f,U,\lambda)=\mathcal{Q}(f-f_{U,m})+J_{m}(U,\lambda), where 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is the quadratic term, bounded below by λ 2 ​ ‖ f − f U , m ‖ ℋ 2 \frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f-f_{U,m}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2} . This leads to the following inequality: J m ​ ( U , λ ) + λ 2 ​ ‖ f U − f U , m ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ I m ​ ( f U , U , λ ) ≤ I ⁡ ( f U , U , λ ) + | ( I m − I ) ​ ( f U , U , λ ) | . J_{m}(U,\lambda)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f_{U}-f_{U,m}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leq I_{m}(f_{U},U,\lambda)\leq I(f_{U},U,\lambda)+|(I_{m}-I)(f_{U},U,\lambda)|. Since I ⁡ ( f U , U , λ ) = J ⁡ ( U , λ ) I(f_{U},U,\lambda)=J(U,\lambda) , this becomes: J m ​ ( U , λ ) + λ 2 ​ ‖ f U − f U , m ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ J ⁡ ( U , λ ) + | ( I m − I ) ​ ( f U , U , λ ) | . J_{m}(U,\lambda)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f_{U}-f_{U,m}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leq J(U,\lambda)+|(I_{m}-I)(f_{U},U,\lambda)|. Similarly we deduce the reverse inequality J ⁡ ( U , λ ) + λ 2 ​ ‖ f U − f U , m ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ J m ​ ( U , λ ) + | ( I m − I ) ​ ( f U , m , U , λ ) | . \begin{split}J(U,\lambda)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f_{U}-f_{U,m}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leq J_{m}(U,\lambda)+|(I_{m}-I)(f_{U,m},U,\lambda)|.\\ \end{split} Recall that | ( I m − I ) ​ ( f U , U , λ ) | |(I_{m}-I)(f_{U},U,\lambda)| and | ( I m − I ) ​ ( f U , m , U , λ ) | |(I_{m}-I)(f_{U,m},U,\lambda)| converge uniformly to zero in U ∈ 𝒰 U\in\mathcal{U} as m → ∞ m\to\infty . Combining these bounds, we obtain that sup U ∈ 𝒰 { | J m ​ ( U , λ ) − J ⁡ ( U , λ ) | + λ 2 ​ ‖ f U − f U , m ‖ ℋ 2 } → 0 ( m → ∞ ) . \sup_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big\{|J_{m}(U,\lambda)-J(U,\lambda)|+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f_{U}-f_{U,m}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\big\}\to 0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (m\to\infty). This shows the uniform convergence of both the minimizers and the minimum values in U U . ∎ Remark 4 . As a caveat, the uniform convergence of the empirical objective to the population objective assumes U U lies in a bounded subset. We shall see later that the behavior of J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) for very large U U is sensitive to the distinction between continuous and discrete distribution for X X , hence the empirical vs. population minimizers may behave in qualitatively different ways. 3 Asymptotic regime We will be interested in the following question: Question 3 . For fixed λ \lambda , how does the minimum and the minimizer depend on U U as U U tends to infinity? More precisely, we fix an orthogonal direct sum decomposition V = W ⊕ W ′ V=W\oplus W^{\prime} with respect to | ⋅ | V 2 |\cdot|_{V}^{2} , which induces a direct sum decomposition of the dual space V ∗ = W ∗ ⊕ W ′ ⁣ ∗ . V^{*}=W^{*}\oplus W^{\prime*}. Thus we can write x ∈ V x\in V and ω ∈ V ∗ \omega\in V^{*} as x = ( x W , x W ′ ) , ω = ( ω W , ω W ′ ) , ⟨ x , ω ⟩ = ⟨ x W , ω W ⟩ + ⟨ x W ′ , ω W ′ ⟩ . x=(x_{W},x_{W^{\prime}}),\quad\omega=(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}}),\quad\langle x,\omega\rangle=\langle x_{W},\omega_{W}\rangle+\langle x_{W^{\prime}},\omega_{W^{\prime}}\rangle. Then a general endomorphism U ∈ End ⁡ ( V ) U\in\End(V) has 4 components, which can be written in matrix form as U = [ U 11 , U 12 U 21 , U 22 ] ∈ [ End ⁡ ( W ) , Hom ⁡ ( W ′ , W ) Hom ⁡ ( W , W ′ ) , End ⁡ ( W ′ ) ] . U=\begin{bmatrix}U_{11},&U_{12}\\ U_{21},&U_{22}\end{bmatrix}\in\begin{bmatrix}\End(W),&\Hom(W^{\prime},W)\\ \Hom(W,W^{\prime}),&\End(W^{\prime})\end{bmatrix}. (16) The action of U U on x = ( x W , x W ′ ) x=(x_{W},x_{W^{\prime}}) is U ​ x = ( U 11 ​ x W + U 12 ​ x W ′ , U 21 ​ x W + U 22 ​ x W ′ ) Ux=(U_{11}x_{W}+U_{12}x_{W^{\prime}},U_{21}x_{W}+U_{22}x_{W^{\prime}}) . We will consider a sequence of U U depending on some index k k which we will usually suppress in the notation. We assume that 1. The entry U 11 U_{11} remains uniformly bounded and converges to a finite limit as k → + ∞ k\to+\infty , denoted as U 11 ∞ U_{11}^{\infty} . 2. The entry U 21 = 0 U_{21}=0 , and U 12 U_{12} is arbitrary. 3. There are lower bounds | U 22 ​ x w ′ | ≥ Λ k ​ | x w ′ | |U_{22}x_{w^{\prime}}|\geq\Lambda_{k}|x_{w^{\prime}}| for any x w ′ ∈ W ′ x_{w^{\prime}}\in W^{\prime} , where Λ k → + ∞ \Lambda_{k}\to+\infty as k → + ∞ k\to+\infty . Intuitively, this limit U → + ∞ U\to+\infty means that the kernel becomes increasingly sensitive to differences in the W ′ W^{\prime} direction, so points with different W ′ W^{\prime} -coordinates become asymptotically separated. The random variable X X can also be decomposed as X = ( X W , X W ′ ) X=(X_{W},X_{W^{\prime}}) . In general, the marginal distribution of X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} is the sum of a discrete part and a non-atomic part (which could have a singular continuous part in general), μ X W ′ ∼ ∑ 1 ∞ p i ​ δ a i + μ N ​ A , p i > 0 , ∑ p i ≤ 1 , a i ∈ W ′ . \mu_{X_{W^{\prime}}}\sim\sum_{1}^{\infty}p_{i}\delta_{a_{i}}+\mu_{NA},\quad p_{i}>0,\sum p_{i}\leq 1,\quad a_{i}\in W^{\prime}. (17) If X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} is a continuous variable, then the discrete part does not appear. It will turn out that the asymptotic behavior of J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) as U → + ∞ U\to+\infty for continuous variables vis à vis discrete variables will be drastically different. Roughly, in the large U U limit, the kernel interaction K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) K(U(X-X^{\prime})) vanishes unless two samples have the same W ′ W^{\prime} component. For a non-atomic distribution, two independent samples almost surely have different W ′ W^{\prime} -components, so the kernel interactions disappear and the limiting contribution is trivial. By contrast, atoms a i a_{i} satisfy ℙ ⁡ ( X W ′ = a i ) > 0 \mathbb{P}(X_{W^{\prime}}=a_{i})>0 , so samples within the same atom continue to interact and give rise to lower-dimensional kernel ridge regression problems on W W . 3.1 Dimensional reduction of the RKHS We can define a new RKHS ℋ W \mathcal{H}_{W} for a class of functions f f on W W with the following norm: ‖ f ‖ ℋ W 2 = ∫ W ∗ | f ^ ​ ( ω W ) | 2 k W ​ d ​ ω W , \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}}^{2}=\int_{W^{*}}\frac{|\hat{f}(\omega_{W})|^{2}}{k_{W}}d\omega_{W}, (18) where k W ​ ( ω W ) = ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ k V ​ ( ω W , ω W ′ ) ​ d ​ ω W ′ . k_{W}(\omega_{W})=\int_{W^{\prime*}}k_{V}(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}})d\omega_{W^{\prime}}. From the integrability assumptions on k V k_{V} , Fubini theorem implies ∫ W ∗ k W ​ d ​ ω W = ∫ V ∗ k V ​ ( ω ) ​ 𝑑 ω = 1 , \int_{W^{*}}k_{W}d\omega_{W}=\int_{V^{*}}k_{V}(\omega)d\omega=1, Thus k W k_{W} inherits the same kind of integrability conditions. The relation between ℋ \mathcal{H} and ℋ W \mathcal{H}_{W} arises from the optimal extension problem. Lemma 3.1 . (Interpretation of ℋ W \mathcal{H}_{W} ) If f ∈ ℋ f\in\mathcal{H} , then its restriction to the subspace W ⊂ V W\subset V lies in ℋ W \mathcal{H}_{W} . Moreover, for any g ∈ ℋ W g\in\mathcal{H}_{W} , ‖ g ‖ ℋ W = min f ∈ ℋ ⁡ { ‖ f ‖ ℋ : f | W = g } . \left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}}=\min_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}:f|_{W}=g\}. Proof. We observe that f | W = g f|_{W}=g if and only if g ⁡ ( x W ) = f ⁡ ( x W , 0 ) = ∫ V ∗ f ^ ​ ( ω W , ω W ′ ) ​ e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ x W , ω W ⟩ ​ 𝑑 ω = ∫ W ∗ e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ x W , ω W ⟩ ​ d ​ ω W ​ ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ f ^ ​ ( ω W , ω W ′ ) ​ d ​ ω W ′ , \begin{split}g(x_{W})&=f(x_{W},0)=\int_{V^{*}}\hat{f}(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}})e^{2\pi i\langle x_{W},\omega_{W}\rangle}d\omega\\ &=\int_{W^{*}}e^{2\pi i\langle x_{W},\omega_{W}\rangle}d\omega_{W}\int_{W^{\prime*}}\hat{f}(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}})d\omega_{W^{\prime}},\end{split} if and only if g ^ ​ ( ω W ) = ∫ W g ⁡ ( x W ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ x W , ω W ⟩ ​ d ​ x W = ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ f ^ ​ ( ω W , ω W ′ ) ​ d ​ ω W ′ . \begin{split}&\hat{g}(\omega_{W})=\int_{W}g(x_{W})e^{-2\pi i\langle x_{W},\omega_{W}\rangle}dx_{W}=\int_{W^{\prime*}}\hat{f}(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}})d\omega_{W^{\prime}}.\end{split} This implies ‖ g ‖ ℋ W 2 = ∫ W ∗ d ​ ω W ​ | g ^ | 2 ​ k W − 1 = ∫ W ∗ d ​ ω W ​ | ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ f ^ ​ ( ω W , ω W ′ ) ​ d ​ ω W ′ | 2 ​ k W − 1 ≤ ∫ W ∗ d ​ ω W ​ ( ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ | f ^ | 2 ​ k V − 1 ​ d ​ ω W ′ ) = ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 . \begin{split}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}}^{2}&=\int_{W^{*}}d\omega_{W}|\hat{g}|^{2}k_{W}^{-1}=\int_{W^{*}}d\omega_{W}|\int_{W^{\prime*}}\hat{f}(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}})d\omega_{W^{\prime}}|^{2}k_{W}^{-1}\\ &\leq\int_{W^{*}}d\omega_{W}(\int_{W^{\prime*}}|\hat{f}|^{2}k_{V}^{-1}d\omega_{W^{\prime}})\\ &=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.\end{split} where the second line uses Cauchy-Schwarz applied to the integrand of the outer integral. This proves ‖ g ‖ ℋ W ≤ ‖ f ‖ ℋ \left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}} , and in particular the restriction of f f to W W defines a function in ℋ W \mathcal{H}_{W} . Moreover, for any given g ∈ ℋ W g\in\mathcal{H}_{W} , the equality is achieved by the f ∈ ℋ f\in\mathcal{H} whose Fourier transform is defined by f ^ ​ ( ω ) = g ^ ​ ( ω W ) ​ k V ​ ( ω ) k W ​ ( ω W ) . \hat{f}(\omega)=\hat{g}(\omega_{W})\frac{k_{V}(\omega)}{k_{W}(\omega_{W})}. This implies that ‖ g ‖ ℋ W = min f ∈ ℋ ⁡ { ‖ f ‖ ℋ : f | W = g } . \left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}}=\min_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}:f|_{W}=g\}. ∎ We shall later need a generalization of Lemma . The optimal interpolation problem asks the following: Question 4 . Given m m distinct points y i ∈ W ′ y_{i}\in W^{\prime} and functions g i ∈ ℋ W g_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{W} for i = 1 , … ​ m i=1,\ldots m , what is the f ∈ ℋ f\in\mathcal{H} with the smallest ℋ \mathcal{H} -norm, such that its restrictions to the affine subspaces y i + W y_{i}+W are prescribed by f ⁡ ( x W , y i ) = g i ​ ( x W ) f(x_{W},y_{i})=g_{i}(x_{W}) for i = 1 , … ​ m i=1,\ldots m ? Lemma 3.2 . (Optimal interpolation) Suppose there exists some f ∈ ℋ f\in\mathcal{H} with f ⁡ ( x W , y i ) = g i ​ ( x W ) f(x_{W},y_{i})=g_{i}(x_{W}) for i = 1 , … ​ m i=1,\ldots m . Then the formula for the minimizer f ∗ f_{*} of the optimal interpolation problem can be written in Fourier transform as f ^ ∗ ​ ( ω ) = ∑ ( M − 1 ) j ​ i ​ g ^ j ​ ( ω W ) ​ k V ​ ( ω ) k W ​ ( ω W ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ ω W ′ , y i ⟩ , \hat{f}_{*}(\omega)=\sum(M^{-1})_{ji}\hat{g}_{j}(\omega_{W})\frac{k_{V}(\omega)}{k_{W}(\omega_{W})}e^{-2\pi i\langle\omega_{W^{\prime}},y_{i}\rangle}, (19) where M − 1 M^{-1} is the inverse matrix to the following Hermitian matrix depending on ω W \omega_{W} , M i ​ j ​ ( ω W ) = k W ​ ( ω W ) − 1 ​ ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ k V ​ ( ω ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ ω W ′ , y i − y j ⟩ ​ d ​ ω W ′ . M_{ij}(\omega_{W})=k_{W}(\omega_{W})^{-1}\int_{W^{\prime*}}k_{V}(\omega)e^{-2\pi i\langle\omega_{W^{\prime}},y_{i}-y_{j}\rangle}d\omega_{W^{\prime}}. (20) The norm of the minimizer is given by ‖ f ∗ ‖ ℋ 2 = ∫ W ∗ ∑ k W − 1 ​ ( M − 1 ) i ​ j ​ g ^ i ​ g ^ ¯ j ​ d ​ ω W . \left\lVert f_{*}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\int_{W^{*}}\sum k_{W}^{-1}(M^{-1})_{ij}\hat{g}_{i}\overline{\hat{g}}_{j}d\omega_{W}. (21) Proof. For any f ∈ ℋ f\in\mathcal{H} , we recall that ‖ f ^ ‖ L 1 ≤ ‖ f ‖ ℋ \left\lVert\hat{f}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}} by Cauchy-Schwarz, and in particular f ^ \hat{f} is L 1 L^{1} . We first reformulate the restriction conditions in terms of the Fourier transform. The requirement is g i ​ ( x W ) = f ⁡ ( x W , y i ) = ∫ W ∗ f ^ ​ ( ω W , ω W ′ ) ​ e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ( ⟨ x W , ω W ⟩ + ⟨ y i , ω W ′ ⟩ ) ​ 𝑑 ω = ∫ W ∗ ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ f ^ ​ ( ω W , ω W ′ ) ​ e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ x W , ω W ⟩ ​ e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ y i , ω W ′ ⟩ ​ d ​ ω W ​ d ​ ω W ′ , \begin{split}g_{i}(x_{W})&=f(x_{W},y_{i})=\int_{W^{*}}\hat{f}(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}})e^{2\pi i(\langle x_{W},\omega_{W}\rangle+\langle y_{i},\omega_{W^{\prime}}\rangle)}d\omega\\ &=\int_{W^{*}}\int_{W^{\prime*}}\hat{f}(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}})e^{2\pi i\langle x_{W},\omega_{W}\rangle}e^{2\pi i\langle y_{i},\omega_{W^{\prime}}\rangle}d\omega_{W}d\omega_{W^{\prime}},\end{split} which is equivalent to g ^ i ( ω W ) = ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ f ^ ( ω W , ω W ′ ) e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ y i , ω W ′ ⟩ d ω W ′ , i = 1 , … m . \hat{g}_{i}(\omega_{W})=\int_{W^{\prime*}}\hat{f}(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}})e^{2\pi i\langle y_{i},\omega_{W^{\prime}}\rangle}d\omega_{W^{\prime}},\quad i=1,\ldots m. (22) Our goal is to find f ^ ∗ \hat{f}_{*} minimizing the integral ∫ V ∗ | f ^ | 2 ​ k V − 1 ​ 𝑑 ω = ∫ W ∗ d ​ ω W ​ ∫ ( W ′ ) ∗ | f ^ | 2 ​ k V − 1 ​ d ​ ω W ′ . \int_{V^{*}}|\hat{f}|^{2}k_{V}^{-1}d\omega=\int_{W^{*}}d\omega_{W}\int_{(W^{\prime})^{*}}|\hat{f}|^{2}k_{V}^{-1}d\omega_{W^{\prime}}. The problem decouples to an infinite collection of independent minimization problems: for each fixed value of ω W \omega_{W} , we view f ^ ​ ( ω W , ⋅ ) \hat{f}(\omega_{W},\cdot) as an unknown function of ω W ′ \omega_{W^{\prime}} , so ( ) prescribes the value of m m linear functionals, and we want to minimize the unknown function with respect to the Hilbert inner product ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ | f ^ | 2 ​ k V − 1 ​ ( ω W , ω W ′ ) ​ d ​ ω W ′ . \int_{W^{\prime*}}|\hat{f}|^{2}k_{V}^{-1}(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}})d\omega_{W^{\prime}}. The point is that the different ω W \omega_{W} do not interact with each other. There is a standard procedure to solve this minimization problem. First, we use the Riesz representation theorem to identify the m m linear functionals as m m elements of the Hilbert spaces, and the minimizer f ^ ​ ( ω W , ⋅ ) \hat{f}(\omega_{W},\cdot) must lie in the ℂ \mathbb{C} -span of these m m elements. This shows that f ^ ​ ( ω W , ⋅ ) = ∑ 1 m a i ​ k V ​ ( ω W , ⋅ ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ ⋅ , y i ⟩ , \hat{f}(\omega_{W},\cdot)=\sum_{1}^{m}a_{i}k_{V}(\omega_{W},\cdot)e^{-2\pi i\langle\cdot,y_{i}\rangle}, (23) where a i a_{i} are m m coefficients depending on ω W \omega_{W} to be determined. The Hilbert inner product evaluated on the spanned of the m m elements k V ​ ( ω W , ⋅ ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ ⋅ , y i ⟩ k_{V}(\omega_{W},\cdot)e^{-2\pi i\langle\cdot,y_{i}\rangle} gives rise to the Hermitian inner product matrix ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ k V − 1 ​ k V ​ ( ω W , ⋅ ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ ⋅ , y i ⟩ ​ k V ​ ( ω W , ⋅ ) ​ e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ ⋅ , y j ⟩ ​ d ​ ω W ′ = k W ​ ( ω W ) ​ M i ​ j ​ ( ω W ) . \int_{W^{\prime*}}k_{V}^{-1}k_{V}(\omega_{W},\cdot)e^{-2\pi i\langle\cdot,y_{i}\rangle}k_{V}(\omega_{W},\cdot)e^{2\pi i\langle\cdot,y_{j}\rangle}d\omega_{W^{\prime}}=k_{W}(\omega_{W})M_{ij}(\omega_{W}). In particular this shows ( M i ​ j ) (M_{ij}) is positive semi-definite. Since k V > 0 k_{V}>0 , we see that the m m functions k V ​ ( ω W , ⋅ ) ​ e − 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ ⋅ , y i ⟩ k_{V}(\omega_{W},\cdot)e^{-2\pi i\langle\cdot,y_{i}\rangle} are linearly independent, so the inner product matrix is also non-degenerate. As a formality check, ∫ W ∗ k W ​ | M i ​ j | ​ d ​ ω W ≤ ∫ W ∗ d ​ ω W ​ ∫ W ′ ⁣ ∗ k V ​ ( ω ) ​ d ​ ω W ′ < + ∞ , ∀ i , j , \int_{W^{*}}k_{W}|M_{ij}|d\omega_{W}\leq\int_{W^{*}}d\omega_{W}\int_{W^{\prime*}}k_{V}(\omega)d\omega_{W^{\prime}}<+\infty,\quad\forall i,j, so ( M i ​ j ) (M_{ij}) is indeed well defined for almost every ω W \omega_{W} . It remains to determine the coefficient a i a_{i} . For this we plug ( ) into the linear constraints ( ) to get g ^ i ​ ( ω W ) = ∫ ( W ′ ) ∗ f ^ ∗ ​ ( ω W , ω W ′ ) ​ e 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ⟨ y i , ω W ′ ⟩ ​ d ​ ω W ′ = k W ​ ∑ a j ​ M j ​ i . \hat{g}_{i}(\omega_{W})=\int_{(W^{\prime})^{*}}\hat{f}_{*}(\omega_{W},\omega_{W^{\prime}})e^{2\pi i\langle y_{i},\omega_{W^{\prime}}\rangle}d\omega_{W^{\prime}}=k_{W}\sum a_{j}M_{ji}. Solving this linear system gives a i = k W − 1 ​ ( M − 1 ) j ​ i ​ g ^ j ​ ( ω W ) . a_{i}=k_{W}^{-1}(M^{-1})_{ji}\hat{g}_{j}(\omega_{W}). This gives the formula for the minimizer ( ). We can then compute the norm by evaluating the inner product matrix for f ∗ f_{*} , ‖ f ∗ ‖ ℋ 2 = ∫ W ∗ ∑ k W ​ M i ​ j ​ a i ​ a ¯ j ​ d ​ ω W = ∫ W ∗ ∑ k W − 1 ​ M i ​ j − 1 ​ g ^ i ​ g ^ ¯ j ​ d ​ ω W \left\lVert f_{*}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\int_{W^{*}}\sum k_{W}M_{ij}a_{i}\bar{a}_{j}d\omega_{W}=\int_{W^{*}}\sum k_{W}^{-1}M^{-1}_{ij}\hat{g}_{i}\bar{\hat{g}}_{j}d\omega_{W} as required. ∎ The matrix M M encodes the coupling between the interpolation conditions imposed on the affine slices y i + W y_{i}+W . When these slices are well separated, the off-diagonal entries of M M become small, the coupling weakens and the interpolation problem approximately decouples into independent problems on the individual slices. This decoupling phenomenon will recur throughout the paper, leading to various orthogonality, decoupling, and dimensional-reduction effects. The following corollary provides a first illustration of this phenomenon in the setting of optimal interpolation. Corollary 3.3 . ( Asymptotic orthogonality in the optimal interpolation problem ) We fix W , W ′ W,W^{\prime} , and consider a sequence of data ( y i ) 1 m (y_{i})_{1}^{m} and ( g i ) 1 m (g_{i})_{1}^{m} depending on some sequence index k k that we suppress in the notation. We suppose that ‖ g i ‖ ℋ W \left\lVert g_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}} are uniformly bounded, and min i ≠ j ⁡ | y i − y j | → + ∞ \min_{i\neq j}|y_{i}-y_{j}|\to+\infty in the k → + ∞ k\to+\infty limit, then the norm of the minimizers satisfies lim inf k → ∞ ( ‖ f ∗ ‖ ℋ 2 − ∑ 1 m ‖ g i ‖ ℋ W 2 ) ≥ 0 . \liminf_{k\to\infty}\left(\left\lVert f_{*}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\sum_{1}^{m}\left\lVert g_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}}^{2}\right)\geq 0. Proof. As noted in the above proof, ( M i ​ j ) (M_{ij}) is defined for almost every ω W \omega_{W} , and is a positive definite Hermitian matrix. By the formula ( ), the diagonal entries M i ​ i = 1 M_{ii}=1 by definition. For i ≠ j i\neq j , we have | y i − y j | → + ∞ |y_{i}-y_{j}|\to+\infty , so the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma implies that for all the ω W \omega_{W} such that k W ​ ( ω W ) k_{W}(\omega_{W}) is finite, we have lim k → ∞ M i ​ j ​ ( ω W ) = 0 . \lim_{k\to\infty}M_{ij}(\omega_{W})=0. To summarize M i ​ j → δ i ​ j M_{ij}\to\delta_{ij} for almost every ω W \omega_{W} . Consequently, the inverse matrix M − 1 M^{-1} also converges to the identity a.e. ω W \omega_{W} , so that near the limit M − 1 ≥ ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ I M^{-1}\geq(1-\epsilon)I for any small given ϵ \epsilon and fixed ω W \omega_{W} , whence lim inf k → + ∞ ( M i ​ j − 1 ​ g ^ i ​ g ^ ¯ j − ∑ 1 m | g ^ i | 2 ​ ( 1 − ϵ ) ) ≥ 0 , a . e . ω W . \liminf_{k\to+\infty}(M^{-1}_{ij}\hat{g}_{i}\bar{\hat{g}}_{j}-\sum_{1}^{m}|\hat{g}_{i}|^{2}(1-\epsilon))\geq 0,\quad a.e.\quad\omega_{W}. By Fatou’s lemma lim inf k → + ∞ ∫ W ∗ k W − 1 ​ ( M i ​ j − 1 ​ g ^ i ​ g ^ ¯ j − ∑ 1 m | g ^ i | 2 ​ ( 1 − ϵ ) ) ​ d ​ ω W ≥ 0 , \liminf_{k\to+\infty}\int_{W^{*}}k_{W}^{-1}(M^{-1}_{ij}\hat{g}_{i}\bar{\hat{g}}_{j}-\sum_{1}^{m}|\hat{g}_{i}|^{2}(1-\epsilon))d\omega_{W}\geq 0, and by the norm formula for the minimizer ( ), we have lim inf k → + ∞ ( ‖ f ∗ ‖ ℋ 2 − ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ ∑ 1 m ‖ g i ‖ ℋ W 2 ) ≥ 0 . \liminf_{k\to+\infty}\left(\left\lVert f_{*}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-(1-\epsilon)\sum_{1}^{m}\left\lVert g_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}}^{2}\right)\geq 0. Since ‖ g i ‖ ℋ W \left\lVert g_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}} are uniformly bounded in the sequence, and ϵ \epsilon is arbitrarily small, we deduce the claim. ∎ Remark 5 . The reason we only prove a one-sided inequality, rather than lim k → ∞ ( ‖ f ∗ ‖ ℋ 2 − ∑ 1 m ‖ g i ‖ ℋ W 2 ) = 0 , \lim_{k\to\infty}\left(\left\lVert f_{*}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\sum_{1}^{m}\left\lVert g_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}}^{2}\right)=0, is that M − 1 M^{-1} may be a priori very large for a very small subset of ω W \omega_{W} , even though M i ​ j M_{ij} is always uniformly bounded. This stronger conclusion can be deduced by the stronger hypothesis that M i ​ j → δ i ​ j M_{ij}\to\delta_{ij} uniformly for all ω W \omega_{W} , rather than a.e ω W \omega_{W} . Only the one-sided version will be used later. 3.2 Dimensionally reduced minimization problems Recall from ( ) that the marginal distribution may have atoms at a i ∈ W ′ a_{i}\in W^{\prime} . We shall consider the minimization problem for functions on the affine subspaces { x W ′ = a i } ⊂ V \{x_{W^{\prime}}=a_{i}\}\subset V . The characteristic function χ m \chi_{m} allows us to focus on the atoms with significant L 2 L^{2} -mass contributions . Lemma 3.12 . Proof. We define the restrictions g i ​ ( x W ) = f U ​ ( x W , U 22 ​ a i ) g_{i}(x_{W})=f_{U}(x_{W},U_{22}a_{i}) . By Lemma , and the translation invariance of the RKHS, we obtain the uniform bounds ‖ g i ‖ ℋ W ≤ ‖ f U ‖ ℋ ≤ λ − 1 / 2 𝔼 [ | Y | 2 ] 1 / 2 . \left\lVert g_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{W}}\leq\left\lVert f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\lambda^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]^{1/2}. By definition f U ∈ ℋ f_{U}\in\mathcal{H} interpolates the functions g i g_{i} at the affine subspaces U 22 ​ a i + W U_{22}a_{i}+W , so its ℋ \mathcal{H} -norm is bounded below by the optimal interpolation solution in Lemma . By applying Lemma to the dimensionally reduced minimization problem J i J_{i} , we see lim inf J i ​ ( U 11 , λ ) = J i ​ ( U 11 ∞ , λ ) . \liminf J_{i}(U_{11},\lambda)=J_{i}(U_{11}^{\infty},\lambda). Combining the above proves the lemma. ∎ We turn the attention to the non-atomic part. Lemma 3.13 . As the sequence index k → + ∞ k\to+\infty , lim inf 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) − Y | 2 ​ ( 1 − χ ) ] ≥ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ​ ( 1 − χ ) ] . \liminf\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)-Y|^{2}(1-\chi)]\geq\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}(1-\chi)]. Proof. It suffices to prove that the cross term lim Re ​ 𝔼 ​ [ f U ​ ( U ​ X ) ​ Y ¯ ​ ( 1 − χ ) ] = 0 . \lim\text{Re}\mathbb{E}[f_{U}(UX)\bar{Y}(1-\chi)]=0. By the reproducing kernel property ( ), f U ​ ( U ​ X ) = ( f U , K ⁡ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) ) ℋ , f_{U}(UX)=(f_{U},K(UX,\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}, hence by Fubini theorem 𝔼 ⁡ [ f U ​ ( U ​ X ) ​ Y ¯ ​ ( 1 − χ ) ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( f U , K ⁡ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) ) ℋ ​ Y ¯ ​ ( 1 − χ ) ] = ( f U , 𝔼 ⁡ [ K ⁡ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) ​ Y ¯ ​ ( 1 − χ ) ] ) ℋ . \mathbb{E}[f_{U}(UX)\bar{Y}(1-\chi)]=\mathbb{E}[(f_{U},K(UX,\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}\bar{Y}(1-\chi)]=(f_{U},\mathbb{E}[K(UX,\cdot)\bar{Y}(1-\chi)])_{\mathcal{H}}. Here we notice that 𝔼 ⁡ [ K ⁡ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) ​ Y ¯ ​ ( 1 − χ ) ] \mathbb{E}[K(UX,\cdot)\bar{Y}(1-\chi)] is a weighted average of kernel functions, and in particular defines an element of ℋ \mathcal{H} . By Cauchy-Schwarz, and the fact that ‖ f ‖ ℋ ≤ λ − 1 / 2 𝔼 [ | Y | 2 ] 1 / 2 \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\lambda^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]^{1/2} , we get | 𝔼 [ f U ( U X ) Z ] | ≤ λ − 1 / 2 𝔼 [ | Y | 2 ] 1 / 2 ‖ 𝔼 [ K ( U X , ⋅ ) Y ¯ ( 1 − χ ) ] ‖ ℋ . |\mathbb{E}[f_{U}(UX)Z]|\leq\lambda^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]^{1/2}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}[K(UX,\cdot)\bar{Y}(1-\chi)]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}. (31) where we denote Z = Y ¯ ​ ( 1 − χ ) Z=\bar{Y}(1-\chi) . We now evaluate the RHS norm. We take an independent double copy ( X ′ , Z ′ ) (X^{\prime},Z^{\prime}) which has the same law as ( X , Z ) (X,Z) . Then by Fubini theorem again, ‖ 𝔼 ⁡ [ K ⁡ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) ​ Z ] ‖ ℋ 2 = ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ K ⁡ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) ​ Z ] , 𝔼 ⁡ [ K ⁡ ( U ​ X ′ , ⋅ ) ​ Z ′ ] ) ℋ = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Z ​ Z ¯ ′ ​ ( K ⁡ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) , K ⁡ ( U ​ X ′ , ⋅ ) ) ℋ ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Z ​ Z ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Z ​ Z ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ] . \begin{split}&\left\lVert\mathbb{E}[K(UX,\cdot)Z]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=(\mathbb{E}[K(UX,\cdot)Z],\mathbb{E}[K(UX^{\prime},\cdot)Z^{\prime}])_{\mathcal{H}}\\ =&\mathbb{E}[Z\bar{Z}^{\prime}(K(UX,\cdot),K(UX^{\prime},\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}]\\ =&\mathbb{E}[Z\bar{Z}^{\prime}K(UX,UX^{\prime})]=\mathbb{E}[Z\bar{Z}^{\prime}K(U(X-X^{\prime}))].\end{split} Here the last line uses the reproducing kernel property. By our assumptions on the matrix U U , | U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) | = | ( U 11 ​ ( X W − X W ′ ) + U 12 ​ ( X W − X W ′ ) , U 22 ​ ( X W ′ − X W ′ ′ ) ) | ≥ | U 22 ( X W ′ − X W ′ ′ ) ) | ≥ Λ k | X W ′ − X W ′ ′ | , Λ k → + ∞ . \begin{split}&|U(X-X^{\prime})|=|(U_{11}(X_{W}-X_{W}^{\prime})+U_{12}(X_{W}-X_{W}^{\prime}),U_{22}(X_{W^{\prime}}-X_{W^{\prime}}^{\prime}))|\\ \geq&|U_{22}(X_{W^{\prime}}-X_{W^{\prime}}^{\prime}))|\geq\Lambda_{k}|X_{W^{\prime}}-X_{W^{\prime}}^{\prime}|,\quad\Lambda_{k}\to+\infty.\end{split} Thus conditional on the off diagonal condition X W ′ ≠ X W ′ ′ X_{W^{\prime}}\neq X_{W^{\prime}}^{\prime} , by the decay of the kernel function K K at infinity, we have the pointwise convergence K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) → 0 , k → + ∞ . K(U(X-X^{\prime}))\to 0,\quad k\to+\infty. By the dominated convergence theorem, lim 𝔼 ⁡ [ Z ​ Z ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ≠ X W ′ ′ ] = 0 . \lim\mathbb{E}[Z\bar{Z}^{\prime}K(U(X-X^{\prime}))\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\neq X_{W^{\prime}}^{\prime}}]=0. On the diagonal X W ′ = X W ′ ′ {X_{W^{\prime}}=X_{W^{\prime}}^{\prime}} of the product measure space, the measure is supported on the atoms of the marginal distribution of X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} . But the factor 1 − χ 1-\chi is precisely engineered so that Z = Z ′ = 0 Z=Z^{\prime}=0 on the atoms, so lim 𝔼 ⁡ [ Z ​ Z ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ] = 0 . \lim\mathbb{E}[Z\bar{Z}^{\prime}K(U(X-X^{\prime}))]=0. whence | 𝔼 [ f ( U X ) Z ] | ≤ λ − 1 / 2 𝔼 [ | Y | 2 ] 1 / 2 ‖ 𝔼 [ K ( U X , ⋅ ) Y ¯ ( 1 − χ ) ] ‖ ℋ → 0 , k → + ∞ . |\mathbb{E}[f(UX)Z]|\leq\lambda^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]^{1/2}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}[K(UX,\cdot)\bar{Y}(1-\chi)]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\to 0,\quad k\to+\infty. This proves the lemma. ∎ Combining the two lemmas, for arbitrarily large fixed m m , lim inf I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) ≥ ∑ 1 m J i ​ ( U 11 ∞ ) + 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ​ ( 1 − χ ) ] . \liminf I(f,U,\lambda)\geq\sum_{1}^{m}J_{i}(U_{11}^{\infty})+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\frac{1}{2}}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}(1-\chi)].
