Thus taking the m → + ∞ m\to+\infty limit, we see lim inf k → + ∞ I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) ≥ ∑ 1 ∞ J i ​ ( U 11 ∞ ) + 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ​ ( 1 − χ ) ] , \liminf_{k\to+\infty}I(f,U,\lambda)\geq\sum_{1}^{\infty}J_{i}(U_{11}^{\infty})+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\frac{1}{2}}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}(1-\chi)], so the theorem is proved. ∎ Remark 6 . It is instructive to reflect on how to make the convergence into an effective estimate for finite but large U U , once an explicit kernel function is given. The number m m comes from selecting the atoms where Y Y has significant L 2 L^{2} -mass contributions, and the rest of the atoms are negligible as long as they carry a small total L 2 L^{2} -mass for Y Y . The asymptotic orthogonality of the optimal interpolation problem is perhaps the most delicate ingredient, and the essential estimate comes from the decay rate of the off-diagonal terms in the matrix M i ​ j M_{ij} in Lemma . Finally, to control the term 𝔼 ⁡ [ Z ​ Z ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ] \mathbb{E}[Z\bar{Z}^{\prime}K(U(X-X^{\prime}))] , we need to compute the decay rate of the kernel function K K so that the integral is localized to a small neighbourhood of the diagonal X W ′ = X W ′ ′ X_{W^{\prime}}=X^{\prime}_{W^{\prime}} . Moreover, we also need the L 2 L^{2} norm of Z Z to be small in this neighbourhood. The main enemy has to do with the local concentration of L 2 L^{2} mass for Z Z , or in other words the measure 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Z | 2 | X W ′ ] ​ μ X W ′ \mathbb{E}[|Z|^{2}|X_{W^{\prime}}]\mu_{X_{W^{\prime}}} behaves like an approximate delta function on W ′ W^{\prime} . 3.5 Asymptotic nature of the minimizer Recall we constructed a test function f ( m ) f^{(m)} ( cf. ( )) in the proof of Theorem . We can in fact deduce that it is a good approximation to the true minimizer , here denoted as f U f_{U} : Theorem 3.14 . (Approximate minimizer) We have lim k → + ∞ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | ( f ( m ) − f U ) ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] + λ ​ ‖ f ( m ) − f U ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } m + 1 ∞ ] . \lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}[|(f^{(m)}-f_{U})(UX)|^{2}]+\lambda\left\lVert f^{(m)}-f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}_{m+1}^{\infty}}]. In particular lim m → + ∞ lim k → + ∞ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | ( f ( m ) − f U ) ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] + λ ​ ‖ f ( m ) − f U ‖ ℋ 2 = 0 . \lim_{m\to+\infty}\lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}[|(f^{(m)}-f_{U})(UX)|^{2}]+\lambda\left\lVert f^{(m)}-f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=0. Proof. The functional I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) is quadratic in f f and minimized at f U f_{U} , with second order term in f f being Q ⁡ ( f ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] + 1 2 ​ λ ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 . Q(f)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|f(UX)|^{2}]+\frac{1}{2}\lambda\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}. By completing the squares, this implies that I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) = Q ⁡ ( f − f U ) + I ⁡ ( f U , U , λ ) = Q ⁡ ( f − f U ) + J ⁡ ( U , λ ) . I(f,U,\lambda)=Q(f-f_{U})+I(f_{U},U,\lambda)=Q(f-f_{U})+J(U,\lambda). We recall from ( ) that as k → + ∞ k\to+\infty , I ⁡ ( f ( m ) , U , λ ) → 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a 1 , … ​ a m } ] + ∑ 1 m J i ​ ( U 11 ∞ , λ ) . I(f^{(m)},U,\lambda)\to\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{1},\ldots a_{m}\}}]+\sum_{1}^{m}J_{i}(U_{11}^{\infty},\lambda). By Theorem , J ⁡ ( U , λ ) → 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } 1 ∞ ] + ∑ 1 ∞ J i ​ ( U 11 ∞ , λ ) . J(U,\lambda)\to\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}_{1}^{\infty}}]+\sum_{1}^{\infty}J_{i}(U_{11}^{\infty},\lambda). Thus Q ⁡ ( f ( m ) − f U ) = I ⁡ ( f ( m ) , U , λ ) − J ⁡ ( U , λ ) → 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } m + 1 ∞ ] − ∑ m + 1 ∞ J i ​ ( U 11 ∞ , λ ) . Q(f^{(m)}-f_{U})=I(f^{(m)},U,\lambda)-J(U,\lambda)\to\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}_{m+1}^{\infty}}]-\sum_{m+1}^{\infty}J_{i}(U_{11}^{\infty},\lambda). In particular lim k → + ∞ Q ⁡ ( f ( m ) − f U ) ≤ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } m + 1 ∞ ] \lim_{k\to+\infty}Q(f^{(m)}-f_{U})\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}_{m+1}^{\infty}}] as required. ∎ Remark 7 . In particular, if X X is a continuous variable, then asymptotically as U → ∞ U\to\infty , lim U → ∞ ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] + λ ​ ‖ f U ‖ ℋ 2 ) = 0 . \lim_{U\to\infty}(\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)|^{2}]+\lambda\left\lVert f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})=0. We shall recover this result by functional analytic methods in Corollary below. Remark 8 . Recall that f ( m ) f^{(m)} is built from m m local contributions coming from the minimizers of the dimensionally reduced problems associated to the distinct points a i ∈ W a_{i}\in W , which become ℋ \mathcal{H} -orthogonal in the limit. The point of the theorem is that in the limit the minimizer essentially decouples into the solutions of these dimensionally reduced problems. Corollary 3.15 . (Asymptotic trivial fitting for the non-atomic part) We have lim k → + ∞ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } 1 ∞ ] = 0 . \lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}_{1}^{\infty}}]=0. Proof. The first statement in Theorem implies lim k → + ∞ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | ( f ( m ) − f U ) ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } 1 ∞ ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } m + 1 ∞ ] . \lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}[|(f^{(m)}-f_{U})(UX)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}_{1}^{\infty}}]\leq\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}_{m+1}^{\infty}}]. Recall from ( ) that lim k → + ∞ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f ( m ) ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } 1 m ] = 0 , \lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}[|f^{(m)}(UX)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}_{1}^{m}}]=0, hence for any fixed m m , lim k → + ∞ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } 1 ∞ ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } m + 1 ∞ ] . \lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}_{1}^{\infty}}]\leq\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}_{m+1}^{\infty}}]. Let m → + ∞ m\to+\infty , we deduce the claim. ∎ 3.6 Discussions We offer a few interpretations on the Theorems. 1. Perhaps the most striking aspect of Theorem is that there is a sharp distinction between the discrete and the continuous variables X X in the large U U limit. This has a very intuitive explanation. Suppose Y Y concerns some question which involves several different cases, such as the earth vs. some other planet, then one would expect that there should be some discrete variable that distinguishes these cases very sharply; in other words, the two cases fall into two very distinct conceptual categories . 2. In Theorem , we saw that in the asymptotic limit, the minimizer decouples into dimensionally reduced problems with ℋ \mathcal{H} -orthogonality property. When humans approach a question involving several very different cases, they would naturally first make a discrete classification, and then analyze the problem separately in each case. This is reflected in the dimensional reduction procedure, where one first looks for the discrete signal variables (in our case the points a i ∈ W ′ a_{i}\in W^{\prime} ), and then solve the sub-problem for each fixed a i a_{i} , which could involve more discrete or continuous variables. In two very different cases, the answers to a given question are intuitively expected to be independent . The orthogonality property may be seen as a mathematical manifestation of this independence. 3. Our limit as U → + ∞ U\to+\infty is an idealization. Along unit vectors in W W , the magnitude | U ​ x | |Ux| stays bounded, while for unit vectors in W ′ W^{\prime} , the magnitude | U ​ x | |Ux| goes to infinity. In practice, if one optimize over all the choices of U U , the minimizer for min U ⁡ J ⁡ ( U , λ ) \min_{U}J(U,\lambda) may fall in some finite region in the space of matrices End ⁡ ( V ) \End(V) , and there is no general reason for the minimizer to be unique. What one could more realistically hope for, is that | U ​ x | |Ux| is much smaller for unit directions x x in some subspace W W of V V , compared to unit directions along some subspace W ′ W^{\prime} transverse to W W . Such a situation may be called scale separation . The intuition is that in a given problem, some variables are more important than others. 4. Suppose the reproducing kernel is rotationally invariant , namely K ⁡ ( x ) K(x) is a function of | x | |x| , which holds for most kernels used in practice. Then I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) = I ⁡ ( f ∘ A , A − 1 ​ U , λ ) , ∀ A ∈ O ⁡ ( V ) ≃ O ⁡ ( d ) , I(f,U,\lambda)=I(f\circ A,A^{-1}U,\lambda),\quad\forall A\in O(V)\simeq O(d), so J ⁡ ( U , λ ) = J ⁡ ( A − 1 ​ U , λ ) J(U,\lambda)=J(A^{-1}U,\lambda) depends only on the group invariant quantity Σ = U T ​ U \Sigma=U^{T}U and λ \lambda , where U T U^{T} is the adjoint of U U with respect to the inner product on V V . Now Σ \Sigma defines a self-adjoint operator on V V , so one can find an orthonormal eigenvector basis , with eigenvalues λ i \lambda_{i} for i = 1 , … ​ d i=1,\ldots d . The scale separation phenomenon above then has the more concrete meaning that some eigenvalues may be much smaller than the others. Intuitively, the large eigenvalues suggest that the eigenspaces are likely to carry the more important information. Collecting together the small vs. large eigenvalue subspaces would give a reasonable way to identify W , W ′ W,W^{\prime} in the scale separation phenomenon. It is worth noting that our standing assumption U 21 = 0 U_{21}=0 holds automatically if W , W ′ W,W^{\prime} are orthogonal, which follows from the eigenspace decomposition. Moreover, when d d is large, it may happen that the eigenvalues exhibit some multi-scale hierarchy . This means that the eigenvalues can be partitioned into subsets, such that eigenvalues in the same subset are of comparable magnitudes, but between different subsets the eigenvalues are separated by many orders of magnitudes: λ 1 ≤ … ≤ λ n 1 ≪ λ n 1 + 1 ≤ … ≤ λ n 2 ≪ λ n 2 + 1 ≤ … ≤ λ d . \lambda_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{n_{1}}\ll\lambda_{n_{1}+1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{n_{2}}\ll\lambda_{n_{2}+1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{d}. Intuitively, this reflects the common sense that conceptual categories can contain subcategories and exhibit hierarchical structure. 5. The discrete/non-atomic dichotomy is only sharp in the U → + ∞ U\to+\infty limit. Suppose instead that U U is finite, but exhibits scale separation, and we want to understand J ⁡ ( U , λ ) J(U,\lambda) . Then we expect that approximate delta functions would have similar effect to the delta functions. In other words, the substitute for the atoms, is local concentration of the probability density of the marginal distribution of X X on W ′ ≃ V / W W^{\prime}\simeq V/W . 6. We have imposed the translation invariant RKHS as the starting point. In fact Corollary gives a hint why this may not always produce a good global fit for the random variable Y Y : for k → + ∞ k\to+\infty , the entire L 2 L^{2} -mass of Y ​ 𝟙 X W ′ ∉ { a i } Y\mathbbm{1}_{X_{W^{\prime}}\notin\{a_{i}\}} is invisible to the minimizer f U f_{U} . The relevance of this issue can be seen from the following thought experiment involving two distinct scales: Example 3.16 . Let V = ℝ V=\mathbb{R} , and X 0 ∈ { ± 1 } X_{0}\in\{\pm 1\} be a Bernoulli variable, which is not directly observable. Let X ∼ N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) X\sim N(0,1) conditional on X 0 = 1 X_{0}=1 , and X ∼ N ⁡ ( 0 , σ 2 ) X\sim N(0,\sigma^{2}) conditional on X 0 = − 1 X_{0}=-1 , where σ ≪ 1 \sigma\ll 1 . Let Y = sin ( X ) + sin ( X σ ) e − | X | 2 / σ 2 . Y=\sin(X)+\sin(\frac{X}{\sigma})e^{-|X|^{2}/\sigma^{2}}. The density of the measure 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 | X ] ​ μ X \mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}|X]\mu_{X} is the superposition of a smooth part and a concentrated cluster around the origin. We suppose λ \lambda is small but fixed, and ℋ \mathcal{H} is one of the standard Sobolev spaces W k , 2 W^{k,2} where k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 . The point is that if we want to fit the sin ⁡ ( X σ ) ​ e − ( X σ ) 2 \sin(\frac{X}{\sigma})e^{-(\frac{X}{\sigma})^{2}} part using a function of the form f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) f(UX) where ‖ f ‖ ℋ ≲ 1 \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\lesssim 1 is not very large, then the parameter U U had better be of order comparable to σ − 1 ≫ 1 \sigma^{-1}\gg 1 . But then Corollary suggests that the information contained in the sin ⁡ ( X ) \sin(X) part would be difficult to detect. Instead, a more reasonable hope is to decompose Y Y into a sum by multiplying by a suitable partition of unity sensitive to the scale parameters inherent in the problem, and try to find a good fit for each summand of Y Y separately . The parameters U U in each sub-problem could be very different. 4 Rotationally invariant kernels 4.1 Rotationally invariant kernels Given a fixed inner product | ⋅ | V |\cdot|_{V} on V V , each matrix U ∈ End ⁡ ( V ) U\in\End(V) has an adjoint operator U T U^{T} , and Σ = U T ​ U \Sigma=U^{T}U defines a (positive semi-definite) form on V V , and Σ − 1 \Sigma^{-1} defines the dual norm on V ∗ V^{*} , namely | x | Σ = | U ​ x | V = ( x , U T ​ U ​ x ) 1 / 2 , | ω | Σ − 1 = max | x | Σ = 1 ⁡ ⟨ ω , x ⟩ = | ( U − 1 ) T ​ ω | . |x|_{\Sigma}=|Ux|_{V}=(x,U^{T}Ux)^{1/2},\quad|\omega|_{\Sigma^{-1}}=\max_{|x|_{\Sigma}=1}\langle\omega,x\rangle=|(U^{-1})^{T}\omega|. We denote S ​ y ​ m + 2 ⊂ S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ V ∗ Sym^{2}_{+}\subset Sym^{2}V^{*} as the open subset consisting of positive definite inner products on V V . In this section we study the first variation of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} as Σ \Sigma varies in S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} . This is a 1-form on S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} , which can be identified with a function valued in S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ V Sym^{2}V . The goal is to prove the following formula, which is [ 12 , Lemma 2.8] with more relaxed assumptions. Theorem 4.2 . (First variation) We denote r Σ ​ ( X , Y ) = Y − F Σ ​ ( X ) = Y − f U ​ ( U ​ X ) r_{\Sigma}(X,Y)=Y-F_{\Sigma}(X)=Y-f_{U}(UX) , and let ( X ′ , Y ′ ) (X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}) be an independent copy of ( X , Y ) (X,Y) . We suppose 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]<+\infty , and the kernel function is K ⁡ ( x ) = 𝒦 ⁡ ( | x | V 2 ) K(x)=\mathcal{K}(|x|^{2}_{V}) for some radial function 𝒦 : ℝ ≥ 0 → ℝ + \mathcal{K}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{+} , such that 1. The derivative 𝒦 ′ ​ ( r ) \mathcal{K}^{\prime}(r) is continuous on ℝ + \mathbb{R}_{+} , and r ​ 𝒦 ′ ​ ( r ) → 0 r\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(r)\to 0 as r → 0 r\to 0 ; 2. One of the following conditions hold: either | 𝒦 ′ ​ ( r ) | ​ r |\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(r)|r is bounded as r → + ∞ r\to+\infty , or | 𝒦 ′ ​ ( r ) | |\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(r)| is monotone decreasing for large enough r r ; 3. The integrability hypothesis holds: for all Σ \Sigma within any given compact subset of S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} , we have sup Σ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | 𝒦 ′ ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ​ | X − X ′ | V 4 ] < + ∞ . \sup_{\Sigma}\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(|X-X^{\prime}|^{2}_{\Sigma})|^{2}|X-X^{\prime}|_{V}^{4}]<+\infty. Then at any Σ ∈ S ​ y ​ m + 2 \Sigma\in Sym^{2}_{+} , D Σ ​ 𝒥 ​ ( Σ , λ ) = − 1 2 ​ λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ r Σ ​ ( X , Y ) ​ r Σ ​ ( X ′ , Y ′ ) ¯ ​ D Σ ​ 𝒦 ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ] = − 1 2 ​ λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ r Σ ​ ( X , Y ) ​ r Σ ​ ( X ′ , Y ′ ) ¯ ​ 𝒦 ′ ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ​ ( X − X ′ ) ⊗ ( X − X ′ ) ] . \begin{split}D_{\Sigma}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\lambda)=&-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\mathbb{E}[r_{\Sigma}(X,Y)\overline{r_{\Sigma}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}D_{\Sigma}\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})]\\ =&-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\mathbb{E}[r_{\Sigma}(X,Y)\overline{r_{\Sigma}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})(X-X^{\prime})\otimes(X-X^{\prime})].\end{split} (34) This formula shows that the variation of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} with respect to Σ \Sigma is governed by pairwise residual correlations weighted by D Σ ​ 𝒦 D_{\Sigma}\mathcal{K} . In particular, when the residual r Σ r_{\Sigma} is small, namely F Σ ​ ( X ) F_{\Sigma}(X) is a good fit for Y Y , then one expects the first variation of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} to be small, as 𝒥 \mathcal{J} is close to its minimum. Remark 10 . We note that the underlying function spaces ℋ Σ \mathcal{H}_{\Sigma} are the same for all Σ \Sigma , if and only if the weight function satisfies C ​ ( Σ ) − 1 ​ k V ​ ( | ω | V 2 ) ≤ k V ​ ( | ω | Σ 2 ) ≤ C ⁡ ( Σ ) ​ k V ​ ( | ω | V 2 ) C(\Sigma)^{-1}k_{V}(|\omega|^{2}_{V})\leq k_{V}(|\omega|^{2}_{\Sigma})\leq C(\Sigma)k_{V}(|\omega|^{2}_{V}) with constant uniform in ω ∈ V ∗ \omega\in V^{*} . This condition holds for the Sobolev kernel, but not for the Gaussian kernel (See Section ). The first variation formula holds for both kernels. Remark 11 . The first variation is naturally a 1-form. To define the gradient of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} as a vector field on S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} , there needs to be an additional choice of a Riemannian metric on S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} . This issue is fundamental to the companion paper [ 28 ] . Remark 12 . Remark 13 . It is an interesting open question to develop a second-order variational analysis of the population objective. This is based on two main ingredients. Using Cauchy-Schwarz and the above claim, up to an error term which uniformly converges to zero, the above expression can be replaced by the empirical expectation of a deterministic function: − 1 2 ​ λ ​ 𝔼 m ​ [ r Σ ​ ( X , Y ) ​ r Σ ​ ( X ′ , Y ′ ) ¯ ​ 𝒦 ′ ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ​ ( X − X ′ ) ⊗ ( X − X ′ ) ] . -\frac{1}{2\lambda}\mathbb{E}_{m}[r_{\Sigma}(X,Y)\overline{r_{\Sigma}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})(X-X^{\prime})\otimes(X-X^{\prime})]. Secondly, we need a uniform law of large numbers to show that with probability one, the above expression converges to the limiting version uniformly in Σ \Sigma . For a start, the integrability hypothesis on 𝒦 \mathcal{K} and Cauchy-Schwarz guarantees the L 1 L^{1} -integrability condition 𝔼 ⁡ [ | r Σ ​ ( X , Y ) ​ r Σ ​ ( X ′ , Y ′ ) ¯ ​ 𝒦 ′ ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ​ ( X − X ′ ) ⊗ ( X − X ′ ) | ] < + ∞ , \mathbb{E}[|r_{\Sigma}(X,Y)\overline{r_{\Sigma}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})(X-X^{\prime})\otimes(X-X^{\prime})|]<+\infty, so the law of large numbers applies pointwise in Σ \Sigma . We denote S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ⊂ S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ V ∗ Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}\subset Sym^{2}V^{*} as the closed subset of positive semi-definite forms on V V . This admits a stratification, with open stratum 𝒮 l = { positive semi-definite forms on V with rank l } , \mathcal{S}_{l}=\{\text{positive semi-definite forms on $V$ with rank $l$}\}, for l = 0 , 1 , … ​ d − 1 l=0,1,\ldots d-1 . Each 𝒮 l \mathcal{S}_{l} is a smooth manifold, and S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 = S ​ y ​ m + 2 ∪ ⋃ l ≤ d − 1 𝒮 l . Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}=Sym^{2}_{+}\cup\bigcup_{l\leq d-1}\mathcal{S}_{l}. In this section we study the extension of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} and its first variation to the boundary of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} . These points represent the degenerate matrices, corresponding to dimensionally reduced configurations, and to test the local minimizing property of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} , one needs a well defined first variation as one moves into the interior. Lemma 4.5 . The functional 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) extends continuously to the boundary of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} . Moreover, the minimizer F Σ F_{\Sigma} can be defined for Σ \Sigma on the boundary of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} , such that F Σ F_{\Sigma} depends continuously on Σ \Sigma in the ‖ ⋅ ‖ 𝔼 \left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbb{E}} -topology. Proof. Motivated by Lemma , for Σ \Sigma on the boundary of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} , we can define 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) := J ⁡ ( U , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda):=J(U,\lambda) by picking U U such that Σ = U T ​ U \Sigma=U^{T}U . By Proposition , this does not depend on the choice of U U , and the functional 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) = J ⁡ ( U , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)=J(U,\lambda) depends continuously on Σ \Sigma , with the uniform modulus of continuity estimate | 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) − 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ′ , λ ) | ≤ 1 2 ​ λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X ′ − X | Σ 2 ) − 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X ′ − X | Σ ′ 2 ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 . |\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)-\mathcal{J}(\Sigma^{\prime};\lambda)|\leq\frac{1}{2\lambda}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{K}(|X^{\prime}-X|_{\Sigma}^{2})-\mathcal{K}(|X^{\prime}-X|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{2})|^{2}]^{1/2}. For positive semi-definite Σ \Sigma , we can still define F Σ := f U ∘ U F_{\Sigma}:=f_{U}\circ U . This depends only on U U through Σ = U T ​ U \Sigma=U^{T}U , by the integral equation characterization in Proposition , F Σ ​ ( x ) = 1 λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ( Y − F Σ ​ ( X ) ) ​ 𝒦 ​ ( | x − X | Σ 2 ) ] . F_{\Sigma}(x)=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}[(Y-F_{\Sigma}(X))\mathcal{K}(|x-X|_{\Sigma}^{2})]. Lemma implies that F Σ F_{\Sigma} depends continuously on Σ \Sigma in the ‖ ⋅ ‖ 𝔼 \left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbb{E}} -topology. ∎ Definition 4.6 . We say 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) extends to a C 1 C^{1} -function on a convex subset of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} , if there is a differential D ​ 𝒥 D\mathcal{J} on S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} which is a S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ V Sym^{2}V -valued continuous function on this subset, such that at any Σ , Σ ′ \Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime} in the subset, we have 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ′ , λ ) = 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) + ⟨ D ​ 𝒥 ​ ( Σ , λ ) , Σ ′ − Σ ⟩ + o ⁡ ( | Σ ′ − Σ | ) . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma^{\prime};\lambda)=\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)+\langle D\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda),\Sigma^{\prime}-\Sigma\rangle+o(|\Sigma^{\prime}-\Sigma|). Example 4.8 . The hypothesis of Proposition is satisfied, if the function 𝒦 ′ : ℝ ≥ 0 → ℝ \mathcal{K}^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R} is a bounded continuous function, and 𝔼 ⁡ [ | X | 4 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|X|^{4}]<+\infty . In particular, the Sobolev kernel with γ > 1 \gamma>1 (Example ), and the Gaussian kernel (Example ) satisfy this condition. Moreover, Proposition applies also in some cases where 𝒦 ′ \mathcal{K}^{\prime} is unbounded near the origin, such as Sobolev kernels with 0 < γ < 1 0<\gamma<1 in Example , where | 𝒦 ′ ​ ( r ) | = O ⁡ ( r γ − 1 ) |\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(r)|=O(r^{\gamma-1}) as r → 0 r\to 0 . Lemma 4.9 . Given a point in the boundary stratum 𝒮 l ⊂ S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 \mathcal{S}_{l}\subset Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} , there is a small compact convex neighbourhood in S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} such that the following holds. Suppose X X is a continuous random variable X X with bounded probability density in V V , and compact support in V V . Suppose the radial function 𝒦 ⁡ ( r ) \mathcal{K}(r) is C 1 C^{1} on ℝ + \mathbb{R}_{+} , and 𝒦 ′ \mathcal{K}^{\prime} remains bounded near infinity, and has a power law bound | 𝒦 ′ ​ ( r ) | = O ⁡ ( r − α ) |\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(r)|=O(r^{-\alpha}) near zero, where 0 < α < l / 4 0<\alpha<l/4 . Then the hypothesis of Proposition is satisfied on the convex neighbourhood. Proof. By the assumptions 𝒦 ′ ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) \mathcal{K}^{\prime}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2}) is only large if | X − X ′ | Σ 2 |X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2} is small, and | 𝒦 ′ ​ ( r ) | = O ⁡ ( r − α ) |\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(r)|=O(r^{-\alpha}) , so for t ≫ 1 t\gg 1 , ℙ ( | 𝒦 ′ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ≥ t ) ≤ ℙ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ≤ C t − 1 / 2 α ) . \mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})|^{2}\geq t)\leq\mathbb{P}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2}\leq Ct^{-1/2\alpha}). For any Σ \Sigma in the small neighbourhood of the boundary point in 𝒮 l \mathcal{S}_{l} , there are l l coordinate components of X − X ′ X-X^{\prime} , such that ∑ 1 l | ( X − X ′ ) i | 2 ≤ C ​ | X − X ′ | Σ 2 \sum_{1}^{l}|(X-X^{\prime})_{i}|^{2}\leq C|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2} . Thus ℙ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ≤ C t − 1 / 2 α ) ≤ ℙ ( ∑ 1 l | ( X − X ′ ) i | 2 ≤ C ′ t − 1 / 2 α ) \mathbb{P}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2}\leq Ct^{-1/2\alpha})\leq\mathbb{P}(\sum_{1}^{l}|(X-X^{\prime})_{i}|^{2}\leq C^{\prime}t^{-1/2\alpha}) The assumptions on X X implies that X − X ′ X-X^{\prime} has bounded density and compact support, so ℙ ( ∑ 1 l | ( X − X ′ ) i | 2 ≤ C t − 1 / 2 α ) ≤ C ′ t − l / 4 α , \mathbb{P}(\sum_{1}^{l}|(X-X^{\prime})_{i}|^{2}\leq Ct^{-1/2\alpha})\leq C^{\prime}t^{-l/4\alpha}, whence 𝔼 ⁡ [ | 𝒦 ′ ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ​ | X − X ′ | 4 ] ≤ C ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | 𝒦 ′ ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ] ≤ C ⁡ ( 1 + ∫ 1 ∞ ℙ ⁡ ( | 𝒦 ′ ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ≥ t ) ​ d t ) ≤ C ( 1 + ∫ 1 ∞ C ′ t − l / 4 α d t ) . \begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(|X-X^{\prime}|^{2}_{\Sigma})|^{2}|X-X^{\prime}|^{4}]\leq C\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(|X-X^{\prime}|^{2}_{\Sigma})|^{2}]\\ &\leq C(1+\int_{1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})|^{2}\geq t)dt)\\ &\leq C(1+\int_{1}^{\infty}C^{\prime}t^{-l/4\alpha}dt).\end{split} Since l / 4 ​ α > 1 l/4\alpha>1 by assumption, this integral converges. The estimate is uniform for all Σ \Sigma in the small neighbourhood, so the uniform integrability hypothesis is verified. The above discussion also shows that the contribution to the integral from the region with | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ≪ 1 |X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2}\ll 1 is small. Since 𝒦 ′ \mathcal{K}^{\prime} is continuous away from the origin, and X − X ′ X-X^{\prime} is bounded, we see the uniform modulus of continuity in Σ \Sigma . ∎ Remark 14 . The lesson from the proof of Lemma is that in order for the first variation to be very big near some boundary stratum 𝒮 l \mathcal{S}_{l} with l > 4 ​ α + 1 l>4\alpha+1 , then X − X ′ X-X^{\prime} should have local concentration along some subspace W ⊂ V W\subset V , where W W is the null subspace of Σ \Sigma . For applications to our companion paper, we also need the following criterion for the Lipschitz continuity of D ​ 𝒥 D\mathcal{J} . Corollary 4.10 . Suppose that 𝒦 ′ : ℝ ≥ 0 → ℝ \mathcal{K}^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R} is a bounded continuous function, r ​ 𝒦 ′ ​ ( r ) r\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(r) is bounded for large r r , and 𝒦 ′′ \mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime} is bounded. Suppose 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ] + 𝔼 ⁡ [ | X | 8 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[|X|^{8}]<+\infty . Then D ​ 𝒥 D\mathcal{J} extends to a Lipschitz continuous tensor valued function on S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ V ∗ Sym^{2}V^{*} . Proof. The conditions of the first variation formula Thm. and the C 1 C^{1} -extension Proposition hold in our setting. It suffices to prove D ​ 𝒥 D\mathcal{J} is Lipschitz continuous as a tensor field on S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} . We apply the first variation formula to estimate | D ​ 𝒥 ​ ( Σ , λ ) − D ​ 𝒥 ​ ( Σ ′ , λ ) | |D\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)-D\mathcal{J}(\Sigma^{\prime};\lambda)| . There are two sources of errors. The first error is 𝔼 ⁡ [ | r Σ ​ ( X , Y ) − r Σ ′ ​ ( X , Y ) | ​ | r Σ ​ ( X ′ , Y ′ ) ¯ | ​ | 𝒦 ′ ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ​ ( X − X ′ ) ⊗ ( X − X ′ ) | ] ≤ 𝔼 ​ [ | r Σ ​ ( X , Y ) − r Σ ′ ​ ( X , Y ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | r Σ ​ ( X ′ , Y ′ ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | X − X ′ | 4 ] 1 / 2 ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 ≤ 𝔼 ​ [ | F Σ ​ ( X ) − F Σ ′ ​ ( X ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] 1 / 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | X − X ′ | 4 ] 1 / 2 ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 ≤ C ​ λ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) − 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ ′ 2 ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | X − X ′ | 4 ] 1 / 2 ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 ≤ C ​ λ − 1 ​ | Σ − Σ ′ | ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | X | 4 ] ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 2 \begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[|r_{\Sigma}(X,Y)-r_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(X,Y)||\overline{r_{\Sigma}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}||\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})(X-X^{\prime})\otimes(X-X^{\prime})|]\\ \leq&\mathbb{E}[|r_{\Sigma}(X,Y)-r_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(X,Y)|^{2}]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[|r_{\Sigma}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})|^{2}]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[|X-X^{\prime}|^{4}]^{1/2}\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}\\ \leq&\mathbb{E}[|F_{\Sigma}(X)-F_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(X)|^{2}]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[|X-X^{\prime}|^{4}]^{1/2}\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}\\ \leq&C\lambda^{-1}\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|^{2}_{\Sigma})-\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|^{2}_{\Sigma^{\prime}})|^{2}]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|X-X^{\prime}|^{4}]^{1/2}\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}\\ \leq&C\lambda^{-1}|\Sigma-\Sigma^{\prime}|\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|X|^{4}]\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}^{2}\end{split} Here the second line uses Cauchy-Schwarz and the independence of ( X , Y ) (X,Y) with ( X ′ , Y ′ ) (X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}) , the third line uses the trivial upper bound Lem. , while the fourth line uses the quantitative continuity Lemma , and the last line uses mean value inequality. This gives a Lipschitz bound on the first error. The partial compactification S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ¯ \overline{Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}} consists of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} and the points at infinity. Here the points at infinity correspond to a pair ( W , Σ W ) (W,\Sigma_{W}) , where W ⊂ V W\subset V is a proper subspace of V V which is allowed to be zero, and Σ W \Sigma_{W} is a positive semidefinite form on W W . Definition 4.12 . (Topology at infinity) A sequence of Σ i ∈ S ​ y ​ m + 2 \Sigma_{i}\in Sym^{2}_{+} is said to converge to a point at infinity ( W , Σ W ) (W,\Sigma_{W}) , if the inner products on W W induced by the restriction of Σ i \Sigma_{i} converge to Σ W \Sigma_{W} , and the quotient inner products on V / W V/W induced by Σ i \Sigma_{i} are bounded below by Λ i 2 ​ I \Lambda_{i}^{2}I , where Λ i → + ∞ \Lambda_{i}\to+\infty . Proposition 4.13 . The function 𝒥 \mathcal{J} extends to a continuous function on the partial compactification S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ¯ \overline{Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}} . Proof. This is a reinterpretation of Theorem . Theorem then implies that 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) has a unique limit. ∎ Intuitively, the points at infinity in the partial compactification models scale separation and cluster decoupling, while the lower rank boundary strata of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} model variable selection. 4.5 Completely monotone kernels A distinguished class of rotationally symmetric kernels consists of kernels representable by a weighted sum of Gaussians, K ⁡ ( x ) = 𝒦 ⁡ ( | x | V 2 ) = ∫ 0 ∞ e − t ​ | x | V 2 ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( t ) , K(x)=\mathcal{K}(|x|^{2}_{V})=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t|x|_{V}^{2}}d\nu(t), (37) for some finite measure μ \mu . (38) In particular, the dimensional constants do not explicitly arise in the first variation formula ( ). (Gaussian sum representation) Suppose γ > 0 \gamma>0 , then k V ​ ( ω ) = ( 4 ​ π ) d / 2 Γ ⁡ ( γ ) ​ ∫ 0 + ∞ y γ + d / 2 − 1 ​ e − ( 1 + | 2 ​ π ​ ω | 2 ) ​ y ​ 𝑑 y . k_{V}(\omega)=\frac{(4\pi)^{d/2}}{\Gamma(\gamma)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}y^{\gamma+d/2-1}e^{-(1+|2\pi\omega|^{2})y}dy. The Sobolev kernel function is intensely studied classically, and admit formulae in terms of modified Bessel functions [ 34 ] . Using the method of steepest descent, one can deduce from the Gaussian sum representation formula that K γ ​ ( | x | 2 ) = O ⁡ ( | x | γ ​ e − | x | ) K_{\gamma}(|x|^{2})=O(|x|^{\gamma}e^{-|x|}) for large x x . For γ > 1 \gamma>1 , then 𝒦 ′ ​ ( r ) \mathcal{K}^{\prime}(r) is bounded near zero, while for 0 < γ < 1 0<\gamma<1 , then 𝒦 ′ ​ ( r ) = O ⁡ ( r γ − 1 ) \mathcal{K}^{\prime}(r)=O(r^{\gamma-1}) blows up near the origin. 5 Landscape of vacua: limiting configurations While the minimization of I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) for fixed U , λ U,\lambda reduces to solving a linear equation in an infinite dimensional function space, the functional 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\lambda) depends on Σ \Sigma in a highly nonlinear fashion. We now consider the problem of locally minimizing the functional 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) with respect to Σ \Sigma for fixed moderately small λ \lambda , and to avoid confusion, we shall refer to the local minima of 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) as vacua . This terminology comes from physics, where it refers to the locally energy minimizing field configurations, and the distinct vacua are separated by some energy barriers, often with distinct characteristic physical behavior. Formally speaking, Definition 5.1 . A vacuum in S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} is a local minimizer of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} in S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} . A vacuum at infinity is a point at infinity ( W , Σ W ) ∈ S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ¯ (W,\Sigma_{W})\in\overline{Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}} , such that for any sequence Σ i ∈ S ​ y ​ m + 2 \Sigma_{i}\in Sym^{2}_{+} converging to ( W , Σ W ) (W,\Sigma_{W}) , whenever i i is large enough, then 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ i , λ ) ≥ 𝒥 ⁡ ( ( W , Σ W ) , λ ) . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma_{i};\lambda)\geq\mathcal{J}((W,\Sigma_{W}),\lambda). In this section we focus on the question Question 5 . In the partial compactification S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ¯ \overline{Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}} , how can we test that Σ \Sigma is locally minimizing? What is the subleading effect of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} near a given vacuum? 5.1 Boundary vacua in S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} Recall that S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 = ⋃ 𝒮 l ∪ S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}=\bigcup\mathcal{S}_{l}\cup Sym^{2}_{+} , where 𝒮 l \mathcal{S}_{l} is the open stratum consisting of all rank l l positive semi-definite forms on V V . The 𝒮 l \mathcal{S}_{l} is a submanifold of dimension l ⁡ ( l + 1 ) 2 + l ⁡ ( d − l ) \frac{l(l+1)}{2}+l(d-l) inside S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ V ∗ Sym^{2}V^{*} . Every Σ ∈ 𝒮 l \Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{l} is equivalent to the data of a null subspace W ⊂ V W\subset V of dimension d − l d-l (whose choice accounts for l ⁡ ( d − l ) l(d-l) parameters), and a non-degenerate inner product on the quotient space V / W V/W (whose choice accounts for l ⁡ ( l + 1 ) 2 \frac{l(l+1)}{2} parameters). In this section, we consider a boundary point Σ ∈ 𝒮 l ⊂ S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 \Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{l}\subset Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} , and assume that the hypothesis of Proposition holds in a neighbourhood of Σ \Sigma , so 𝒥 \mathcal{J} is locally a C 1 C^{1} -function on S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} . We write W W as the null subspace of Σ \Sigma , and W ′ W^{\prime} as its orthogonal complement with respect to | ⋅ | V 2 |\cdot|_{V}^{2} , which induces an orthogonal direct sum decomposition V = W ⊕ W ′ V=W\oplus W^{\prime} , V ∗ = W ∗ ⊕ W ′ ⁣ ∗ , V^{*}=W^{*}\oplus W^{\prime*}, so that { S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ V ≃ S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ W ⊕ S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ W ′ ⊕ W ⊗ W ′ , S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ V ∗ ≃ S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ W ∗ ⊕ S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ W ′ ⁣ ∗ ⊕ W ∗ ⊗ W ′ ⁣ ∗ . \begin{cases}Sym^{2}V\simeq Sym^{2}W\oplus Sym^{2}W^{\prime}\oplus W\otimes W^{\prime},\\ Sym^{2}V^{*}\simeq Sym^{2}W^{*}\oplus Sym^{2}W^{\prime*}\oplus W^{*}\otimes W^{\prime*}.\end{cases} The tangent space of the submanifold 𝒮 l ⊂ S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ V ∗ \mathcal{S}_{l}\subset Sym^{2}V^{*} at the point Σ \Sigma is identified with S ​ y ​ m 2 ​ W ′ ⁣ ∗ ⊕ W ∗ ⊗ W ′ ⁣ ∗ Sym^{2}W^{\prime*}\oplus W^{*}\otimes W^{\prime*} . This follows from a similar computation as in Lemma . ∎ 5.1.3 Discussions 1. A boundary point Σ ∈ 𝒮 l \Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{l} specifies some signal variables X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} , and Y 0 Y_{0} measures the information of Y Y predictable from X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} , while Y 1 Y_{1} detects how Y Y depends on the secondary variables X W X_{W} conditional on X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} . Under the assumptions of Proposition , the first variation at Σ \Sigma only depends on Y 1 Y_{1} through the up to second order conditional moments 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y 1 ​ X W | X W ′ ] \mathbb{E}[Y_{1}X_{W}|X_{W^{\prime}}] (the ‘linear signal’), and 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y 1 ​ X W ⊗ X W | X W ′ ] \mathbb{E}[Y_{1}X_{W}\otimes X_{W}|X_{W^{\prime}}] (the ‘quadratic signal’). 2. Lemma suggests that if both the linear and quadratic signals are weak compared to Y 0 Y_{0} , and the covariance matrix of X W X_{W} conditional on X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} is independent of X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} , then 𝒥 \mathcal{J} has the tendency to increase as Σ \Sigma moves into the interior of S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} . If Σ \Sigma locally minimizes 𝒥 \mathcal{J} restricted to 𝒮 l \mathcal{S}_{l} , then we expect Σ \Sigma is a boundary vacuum. The interpretation is that the information of the secondary variables X W X_{W} is suppressed by the signal variables X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} , and the prediction function F Σ F_{\Sigma} depends only on X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} , which has lower dimension compared to X X , so we have achieved variable selection . 3. A competing effect is that by Lemma , the linear signal drives 𝒥 \mathcal{J} to decrease as Σ \Sigma moves into the interior of S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} . 4. If one wishes to design the algorithm so that the linear signal effect dominates, then one can first try to learn Y 0 = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y | X W ′ ] Y_{0}=\mathbb{E}[Y|X_{W^{\prime}}] to sufficient accuracy, which would be much more efficient than the higher dimensional problem of learning Y Y . Then one replaces Y Y with Y − Y 0 Y-Y_{0} , to reduce to the situation where Y 0 = 0 Y_{0}=0 . In the special case W ′ = 0 W^{\prime}=0 , then Y 0 = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ] Y_{0}=\mathbb{E}[Y] is simply the average value of Y Y , and the above discussion says that subtracting this average is beneficial for promoting the linear signal. This subtraction has a similar effect as the common practice of inserting an ‘intercept’, namely to introduce an extra parameter c c into the kernel ridge regression loss function 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y − f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) − c | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 , \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y-f(UX)-c|^{2}]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}, and minimize f f together with c c . 5.2 Vacua at infinity Question 6 . Does the functional 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) always achieve its minimum in S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} ? By Theorem , this question is sensitive to whether or not the marginal distribution of any component of X X contains a discrete part. In the continuous variable case, we have lim Σ → ∞ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] . \lim_{\Sigma\to\infty}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]. In particular no vacua can sit at infinity in S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ¯ \overline{Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}} . We now revisit the setting of discrete variables as in Section , where X X takes value in a finite number of points { a 1 , … ​ a m } ⊂ V \{a_{1},\ldots a_{m}\}\subset V , with ℙ ⁡ ( X = a i ) = p i \mathbb{P}(X=a_{i})=p_{i} , and Y = y i Y=y_{i} conditional on X = a i X=a_{i} . The representer theorem Proposition says 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ 1 m p i ​ y i 2 − 1 2 ​ ∑ i , j = 1 m ( λ ​ M − 1 + P ) i ​ j − 1 ​ p i ​ y i ​ p j ​ y ¯ j , \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1}^{m}p_{i}y_{i}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}(\lambda M^{-1}+P)^{-1}_{ij}p_{i}y_{i}p_{j}\bar{y}_{j}, where M = ( M i ​ j ) M=(M_{ij}) is the m × m m\times m inner product matrix with entries M i ​ j = K ⁡ ( U ​ a i , U ​ a j ) = K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( a i − a j ) ) = 𝒦 ⁡ ( | a i − a j | Σ 2 ) , M_{ij}=K(Ua_{i},Ua_{j})=K(U(a_{i}-a_{j}))=\mathcal{K}(|a_{i}-a_{j}|_{\Sigma}^{2}), and P = diag ​ ( p 1 , … ​ p m ) P=\text{diag}(p_{1},\ldots p_{m}) encodes the probability of the atoms at a i a_{i} . Corollary 5.9 . (Asymptotic for large Σ \Sigma ) Suppose that min i , j ⁡ | a i − a j | Σ \min_{i,j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|_{\Sigma} is large. Then 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ i = 1 m λ ​ p i λ + p i ​ | y i | 2 − λ 2 ​ ∑ i ≠ j , 1 ≤ i , j ≤ m p i ​ y i λ + p i ​ p j ​ y ¯ j λ + p j ​ 𝒦 ​ ( | a i − a j | Σ 2 ) + O ⁡ ( | M − I | 2 ) . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda p_{i}}{\lambda+p_{i}}|y_{i}|^{2}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{i\neq j,1\leq i,j\leq m}\frac{p_{i}y_{i}}{\lambda+p_{i}}\frac{p_{j}\bar{y}_{j}}{\lambda+p_{j}}\mathcal{K}(|a_{i}-a_{j}|_{\Sigma}^{2})+O(|M-I|^{2}). Proof. By the decay of the kernel function, M = I + o ⁡ ( 1 ) M=I+o(1) when min i , j ⁡ | a i − a j | Σ → + ∞ \min_{i,j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|_{\Sigma}\to+\infty . Thus we can make the expansion ( P + λ ​ M − 1 ) − 1 = ( P + λ ​ I − λ ⁡ ( M − I ) + O ⁡ ( | M − I | 2 ) ) − 1 = ( P + λ ​ I ) − 1 + λ ​ ( P + λ ​ I ) − 1 ​ ( M − I ) ​ ( P + λ ​ I ) − 1 + O ⁡ ( | M − I | 2 ) . \begin{split}&(P+\lambda M^{-1})^{-1}=(P+\lambda I-\lambda(M-I)+O(|M-I|^{2}))^{-1}\\ =&(P+\lambda I)^{-1}+\lambda(P+\lambda I)^{-1}(M-I)(P+\lambda I)^{-1}+O(|M-I|^{2}).\end{split} Using the representer theorem, 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ 1 m p i ​ y i 2 − 1 2 ​ ∑ i , j = 1 m ( λ ​ M − 1 + P ) i ​ j − 1 ​ p i ​ y i ​ p j ​ y ¯ j = 1 2 ​ ∑ i = 1 m λ ​ p i λ + p i ​ | y i | 2 − λ 2 ​ ∑ i , j = 1 m p i ​ y i λ + p i ​ p j ​ y ¯ j λ + p j ​ ( M − I ) i ​ j + O ⁡ ( | M − I | 2 ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ i = 1 m λ ​ p i λ + p i ​ | y i | 2 − λ 2 ​ ∑ i ≠ j p i ​ y i λ + p i ​ p j ​ y ¯ j λ + p j ​ 𝒦 ​ ( | a i − a j | Σ 2 ) + O ⁡ ( | M − I | 2 ) . \begin{split}&\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1}^{m}p_{i}y_{i}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}(\lambda M^{-1}+P)^{-1}_{ij}p_{i}y_{i}p_{j}\bar{y}_{j}\\ =&\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda p_{i}}{\lambda+p_{i}}|y_{i}|^{2}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\frac{p_{i}y_{i}}{\lambda+p_{i}}\frac{p_{j}\bar{y}_{j}}{\lambda+p_{j}}(M-I)_{ij}+O(|M-I|^{2})\\ =&\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda p_{i}}{\lambda+p_{i}}|y_{i}|^{2}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{p_{i}y_{i}}{\lambda+p_{i}}\frac{p_{j}\bar{y}_{j}}{\lambda+p_{j}}\mathcal{K}(|a_{i}-a_{j}|_{\Sigma}^{2})+O(|M-I|^{2}).\end{split} as required. ∎ Remark 15 . The asymptotic expansion in Corollary is a fixed- λ \lambda statement . Besides the explicit λ \lambda -dependence of the leading and subleading terms, the constant implicit in the O ⁡ ( | M − I | 2 ) O(|M-I|^{2}) remainder also depends on λ \lambda . Consequently, the expansion is not uniform in the limit λ → 0 + \lambda\to 0^{+} . Extending the analysis to a singular regime in which λ → 0 + \lambda\to 0^{+} would require controlling the joint behavior of the limits Σ → ∞ \Sigma\to\infty and λ → 0 + \lambda\to 0^{+} , which lies beyond the scope of the present paper. 5.2.1 Discussions 1. The limiting value is lim Σ → + ∞ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ i = 1 m λ ​ p i λ + p i ​ | y i | 2 . \lim_{\Sigma\to+\infty}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda p_{i}}{\lambda+p_{i}}|y_{i}|^{2}. (44) Notice that the contributions of the different atoms completely decouple, and we recover Theorem . 2. We notice a few features of the limiting value ( ). First, this formula is an increasing function of λ \lambda , and goes to zero as λ → 0 \lambda\to 0 . The interpretation is that there is a tradeoff between regularization effect and approximation, and perfect approximation is only achieved when the regularization goes to zero. Second, for fixed λ \lambda , if m m is large and max ⁡ p i \max p_{i} is small compared to λ \lambda (e.g. if max ⁡ p i \max p_{i} comparable to m − 1 m^{-1} ), then ( ) is close to 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ | Y | 2 \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}|Y|^{2} . The interpretation is that discrete variables behave like continuous variables when the probability density is spread out throughout many atoms. 3. Corollary admits a nice ‘physical’ interpretation: in the large Σ \Sigma regime, the m m particles interact via a potential of the form − λ 2 ∑ i , j = 1 m p i ​ y i λ + p i p j ​ y ¯ j λ + p j 𝒦 ( | a i − a j | Σ 2 ) . -\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\frac{p_{i}y_{i}}{\lambda+p_{i}}\frac{p_{j}\bar{y}_{j}}{\lambda+p_{j}}\mathcal{K}(|a_{i}-a_{j}|_{\Sigma}^{2}). The higher term O ⁡ ( | M − I | 2 ) O(|M-I|^{2}) is negligible. For completely monotone kernels 𝒦 \mathcal{K} is positive, so the sign of the potential depends on the signs of Re ​ ( y i ​ y ¯ j ) \text{Re}(y_{i}\bar{y}_{j}) , which physically corresponds to attractive vs. repulsive forces between the particles. 4. Suppose m = 2 m=2 , and Re ​ ( y 1 ​ y ¯ 2 ) < 0 \text{Re}(y_{1}\bar{y}_{2})<0 . For all Σ \Sigma such that min i , j ⁡ | a i − a j | Σ \min_{i,j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|_{\Sigma} is sufficiently large, we have 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) > 1 2 ​ ∑ i = 1 m λ ​ p i λ + p i ​ | y i | 2 . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)>\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda p_{i}}{\lambda+p_{i}}|y_{i}|^{2}. The interpretation is that 1 2 ​ ∑ i = 1 m p i 2 λ + p i ​ | y i | 2 \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{p_{i}^{2}}{\lambda+p_{i}}|y_{i}|^{2} should be viewed as a local minimum value for 𝒥 \mathcal{J} , achieved at a vacuum at infinity . Consequently, vacua at infinity can occur sometimes in the presence of discrete structures . The converse is not true: the presence of discrete structure does not imply the existence of any vacua at infinity. The easiest example is when m = 2 m=2 and Re ​ ( y 1 ​ y ¯ 2 ) > 0 \text{Re}(y_{1}\bar{y}_{2})>0 . 5. For Sobolev and Gaussian kernels, the potential decays exponentially at infinity. If we would like to detect the vacua at infinity by running some gradient flow on S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} , then we expect to require infinite time to reach such vacua. To speed up the flow, one should use a kernel function 𝒦 \mathcal{K} which decays more slowly at infinity. 6. More generally Σ \Sigma can converge to infinity, but remain bounded on some proper subspace W ⊂ V W\subset V . The limiting value for 𝒥 \mathcal{J} is sensitive to the choice of W W . 6 Cluster interactions In the kernel ridge regression problem, we now assume there is a hidden discrete random variable X 0 X_{0} valued in { 1 , 2 , … ​ m } \{1,2,\dots m\} , which is not directly accessible to the observer, but controls how the data ( X , Y ) (X,Y) is generated. One should imagine that for different X 0 = i X_{0}=i , the conditional probability distributions of X X have distinct characteristic behaviors, e.g., their supports may cluster around far separated points, or they may have very different scale parameters. Typical examples are Example and . We shall develop more functional analytic tools to analyze the interactions between clusters of probability distributions. The main result is a collection of operator norm estimates for ‘cluster operators’, showing that interactions between clusters are weak when these clusters are sufficiently separated in space or scale. As a consequence, the kernel ridge regression problem approximately decomposes into simpler cluster-wise problems. These operator estimates have two main applications. First, they provide a quantitative, non-asymptotic counterpart to the asymptotic decoupling phenomena discussed in Section ; see Section . Second, they form a key technical ingredient in the analysis of multi-scale data distributions; specifically, with the dimensional reduction mechanism developed in Section , they allow us to isolate the contributions of individual clusters and construct vacua associated with different scales, see Section . 6.1 Operator theoretic framework The Euler-Lagrange equation ( ) can be cast in the operator theoretic framework. Following Cucker and Smale’s work on standard kernel ridge regression [ 15 ] , we define the bounded self-adjoint operator T T by 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) ​ g ¯ ​ ( U ​ X ) ] = ( T ​ f , g ) ℋ , ∀ g ∈ ℋ . \mathbb{E}[f(UX)\bar{g}(UX)]=(Tf,g)_{\mathcal{H}},\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{H}. By substituting g = K ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) g=K(x,\cdot) , we obtain the explicit formula T ​ f ​ ( x ) = ( T ​ f , K ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) ) ℋ = 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) ​ K ⁡ ( x , U ​ X ) ¯ ] . Tf(x)=(Tf,K(x,\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathbb{E}[f(UX)\overline{K(x,UX)}]. We define 𝒴 ∈ ℋ \mathcal{Y}\in\mathcal{H} by Riesz representation, 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ​ g ¯ ​ ( U ​ X ) ] = ( 𝒴 , g ) ℋ , ∀ g ∈ ℋ . \mathbb{E}[Y\bar{g}(UX)]=(\mathcal{Y},g)_{\mathcal{H}},\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{H}. This admits the explicit formula 𝒴 = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ​ K ⁡ ( ⋅ , U ​ X ) ¯ ] . \mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{E}[Y\overline{K(\cdot,UX)}]. Then the Euler-Lagrange equation ( ) becomes a linear equation in the Hilbert space ℋ \mathcal{H} , ( λ ​ I + T ) ​ f U = 𝒴 . (\lambda I+T)f_{U}=\mathcal{Y}. (45) Lemma 6.1 . Let ( X ′ , Y ′ ) (X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}) be an independent copy of ( X , Y ) (X,Y) . Then ‖ 𝒴 ‖ ℋ 2 = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) ] . \left\lVert\mathcal{Y}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathbb{E}[Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}K(UX,UX^{\prime})]. Proof. We apply the reproducing kernel property, and the fact that K ⁡ ( x , y ) = K ⁡ ( y , x ) ¯ K(x,y)=\overline{K(y,x)} , to compute ‖ 𝒴 ‖ ℋ 2 = ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ​ K ​ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) ] , 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ′ ​ K ​ ( U ​ X ′ , ⋅ ) ] ) ℋ = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ ( K ⁡ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) , K ⁡ ( U ​ X ′ , ⋅ ) ) ℋ ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) ] \begin{split}\left\lVert\mathcal{Y}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}=&(\mathbb{E}[YK(UX,\cdot)],\mathbb{E}[Y^{\prime}K(UX^{\prime},\cdot)])_{\mathcal{H}}\\ =&\mathbb{E}[Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}(K(UX,\cdot),K(UX^{\prime},\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}]\\ =&\mathbb{E}[Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}K(UX,UX^{\prime})]\end{split} as required. ∎ Corollary 6.2 . (Decay estimate for the minimizer) We have { λ ​ ‖ f U ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ≤ 1 λ ​ ‖ 𝒴 ‖ ℋ 2 = 1 λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) ] , J ( U , λ ) ≥ 1 2 𝔼 [ | Y | 2 ] − ( 2 λ ) − 1 𝔼 [ Y Y ¯ ′ K ( U X , U X ′ ) ) ] . \begin{cases}\lambda\left\lVert f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)|^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\lVert\mathcal{Y}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}[Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}K(UX,UX^{\prime})],\\ J(U,\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-(2\lambda)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}K(UX,UX^{\prime}))].\end{cases} In particular, if X X is a continuous variable, then λ ​ ‖ f U ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] → 0 , U → ∞ . \lambda\left\lVert f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)|^{2}]\to 0,\quad U\to\infty. Proof. Using the Euler-Lagrange equation, λ ​ ‖ f U ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ ( ( λ ​ I + T ) ​ f U , f U ) ℋ = ( 𝒴 , f U ) ℋ ≤ ‖ f U ‖ ℋ ​ ‖ 𝒴 ‖ ℋ , \lambda\left\lVert f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq((\lambda I+T)f_{U},f_{U})_{\mathcal{H}}=(\mathcal{Y},f_{U})_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\left\lVert f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\left\lVert\mathcal{Y}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}, hence ‖ f U ‖ ℋ ≤ λ − 1 ​ ‖ 𝒴 ‖ ℋ \left\lVert f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\lambda^{-1}\left\lVert\mathcal{Y}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}} , so λ ​ ‖ f U ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ≤ 1 λ ​ ‖ 𝒴 ‖ ℋ 2 = 1 λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) ] . \lambda\left\lVert f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)|^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\lVert\mathcal{Y}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}[Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}K(UX,UX^{\prime})]. Therefore J ⁡ ( U , λ ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − 1 2 ​ E ​ [ Y ​ f U ​ ( U ​ X ) ¯ ] = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − 1 2 ​ ( 𝒴 , f U ) ℋ ≥ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − ( 2 ​ λ ) − 1 ​ ‖ 𝒴 ‖ ℋ 2 ≥ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − ( 2 ​ λ ) − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) ] . \begin{split}J(U,\lambda)=&\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\frac{1}{2}E[Y\overline{f_{U}(UX)}]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{Y},f_{U})_{\mathcal{H}}\\ \geq&\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-(2\lambda)^{-1}\left\lVert\mathcal{Y}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-(2\lambda)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}K(UX,UX^{\prime})].\end{split} By Cauchy-Schwarz, 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | K ⁡ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 . \mathbb{E}[Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}K(UX,UX^{\prime})]\leq\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|K(UX,UX^{\prime})|^{2}]^{1/2}. In particular when X X is a continuous variable, then X − X ′ X-X^{\prime} is a continuous variable, so by dominated convergence 𝔼 ⁡ [ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) | 2 ] → 0 \mathbb{E}[|K(U(X-X^{\prime}))|^{2}]\to 0 as U → ∞ U\to\infty , whence λ ​ ‖ f U ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f U ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] → 0 \lambda\left\lVert f_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}(UX)|^{2}]\to 0 . ∎ 6.1.1 Operator norm estimates Proposition 6.3 . (Operator norm estimate) Let X ′ X^{\prime} denote an independent copy of X X . Then ‖ T ​ f ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ ‖ f ‖ C 0 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) | ] ≤ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) | ] , \left\lVert Tf\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{0}}^{2}\mathbb{E}[|K(U(X-X^{\prime}))|]\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathbb{E}[|K(U(X-X^{\prime}))|], ‖ T ​ f ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 , \left\lVert Tf\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[|f(UX)|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|K(U(X-X^{\prime}))|^{2}]^{1/2}, ‖ T 𝒴 ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ 𝔼 [ | Y | 2 ] 𝔼 [ | K ( U ( X − X ′ ) | 2 ] . \left\lVert T\mathcal{Y}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|K(U(X-X^{\prime})|^{2}]. Moreover, the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of T T acting on ℋ \mathcal{H} is ‖ T ‖ H ​ S 2 = 𝔼 [ | K ( U ( X − X ′ ) | 2 ] . \left\lVert T\right\rVert_{HS}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[|K(U(X-X^{\prime})|^{2}]. Proof. We use the T ∗ ​ T T^{*}T -argument. For any g ∈ ℋ g\in\mathcal{H} , we compute ( T ∗ ​ T ​ f , g ) ℋ = ( T ​ f , T ​ g ) ℋ = 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( U ​ X ′ ) ​ T ​ g ​ ( U ​ X ′ ) ¯ ] . Moreover by Cauchy-Schwarz, ‖ T ​ f ‖ ℋ 2 = 𝔼 ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( U ​ X ′ ) ​ K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ¯ ​ f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) ¯ ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 . \left\lVert Tf\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[f(UX^{\prime})\overline{K(U(X-X^{\prime}))}\overline{f(UX)}]\leq\mathbb{E}[|f(UX)|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|K(U(X-X^{\prime}))|^{2}]^{1/2}. We now compute the Hilbert-Schmidt norm, by taking an orthonormal basis ψ i \psi_{i} of ℋ \mathcal{H} , and repeatedly applying the reproducing kernel property: ‖ T ‖ H ​ S 2 = ∑ i ‖ T ​ ψ i ‖ ℋ 2 = 𝔼 ⁡ [ ∑ i ψ i ​ ( U ​ X ′ ) ​ K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ¯ ​ ψ i ​ ( U ​ X ) ¯ ] = ∑ i 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( ψ i , K ⁡ ( U ​ X ′ , ⋅ ) ) ℋ ​ K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ¯ ​ ( ψ i , K ⁡ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) ) ℋ ¯ ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ ( K ⁡ ( U ​ X , ⋅ ) , K ⁡ ( U ​ X ′ , ⋅ ) ) ℋ ​ K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ¯ ] = 𝔼 [ K ( U X , U X ′ ) K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ¯ ] = 𝔼 [ | K ( U ( X − X ′ ) | 2 ] . \begin{split}&\left\lVert T\right\rVert_{HS}^{2}=\sum_{i}\left\lVert T\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\sum_{i}\psi_{i}(UX^{\prime})\overline{K(U(X-X^{\prime}))}\overline{\psi_{i}(UX)}]\\ =&\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}[(\psi_{i},K(UX^{\prime},\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}\overline{K(U(X-X^{\prime}))}\overline{(\psi_{i},K(UX,\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}}]\\ =&\mathbb{E}[(K(UX,\cdot),K(UX^{\prime},\cdot))_{\mathcal{H}}\overline{K(U(X-X^{\prime}))}]\\ =&\mathbb{E}[K(UX,UX^{\prime})\overline{K(U(X-X^{\prime}))}]=\mathbb{E}[|K(U(X-X^{\prime})|^{2}].\end{split} as required. We note that similar computational ideas appeared in [ 20 ] . ∎ We now derive some sufficient conditions for the operator norm of T T to be small, in the case of completely monotone kernels. Lemma 6.4 . Let W ⊂ V W\subset V be a proper subspace, and the probability density of the marginal distribution of X X on V / W V/W has L ∞ L^{\infty} -bound by p 0 p_{0} with respect to the Lebesgue measure on W ′ ≃ V / W W^{\prime}\simeq V/W . Suppose K ⁡ ( x ) = 𝒦 ⁡ ( | x | V 2 ) K(x)=\mathcal{K}(|x|_{V}^{2}) is the completely monotone kernel ( ). The positive semi-definite Σ \Sigma induces the quotient inner product Σ V / W \Sigma_{V/W} on V / W V/W , namely ‖ x ‖ Σ V / W 2 = min ⁡ { | x + y | Σ 2 : y ∈ W } . \left\lVert x\right\rVert_{\Sigma_{V/W}}^{2}=\min\{|x+y|_{\Sigma}^{2}:y\in W\}. Then 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ] ≤ p 0 det Σ V / W ​ ∫ 0 ∞ ( π ​ t − 1 ) d W ′ / 2 ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( t ) . \mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})]\leq\frac{p_{0}}{\sqrt{\det\Sigma_{V/W}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\pi t^{-1})^{d_{W^{\prime}}/2}d\nu(t). 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝒦 2 ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ] ≤ p 0 det Σ V / W ​ ∬ ( π ​ ( t + s ) − 1 ) d W ′ / 2 ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( s ) . \mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}^{2}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})]\leq\frac{p_{0}}{\sqrt{\det\Sigma_{V/W}}}\iint(\pi(t+s)^{-1})^{d_{W^{\prime}}/2}d\nu(t)d\nu(s). Proof. For the completely monotone kernel, 𝒦 \mathcal{K} is a decreasing function non-negative function, so 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ V / W 2 ) ] = 𝔼 [ 𝔼 [ 𝒦 ( | X W ′ − X ′ W ′ | Σ V / W 2 ) | | X W ′ , X W ′ ′ ] ] ≤ 𝔼 X W ′ ​ [ p 0 ​ ∫ W ′ 𝒦 ⁡ ( | y − X W ′ | Σ V / W 2 ) ​ d y ] = p 0 ​ ∫ W ′ 𝒦 ⁡ ( | y | Σ V / W 2 ) ​ 𝑑 y = p 0 det Σ V / W ​ ∫ W ′ 𝒦 ⁡ ( | y | 2 ) ​ d y . \begin{split}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})]&\leq\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma_{V/W}}^{2})]\\ &=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}(|X_{W^{\prime}}-X^{\prime}_{W^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma_{V/W}}^{2})||X_{W^{\prime}},X_{W^{\prime}}^{\prime}]]\\ &\leq\mathbb{E}_{X_{W^{\prime}}}[p_{0}\int_{W^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}(|y-X_{W^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma_{V/W}}^{2})dy]\\ &=p_{0}\int_{W^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}(|y|_{\Sigma_{V/W}}^{2})dy\\ &=\frac{p_{0}}{\sqrt{\det\Sigma_{V/W}}}\int_{W^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}(|y|^{2})dy.\end{split} where the second line uses conditional expectation, and the third line uses the L ∞ L^{\infty} -bound on the marginal density of X W ′ ′ X_{W^{\prime}}^{\prime} . By the Gaussian sum representation ( ), ∫ W ′ 𝒦 ⁡ ( | y | 2 ) ​ 𝑑 y = ∫ 0 ∞ e − t ​ | y | 2 ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( t ) = ∫ 0 ∞ ( π ​ t − 1 ) d W ′ / 2 ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( t ) . \int_{W^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}(|y|^{2})dy=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t|y|^{2}}d\nu(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}(\pi t^{-1})^{d_{W^{\prime}}/2}d\nu(t). hence the first statement. If 𝒦 \mathcal{K} is completely monotone, then so is 𝒦 2 \mathcal{K}^{2} (See Section ). The same argument then gives 𝔼 [ 𝒦 2 ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ] ≤ p 0 det Σ V / W ∫ 0 ∞ ( π t − 1 ) d W ′ / 2 d ν ~ ( t ) = p 0 det Σ V / W ​ ∬ ( π ​ ( t + s ) − 1 ) d W ′ / 2 ​ d ν ​ ( t ) ​ d ν ​ ( s ) . \begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}^{2}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2}]\leq\frac{p_{0}}{\sqrt{\det\Sigma_{V/W}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\pi t^{-1})^{d_{W^{\prime}}/2}d\tilde{\nu}(t)\\ =&\frac{p_{0}}{\sqrt{\det\Sigma_{V/W}}}\iint(\pi(t+s)^{-1})^{d_{W^{\prime}}/2}d\nu(t)d\nu(s).\end{split} as required. ∎ The upshot is that under quantitative bounds on the probability density of the marginal distribution on V / W V/W , then the operator norm of T T is small when det Σ V / W \det\Sigma_{V/W} is very large. Notice also that while V V may be very high dimensional, W ′ W^{\prime} may be low dimensional, and to apply the result we only need to know the marginal distribution. Example 6.5 . (Gaussian example) Suppose X X is a Gaussian variable with covariance matrix C ​ o ​ v ​ ( X ) = ( C i ​ j ) Cov(X)=(C^{ij}) in the coordinates on V V , so the inverse covariance matrix is C ​ o ​ v ​ ( X ) − 1 = ( C i ​ j ) Cov(X)^{-1}=(C_{ij}) , and X − X ′ X-X^{\prime} has covariance matrix 2 ​ C ​ o ​ v ​ ( X ) 2Cov(X) . Suppose K ⁡ ( x ) = 𝒦 ⁡ ( | x | V 2 ) K(x)=\mathcal{K}(|x|_{V}^{2}) is the completely monotone kernel ( ), then 𝔼 [ 𝒦 ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ] = ∫ V 1 ( 4 ​ π ) d / 2 ​ det ( C i ​ j ) exp ( − 1 4 C i ​ j x i x j ) 𝒦 ( | x | Σ 2 ) d x = ∫ 0 ∞ ∫ V 1 ( 4 ​ π ) d / 2 ​ det ( C i ​ j ) ​ exp ⁡ ( − 1 4 ​ C i ​ j ​ x i ​ x j − t ​ | x | Σ 2 ) ​ d x ​ d ν ​ ( t ) = ∫ 0 ∞ 1 det ( C i ​ j ) ​ det ( C i ​ j + 4 ​ t ​ Σ i ​ j ) ​ d ν ​ ( t ) = ∫ 0 ∞ 1 det ( δ i ​ j + 4 ​ t ​ C i ​ k ​ Σ k ​ j ) ​ d ν ​ ( t ) . \begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2}]=\int_{V}\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{d/2}\sqrt{\det(C^{ij})}}\exp(-\frac{1}{4}C_{ij}x^{i}x^{j})\mathcal{K}(|x|_{\Sigma}^{2})dx\\ =&\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{V}\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{d/2}\sqrt{\det(C^{ij})}}\exp(-\frac{1}{4}C_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}-t|x|_{\Sigma}^{2})dxd\nu(t)\\ =&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det(C^{ij})}\sqrt{\det(C_{ij}+4t\Sigma_{ij})}}d\nu(t)\\ =&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det(\delta_{ij}+4tC^{ik}\Sigma_{kj})}}d\nu(t).\end{split} This is small when Σ \Sigma is large compared to C ​ o ​ v − 1 ​ ( X ) Cov^{-1}(X) in at least one direction. Example 6.6 . Suppose X X is a discrete variable consisting of a large number of atoms at a i ∈ V a_{i}\in V , each with very small probability p i p_{i} , and suppose all the atoms are far separated with respect to the inner product Σ \Sigma . Then 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ] = ∑ i , j p i ​ p j ​ 𝒦 ​ ( | a i − a j | Σ 2 ) , \mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})]=\sum_{i,j}p_{i}p_{j}\mathcal{K}(|a_{i}-a_{j}|_{\Sigma}^{2}), which will be a small quantity of an order comparable to O ⁡ ( ∑ p i 2 ) O(\sum p_{i}^{2}) . The intuition is that a very dispersed discrete variable behaves like a continuous variable. 6.2 Cluster operators We let χ i = 𝟙 X 0 = i \chi_{i}=\mathbbm{1}_{X_{0}=i} , and define a self-adjoint operator T i T_{i} by 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ f ​ ( U ​ X ) ​ g ¯ ​ ( U ​ X ) ] = ( T i ​ f , g ) ℋ , ∀ g ∈ ℋ , \mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}f(UX)\bar{g}(UX)]=(T_{i}f,g)_{\mathcal{H}},\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{H}, This induces the decomposition T = ∑ T i T=\sum T_{i} , with each T i T_{i} describing one ‘cluster’. As in Section , the explicit formula for T i T_{i} is T i ​ f ​ ( x ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ f ​ ( U ​ X ) ​ K ⁡ ( x , U ​ X ) ¯ ] . T_{i}f(x)=\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}f(UX)\overline{K(x,UX)}]. (46) Similarly, we define 𝒴 i ∈ ℋ \mathcal{Y}_{i}\in\mathcal{H} by 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ Y ​ g ¯ ​ ( U ​ X ) ] = ( 𝒴 i , g ) ℋ , ∀ g ∈ ℋ , \mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}Y\bar{g}(UX)]=(\mathcal{Y}_{i},g)_{\mathcal{H}},\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{H}, so 𝒴 i = 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ Y ​ K ⁡ ( ⋅ , U ​ X ) ¯ ] \mathcal{Y}_{i}=\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}Y\overline{K(\cdot,UX)}] gives a decomposition 𝒴 = ∑ i 𝒴 i \mathcal{Y}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{Y}_{i} . The following Proposition can be proved by the same arguments as Proposition . Proposition 6.7 . Let X ′ X^{\prime} denote an independent copy of X X , and let χ i = 𝟙 X 0 = i \chi_{i}=\mathbbm{1}_{X_{0}=i} and χ i ′ = 𝟙 X 0 ′ = i \chi_{i}^{\prime}=\mathbbm{1}_{X_{0}^{\prime}=i} . Then ‖ T i ​ f ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ ‖ f ‖ C 0 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ χ i ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) | ] ≤ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ χ i ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) | ] , \left\lVert T_{i}f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{0}}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{i}^{\prime}|K(U(X-X^{\prime}))|]\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{i}^{\prime}|K(U(X-X^{\prime}))|], ‖ T i ​ f ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ | f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ χ i ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 , \left\lVert T_{i}f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|f(UX)|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{i}^{\prime}|K(U(X-X^{\prime}))|^{2}]^{1/2}, The Hilbert-Schmidt norm of T i T_{i} acting on ℋ \mathcal{H} is ‖ T i ‖ H ​ S 2 = 𝔼 [ χ i χ i ′ | K ( U ( X − X ′ ) | 2 ] . \left\lVert T_{i}\right\rVert_{HS}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{i}^{\prime}|K(U(X-X^{\prime})|^{2}]. Moreover the operator T i ​ T j T_{i}T_{j} satisfies the bound ‖ T i ​ T j ​ f ‖ ℋ ≤ ‖ f ‖ C 0 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) | ] \left\lVert T_{i}T_{j}f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{0}}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}|K(U(X-X^{\prime}))|] ‖ T i ​ T j ​ f ‖ ℋ 2 ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ j ​ | f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) | 2 ] . \left\lVert T_{i}T_{j}f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[\chi_{j}|f(UX)|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}|K(U(X-X^{\prime}))|^{2}]. The estimates on the operator norms of T T apply also to T i T_{i} with cosmetic changes. Moreover, there are two basic mechanisms for ‖ T i ​ T j ‖ \left\lVert T_{i}T_{j}\right\rVert to be small: the clusters may be far separated, or have very different scale parameters. Lemma 6.8 . Suppose K ⁡ ( x ) = 𝒦 ⁡ ( | x | V 2 ) K(x)=\mathcal{K}(|x|_{V}^{2}) is a completely monotone kernel function, and suppose dist Σ ​ ( supp ​ ( χ i ​ X ) , supp ​ ( χ j ​ X ) ) ≥ a . \text{dist}_{\Sigma}(\text{supp}(\chi_{i}X),\text{supp}(\chi_{j}X))\geq a. Then 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) | ] ≤ 𝒦 ⁡ ( a 2 ) ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ j ] . \mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}|\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})|]\leq\mathcal{K}(a^{2})\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}]\mathbb{E}[\chi_{j}]. In particular, for the Sobolev kernel or the Gaussian kernel, the function 𝒦 \mathcal{K} decays exponentially, so the operator norm of T i ​ T j T_{i}T_{j} is exponentially small. The interpretation is that spatially far separated clusters have very weak interactions . Lemma 6.9 . Let W ⊂ V W\subset V be a proper subspace, and the probability density of the marginal distribution of χ i ​ X \chi_{i}X on V / W V/W has L ∞ L^{\infty} -bound by p 0 p_{0} with respect to the Lebesgue measure on W ′ ≃ V / W W^{\prime}\simeq V/W . Suppose K ⁡ ( x ) = 𝒦 ⁡ ( | x | V 2 ) K(x)=\mathcal{K}(|x|_{V}^{2}) is the completely monotone kernel ( ). The positive semi-definite form Σ \Sigma induces the quotient inner product Σ V / W \Sigma_{V/W} on V / W V/W . Then 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ 𝒦 ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ] ≤ p 0 det Σ V / W ​ ∫ 0 ∞ ( π ​ t − 1 ) d W ′ / 2 ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( t ) . \mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})]\leq\frac{p_{0}}{\sqrt{\det\Sigma_{V/W}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\pi t^{-1})^{d_{W^{\prime}}/2}d\nu(t). This situation allows the support of χ i ​ X \chi_{i}X and χ j ​ X \chi_{j}X to have overlaps, but when the marginal distribution of χ i ​ X \chi_{i}X is very shallow and spread out, while χ j ​ X \chi_{j}X is very concentrated, then p 0 det Σ V / W \frac{p_{0}}{\sqrt{\det\Sigma_{V/W}}} would be small. The interpretation is that scale separation between two clusters can lead to weakness of interactions . 6.3 Non-interaction between far separated clusters We now construct an approximate solution to the Euler-Lagrange equation ( ). Each cluster leads to a decoupled Euler-Lagrange equation ( λ ​ I + T i ) ​ f i = 𝒴 i , (\lambda I+T_{i})f_{i}=\mathcal{Y}_{i}, whose solution f i f_{i} is the minimizer of the functional I i ​ ( f , U , λ ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ | Y − f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 . I_{i}(f,U,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|Y-f(UX)|^{2}]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}. We set f ~ U = ∑ 1 m f i \tilde{f}_{U}=\sum_{1}^{m}f_{i} . The intuition is that f ~ U \tilde{f}_{U} is a good approximation of f U f_{U} when the cluster interaction is weak; this gives a more quantitative perspective on Theorem . Theorem 6.10 . Let 𝒵 = 𝒴 − ( λ ​ I + T ) ​ f ~ U \mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Y}-(\lambda I+T)\tilde{f}_{U} . Then there exists some random variable Z Z with { ( 𝒵 , g ) ℋ = 𝔼 [ Z g ⁡ ( U ​ X ) ¯ ] , ∀ g ∈ ℋ , 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Z | 2 ] ≤ 1 λ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ ( ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) | 2 ] ) . \begin{cases}(\mathcal{Z},g)_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathbb{E}[Z\overline{g(UX)}],\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{H},\\ \mathbb{E}[|Z|^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}](\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}|K(UX,UX^{\prime})|^{2}]).\end{cases} (47) Moreover, the deviation between f U f_{U} and f ~ U \tilde{f}_{U} satisfies λ ​ ‖ f U − f ~ U ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Z − ( f U − f ~ U ) ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Z | 2 ] . \lambda\left\lVert f_{U}-\tilde{f}_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[|Z-(f_{U}-\tilde{f}_{U})(UX)|^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[|Z|^{2}]. (48) Proof. We compute ( λ ​ I + T ) ​ f ~ U = ∑ j ( λ ​ I + ∑ i T i ) ​ f j = ∑ j 𝒴 j + ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m T i ​ f j = 𝒴 + 1 λ ​ ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m T i ​ ( 𝒴 j − T j ​ f j ) . Hence 𝒵 = − 1 λ ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m T i ( 𝒴 j − T j f j ) . \mathcal{Z}=-\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}T_{i}(\mathcal{Y}_{j}-T_{j}f_{j}). For any g ∈ ℋ g\in\mathcal{H} , using the definition of T i T_{i} and 𝒴 i \mathcal{Y}_{i} , we compute ( 𝒵 , g ) ℋ = − 1 λ ​ ( ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m T i ​ ( 𝒴 j − T j ​ f j ) , g ) ℋ = − 1 λ ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m 𝔼 [ χ i ( 𝒴 j − T j f j ) ( U X ) g ⁡ ( U ​ X ) ¯ ] = − 1 λ ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m 𝔼 [ χ i χ j ′ ( Y ′ − f j ( U X ′ ) ) K ⁡ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) ¯ g ⁡ ( U ​ X ) ¯ ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Z ​ g ⁡ ( U ​ X ) ¯ ] \begin{split}&(\mathcal{Z},g)_{\mathcal{H}}=-\frac{1}{\lambda}(\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}T_{i}(\mathcal{Y}_{j}-T_{j}f_{j}),g)_{\mathcal{H}}\\ =&-\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}(\mathcal{Y}_{j}-T_{j}f_{j})(UX)\overline{g(UX)}]\\ =&-\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}(Y^{\prime}-f_{j}(UX^{\prime}))\overline{K(UX,UX^{\prime})}\overline{g(UX)}]\\ =&\mathbb{E}[Z\overline{g(UX)}]\end{split} where the random variable Z Z is obtained by taking expectation over the ( X ′ , Y ′ ) (X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}) variables, but not the ( X , Y ) (X,Y) variables: Z = − 1 λ ​ 𝔼 X ′ , Y ′ ​ [ ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ ( Y ′ − f j ​ ( U ​ X ′ ) ) ​ K ⁡ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) ¯ ] . By Cauchy-Schwarz, 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Z | 2 ] ≤ 1 λ 2 ​ ( ∑ j 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ j ​ | Y − f j ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ​ ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) | 2 ] CLOSE ≤ 1 λ 2 ​ ( ∑ j 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ j ​ | Y | 2 ] ​ ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) | 2 ] CLOSE = 1 λ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) | 2 ] \begin{split}\mathbb{E}[|Z|^{2}]\leq&\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}(\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{j}|Y-f_{j}(UX)|^{2}]\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}|K(UX,UX^{\prime})|^{2}]\\ \leq&\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}(\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{j}|Y|^{2}]\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}|K(UX,UX^{\prime})|^{2}]\\ =&\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}|K(UX,UX^{\prime})|^{2}]\end{split} Here the second line uses the trivial upper bound Lemma . Now f U − f ~ U f_{U}-\tilde{f}_{U} satisfies the Euler-Lagrange equation ( λ ​ I + T ) ​ ( f U − f ~ U ) = 𝒵 . (\lambda I+T)(f_{U}-\tilde{f}_{U})=\mathcal{Z}. (49) This has the same form as the Euler-Lagrange equation ( ), with f U f_{U} replaced by f U − f ~ U f_{U}-\tilde{f}_{U} , and Y Y replaced by Z Z . Thus the trivial upper bound Lemma applied to Z Z instead of Y Y implies ( ). ∎ The point is that if the interaction between clusters satisfies 1 λ 2 ​ ( ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) | 2 ] ) < 1 , \frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}(\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}|K(UX,UX^{\prime})|^{2}])<1, then 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Z | 2 ] \mathbb{E}[|Z|^{2}] is smaller than 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ] \mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}] by a factor less than one. We can replace Y Y by Z Z , and f U f_{U} by f U − f ~ U f_{U}-\tilde{f}_{U} , and the resulting equation ( ) has the same form as ( ). Banach iteration of this process then yield the exact solution to ( ). Remark 16 . In applications, it may happen that not all the clusters are far separated. We have the flexibility to group together several cases of X 0 X_{0} to form bigger clusters, and apply the Theorem to study the interaction of these bigger clusters. Corollary 6.11 . The deviation between f U f_{U} and f ~ U \tilde{f}_{U} satisfies the bound λ ​ ‖ f U − f ~ U ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ | ( f U − f ~ U ) ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ≤ 4 λ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ ∑ 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ χ j ′ ​ | K ⁡ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) | 2 ] . \lambda\left\lVert f_{U}-\tilde{f}_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[|(f_{U}-\tilde{f}_{U})(UX)|^{2}]\leq\frac{4}{\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime}|K(UX,UX^{\prime})|^{2}].
