Distinct values of X 0 X_{0} signify what we would interpret as different conceptual subcategories , which is often not directly accessible to empirical observers. • Instead, only the clustering in the distribution of X X could in principle be observed empirically, and this is how empirical observers may infer the existence of X 0 X_{0} in the model. • It is intuitively clear that if an observer may gain access to X 0 X_{0} (prior knowledge of qualitative classification), it would lead to more efficient learning. The motivation of kernel learning is that the information of X 0 X_{0} is as good as the scale parameters and the essential degree of freedom in the learning problem, and this information is hopefully reflected in the distribution of the vacua. 7 Landscape of vacua: scale detectors 7.1 What is a significant vacuum? As discussed in Section , the main motivation for the kernel learning problem is to find the best parameters U U or Σ = U T ​ U \Sigma=U^{T}U , which detect the inherent scale parameters, and select out the essential degrees of freedom in X X , in order for the kernel ridge regression minimizer F Σ F_{\Sigma} to efficiently predict Y Y . This lead us to study the vacua of 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) , namely the local minimizers of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} in the partial compactification S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ¯ \overline{Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}} . However, not necessarily all the vacua are equally important for the task; for instance, if 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) is close to the trivial upper bound 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}] , then the minimizer has small norm 𝔼 ⁡ [ | F Σ | 2 ] ≪ 1 \mathbb{E}[|F_{\Sigma}|^{2}]\ll 1 , so Σ \Sigma does not carry meaningful information of Y Y . We wish to formulate a notion of significant vacuum, which intuitively excludes such ‘shallow local minima’. 7.1.1 Scale detector We will now formalize the idea that the most interesting vacua are those which identify the scale parameters in the learning problem; in general dimensions, scale parameters are specified by inner products rather than numbers. We begin with the interior of S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} . Definition 7.1 . Given Σ ∈ S ​ y ​ m + 2 \Sigma\in Sym^{2}_{+} , and a parameter Λ > 1 \Lambda>1 , the Λ \Lambda -neighbourhood of Σ \Sigma denotes the subset of inner products which are uniformly equivalent to Σ \Sigma with constant Λ \Lambda , 𝒩 ⁡ ( Σ , Λ ) := { Σ ′ ∈ S ​ y ​ m + 2 : Λ − 1 ​ Σ ≤ Σ ′ ≤ Λ ​ Σ } . \mathcal{N}(\Sigma,\Lambda):=\{\Sigma^{\prime}\in Sym^{2}_{+}:\Lambda^{-1}\Sigma\leq\Sigma^{\prime}\leq\Lambda\Sigma\}. Here ≤ \leq is meant in the sense of the partial order on the inner products. Informally, a scale detector Σ \Sigma is a vacuum that identifies the intrinsic scales of the data up to a multiplicative factor Λ \Lambda , with a quantitative energy gap ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ | Y | 2 \epsilon\mathbb{E}|Y|^{2} separating it from competing scale configurations. Definition 7.2 . Let ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 and Λ > 1 \Lambda>1 be given parameters. A vacuum Σ ∈ S ​ y ​ m + 2 \Sigma\in Sym^{2}_{+} is called an ( ϵ , Λ ) (\epsilon,\Lambda) - scale detector , if it has the local minimizing property 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ′ , λ ) ≥ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) , ∀ Σ ′ ∈ 𝒩 ⁡ ( Σ , Λ ) , \mathcal{J}(\Sigma^{\prime},\lambda)\geq\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\lambda),\quad\forall\Sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(\Sigma,\Lambda), and the strict gap property 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ′ , λ ) ≥ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) + ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ | Y | 2 , ∀ Σ ′ ∈ ∂ 𝒩 ⁡ ( Σ , Λ ) . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma^{\prime},\lambda)\geq\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\lambda)+\epsilon\mathbb{E}|Y|^{2},\quad\forall\Sigma^{\prime}\in\partial\mathcal{N}(\Sigma,\Lambda). Remark 17 . One should think that ϵ \epsilon is moderately small (say around 10 − 1 10^{-1} ), and Λ \Lambda is moderately large (say around 5 5 ). The motivation is that the eigenvalues of Σ \Sigma detects the correct scale parameters in the problem, and what really matters is not the precise value of Σ \Sigma , but its uniform equivalence neighbourhood. We point out the obvious fact that for scale detectors 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) ≤ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] , \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}], which means 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) is bounded away from the trivial upper bound 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}] , and therefore carries some information about Y Y . Remark 18 . As another motivation, the scale detector is stable under small perturbation , in the sense that due to the strict gap property, the 𝒥 \mathcal{J} -minimizers within 𝒩 ⁡ ( Σ , Λ ) \mathcal{N}(\Sigma,\Lambda) are forced to lie in the interior of 𝒩 ⁡ ( Σ , Λ ) \mathcal{N}(\Sigma,\Lambda) , even if 𝒥 \mathcal{J} is slightly perturbed. On the other hand, if the strict gap property fails, that would suggest the existence of some escape directions , along which 𝒥 \mathcal{J} is almost constant or could further decrease, so the vacuum Σ \Sigma would not be very stable. The following is an attempt to formulate the notion of scale detectors on the boundary of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} . Definition 7.3 . Given a positive semi-definite Σ \Sigma with null subspace W W , we denote W ′ W^{\prime} as the orthogonal complement of W W with respect to the ambient inner product | ⋅ | V 2 |\cdot|_{V}^{2} on V V . Given parameters Λ > 1 \Lambda>1 and a > 0 a>0 , the ( Λ , a ) (\Lambda,a) - neighbourhood of Σ \Sigma is the subset 𝒩 ⁡ ( Σ , Λ , a ) \mathcal{N}(\Sigma,\Lambda,a) , consisting of all Σ ′ ∈ S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 \Sigma^{\prime}\in Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} satisfying the following conditions: • The restriction of Σ ′ \Sigma^{\prime} to W W is bounded above by a | ⋅ | V 2 a|\cdot|_{V}^{2} . • The restriction of Σ ′ \Sigma^{\prime} to W ′ W^{\prime} satisfies the uniform equivalence condition Λ − 1 ​ Σ | W ′ ≤ Σ ′ | W ′ ≤ Λ ​ Σ | W ′ . \Lambda^{-1}\Sigma|_{W^{\prime}}\leq\Sigma^{\prime}|_{W^{\prime}}\leq\Lambda\Sigma|_{W^{\prime}}. Definition 7.4 . Let ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 and Λ > 1 , a > 0 \Lambda>1,a>0 be given parameters. A boundary vacuum Σ ∈ S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ∖ S ​ y ​ m + 2 \Sigma\in Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}\setminus Sym^{2}_{+} is called an ( ϵ , Λ , a ) (\epsilon,\Lambda,a) - scale detector , if it has the local minimizing property 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ′ , λ ) ≥ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) , ∀ Σ ′ ∈ 𝒩 ⁡ ( Σ , Λ , a ) , \mathcal{J}(\Sigma^{\prime},\lambda)\geq\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\lambda),\quad\forall\Sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(\Sigma,\Lambda,a), and the strict gap property 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ′ , λ ) ≥ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) + ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ | Y | 2 , ∀ Σ ′ ∈ ∂ 𝒩 ⁡ ( Σ , Λ , a ) . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma^{\prime},\lambda)\geq\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\lambda)+\epsilon\mathbb{E}|Y|^{2},\quad\forall\Sigma^{\prime}\in\partial\mathcal{N}(\Sigma,\Lambda,a). Given a boundary scale detector, we can regard W ′ ≃ V / W W^{\prime}\simeq V/W -component of X X as the primary variables, and the W W -component as the secondary variables. The problem of scale detection is mixed with the problem of variable selection. 7.2 Where can the scale detectors appear? We shall focus on the scale detectors in the interior of S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} , since the boundary vacua reduce to a lower dimensional problem, as studied in Section . We showcase some sample results to illustrate conceptual features. 7.2.1 A priori restrictions First, we show that interior ( ϵ , Λ ) (\epsilon,\Lambda) -scale detectors cannot be too close to the boundary of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} . Lemma 7.5 . Suppose K ⁡ ( x ) = 𝒦 ⁡ ( | x | V 2 ) K(x)=\mathcal{K}(|x|_{V}^{2}) where 𝒦 ′ \mathcal{K}^{\prime} is a bounded continuous function, and β := sup ω ∈ V ∗ : | ω | V = 1 𝔼 [ | ⟨ X , ω ⟩ | 4 ] 1 / 2 < + ∞ . \beta:=\sup_{\omega\in V^{*}:|\omega|_{V}=1}\mathbb{E}[|\langle X,\omega\rangle|^{4}]^{1/2}<+\infty. Then any ( ϵ , Λ ) (\epsilon,\Lambda) -scale detector Σ \Sigma must be bounded below by ϵ ​ λ 2 ​ β ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 | ⋅ | V 2 \frac{\epsilon\lambda}{2\beta\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}}|\cdot|_{V}^{2} . Remark 19 . Here the finiteness of β \beta is of course equivalent to 𝔼 ⁡ [ | X | 4 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|X|^{4}]<+\infty , but the constant β \beta is far more efficient if the dimension is high. Proof. For any Σ \Sigma , we let Σ ′ = Σ + t ​ ω ⊗ ω \Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma+t\omega\otimes\omega for some ω ∈ V ∗ \omega\in V^{*} with | ω | V = 1 |\omega|_{V}=1 , then | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X ′ − X | Σ 2 ) − 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X ′ − X | Σ ′ 2 ) | ≤ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 ​ | t | ​ | ⟨ ω , X − X ′ ⟩ | 2 . |\mathcal{K}(|X^{\prime}-X|_{\Sigma}^{2})-\mathcal{K}(|X^{\prime}-X|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{2})|\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}|t||\langle\omega,X-X^{\prime}\rangle|^{2}. By the quantitative continuity estimate of Proposition , | 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) − 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ′ , λ ) | ≤ 1 2 ​ λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X ′ − X | Σ 2 ) − 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X ′ − X | Σ ′ 2 ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 . ≤ 1 2 ​ λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ | t | ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | ⟨ X ′ − X , ω ⟩ | 4 ] 1 / 2 \begin{split}|\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)-\mathcal{J}(\Sigma^{\prime};\lambda)|\leq&\frac{1}{2\lambda}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{K}(|X^{\prime}-X|_{\Sigma}^{2})-\mathcal{K}(|X^{\prime}-X|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{2})|^{2}]^{1/2}.\\ \leq&\frac{1}{2\lambda}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]|t|\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}\mathbb{E}[|\langle X^{\prime}-X,\omega\rangle|^{4}]^{1/2}\end{split} Using the Minkowski inequality 𝔼 [ | ⟨ X ′ − X , ω ⟩ | 4 ] 1 / 4 ≤ 𝔼 [ ⟨ X ′ , ω ⟩ | 4 ] 1 / 4 + 𝔼 [ ⟨ X , ω ⟩ | 4 ] 1 / 4 ≤ 2 β 1 / 2 . \mathbb{E}[|\langle X^{\prime}-X,\omega\rangle|^{4}]^{1/4}\leq\mathbb{E}[\langle X^{\prime},\omega\rangle|^{4}]^{1/4}+\mathbb{E}[\langle X,\omega\rangle|^{4}]^{1/4}\leq 2\beta^{1/2}. Consequently, | 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) − 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ + t ​ ω ⊗ ω , λ ) | ≤ 2 ​ β λ ​ | t | ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] . |\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)-\mathcal{J}(\Sigma+t\omega\otimes\omega,\lambda)|\leq\frac{2\beta}{\lambda}|t|\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]. (52) We now suppose Σ \Sigma is an ( ϵ , Λ ) (\epsilon,\Lambda) -scale detector in S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} . We find an eigenbasis { e i } \{e_{i}\} of Σ \Sigma with respect to the ambient inner product | ⋅ | V 2 |\cdot|_{V}^{2} , with eigenvalues 0 < λ 1 ≤ … ≤ λ d 0<\lambda_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{d} . By the strict ϵ \epsilon -gap property, if we choose Σ ′ = Σ + t ​ ω ⊗ ω = Λ − 1 ​ λ 1 ​ e 1 ∗ ⊗ e 1 ∗ + ∑ 2 d λ i ​ e i ∗ ⊗ e i ∗ , t = − ( 1 − Λ − 1 ) ​ λ 1 , ω = e 1 ∗ , \Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma+t\omega\otimes\omega=\Lambda^{-1}\lambda_{1}e_{1}^{*}\otimes e_{1}^{*}+\sum_{2}^{d}\lambda_{i}e_{i}^{*}\otimes e_{i}^{*},\quad t=-(1-\Lambda^{-1})\lambda_{1},\quad\omega=e_{1}^{*}, then 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ′ , λ ) − 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) ≥ ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma^{\prime};\lambda)-\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)\geq\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]. Thus 2 ​ β λ ​ | t | ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 ≥ ϵ \frac{2\beta}{\lambda}|t|\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}\geq\epsilon , whence λ 1 ≥ | t | ≥ ϵ ​ λ 2 ​ β ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 . \lambda_{1}\geq|t|\geq\frac{\epsilon\lambda}{2\beta\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}}. Since λ 1 \lambda_{1} is the smallest eigenvalue, this implies Σ ≥ ϵ ​ λ 2 ​ β ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 | ⋅ | V 2 \Sigma\geq\frac{\epsilon\lambda}{2\beta\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}}|\cdot|_{V}^{2} . ∎ Remark 20 . This argument of course does not rule out scale detectors on the boundary. For instance, in dimension one, to test scale detection at an interior point Σ ∈ S ​ y ​ m + 2 ​ ( ℝ ) = ℝ + \Sigma\in Sym^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}_{+} , we only need to consider the value of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} on the dyadic annulus ( Λ − 1 ​ Σ , Λ ​ Σ ) (\Lambda^{-1}\Sigma,\Lambda\Sigma) , while at the boundary point Σ = 0 \Sigma=0 , we would need the value of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} on some ball in S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ​ ( ℝ ) Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}(\mathbb{R}) around the origin. Next we prove that under quantitative bounds on the probability density of X X , then the scale detectors in S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} (including the boundary scale detectors) remain in a bounded region. Lemma 7.6 . Suppose K ⁡ ( x ) = 𝒦 ⁡ ( | x | V 2 ) K(x)=\mathcal{K}(|x|_{V}^{2}) is a completely monotone kernel ( ), such that C 𝒦 = ∬ ( π ​ ( t + s ) − 1 ) 1 / 2 ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 ν ​ ( s ) < + ∞ . C_{\mathcal{K}}=\iint(\pi(t+s)^{-1})^{1/2}d\nu(t)d\nu(s)<+\infty. Suppose also that along any unit direction ξ ∈ V \xi\in V , the marginal distribution of X X projected to ℝ ​ ξ \mathbb{R}\xi has probability density L ∞ L^{\infty} -bound less than p 0 p_{0} . Then any ( ϵ , Λ ) (\epsilon,\Lambda) -scale detector Σ \Sigma in S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} is bounded above by Σ ≤ ( C 𝒦 p 0 ) 2 ( λ ϵ ) − 4 | ⋅ | V 2 . \Sigma\leq(C_{\mathcal{K}}p_{0})^{2}(\lambda\epsilon)^{-4}|\cdot|_{V}^{2}. Proof. By Corollary , 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) ≥ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − λ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ 𝒦 ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ] ≥ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − λ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ 𝒦 2 ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ] 1 / 2 . \begin{split}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)\geq&\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\lambda^{-1}\mathbb{E}[Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})]\\ \geq&\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\lambda^{-1}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}^{2}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})]^{1/2}.\end{split} We take an eigenbasis { e i } \{e_{i}\} of Σ \Sigma with respect to | ⋅ | V 2 |\cdot|_{V}^{2} , with eigenvalues 0 ≤ λ 1 ≤ … ≤ λ d 0\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{d} . By Lemma , choosing ξ = e d \xi=e_{d} the highest eigenvector, W ′ = ℝ ​ ξ W^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}\xi , and W W its orthogonal complement, we deduce 𝔼 [ 𝒦 2 ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ] ≤ p 0 λ d ∬ ( π ( t + s ) − 1 ) 1 / 2 d ν ( t ) d ν ( s ) . \mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}^{2}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2}]\leq\frac{p_{0}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{d}}}\iint(\pi(t+s)^{-1})^{1/2}d\nu(t)d\nu(s). Thus 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) ≥ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − λ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ ( C 𝒦 ​ p 0 λ d ) 1 / 2 . \begin{split}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)&\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\lambda^{-1}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}](\frac{C_{\mathcal{K}}p_{0}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{d}}})^{1/2}.\end{split} (53) If Σ \Sigma is a scale detector, then 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) ≤ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}] , so λ − 1 ​ ( C 𝒦 ​ p 0 λ d ) 1 / 2 ≥ ϵ , \lambda^{-1}(\frac{C_{\mathcal{K}}p_{0}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{d}}})^{1/2}\geq\epsilon, whence λ d ≤ ( C 𝒦 ​ p 0 ) 2 ​ ( λ ​ ϵ ) − 4 \lambda_{d}\leq(C_{\mathcal{K}}p_{0})^{2}(\lambda\epsilon)^{-4} , which implies the upper bound on Σ \Sigma . ∎ Remark 21 . (Improved estimates) In the above proof, we have only used the dim W ′ = 1 \dim W^{\prime}=1 case of Proposition . If Σ V / W \Sigma_{V/W} is large in dim W ′ > 1 \dim{W^{\prime}}>1 directions, then the suppression factor det ( Σ V / W ) − 1 / 2 \det(\Sigma_{V/W})^{-1/2} from Proposition gives much stronger estimates than λ d − 1 / 2 \lambda_{d}^{-1/2} . Remark 22 . The Lemmas are applicable to the Gaussian kernels, or the Sobolev kernels with γ > 1 \gamma>1 (See Example , ). The upshot is that if the conditions of both Lemmas are satisfied, then any interior ( ϵ , Λ ) (\epsilon,\Lambda) -scale detector must be uniformly equivalent to | ⋅ | V 2 |\cdot|_{V}^{2} with estimable constants. The intuitive explanation is that the condition 𝔼 ⁡ [ | X | 4 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|X|^{4}]<+\infty rules out divergence of X X to infinity, while the quantitative L ∞ L^{\infty} -bound on the probability density rules out concentration of X X , so the random variable X X has only one inherent scale parameter, equivalent to the inverse covariance matrix of X X up to controlled uniform constants. On the other hand, if we drop the L ∞ L^{\infty} -bound on the probability density, then X X can have several far separated scale parameters, and scale detectors can be far from | ⋅ | V 2 |\cdot|_{V}^{2} . 7.2.2 Global minimum and scale detector We now find a sufficient condition to find an interior scale detector. We shall need the following: • Essential Dependence Assumption: inf S ​ y ​ m + 2 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) ≤ inf S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ∖ S ​ y ​ m + 2 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) − 2 ​ ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] . \inf_{Sym^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)\leq\inf_{Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}\setminus Sym^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)-2\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]. The intuitive meaning is that Y Y should depend on all the degrees of freedom in X X , and missing any degree of freedom would result in worse predictive power, as we now explain. We suppose Y Y is a function of X X , so Var ​ ( Y | X ) = 0 \text{Var}(Y|X)=0 , but for any decomposition V = W ⊕ W ′ V=W\oplus W^{\prime} , Y Y cannot be expressed as a function of X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} alone, with failure measured by 𝔼 ⁡ [ Var ​ ( Y | X W ′ ) ] > 4 ​ ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] . \mathbb{E}[\text{Var}(Y|X_{W^{\prime}})]>4\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]. Suppose Σ \Sigma is positive semi-definite with null subspace W W , and X W ′ X_{W^{\prime}} is the marginal of X X on V / W V/W . Then by the trivial lower bound in Lemma , 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) ≥ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ Var ​ ( Y | X W ′ ) ] > 2 ​ ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\text{Var}(Y|X_{W^{\prime}})]>2\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]. When λ \lambda is small enough, Y Y should be well approximated by some function in ℋ \mathcal{H} , so inf Σ ∈ S ​ y ​ m + 2 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \inf_{\Sigma\in Sym^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) is close to zero, and the essential dependence assumption is satisfied. Theorem 7.7 . We suppose the essential dependence assumption, and the hypotheses of Lemma , . Let Λ = max ⁡ ( β ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 2 ​ ϵ ​ λ , ( C 𝒦 ​ p 0 ) 2 ​ ( λ ​ ϵ ) − 4 ) . \Lambda=\max(\frac{\beta\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}}{2\epsilon\lambda},(C_{\mathcal{K}}p_{0})^{2}(\lambda\epsilon)^{-4}). Then any global minimizing vacuum Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} lies within the neighbourhood 𝒩 ( | ⋅ | V 2 , Λ ) \mathcal{N}(|\cdot|_{V}^{2},\Lambda) , and such Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} is an ( ϵ , Λ 2 ) (\epsilon,\Lambda^{2}) -scale detector. Proof. Since X X is a continuous variable, we know lim Σ → ∞ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] \lim_{\Sigma\to\infty}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}] , so the global minimum is achieved in S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} . By the essential dependence assumption, any global minimizer Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} must be inside the interior S ​ y ​ m + 2 Sym^{2}_{+} . The main idea is to prove that 𝒥 \mathcal{J} has a quantitative gap from its minimum, for any Σ \Sigma close to the boundary or close to infinity. For any Σ ∈ S ​ y ​ m + 2 \Sigma\in Sym^{2}_{+} , we find an eigenbasis { e i } \{e_{i}\} of Σ \Sigma with respect to the ambient inner product | ⋅ | V 2 |\cdot|_{V}^{2} , with eigenvalues 0 < λ 1 ≤ … ≤ λ d 0<\lambda_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{d} . Choosing t = − λ 1 , ω = e 1 ∗ , Σ ′ = ∑ 2 d λ i ​ e i ∗ ⊗ e i ∗ , t=-\lambda_{1},\quad\omega=e_{1}^{*},\quad\Sigma^{\prime}=\sum_{2}^{d}\lambda_{i}e_{i}^{*}\otimes e_{i}^{*}, then upon applying ( ), and the essential dependence assumption, 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ) ≥ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ′ ) − λ 1 ​ 2 ​ β λ ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ≥ inf S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 ∖ S ​ y ​ m + 2 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) − λ 1 ​ 2 ​ β λ ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ≥ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ 0 ) + 2 ​ ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − λ 1 ​ 2 ​ β λ ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] , \begin{split}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)&\geq\mathcal{J}(\Sigma^{\prime})-\lambda_{1}\frac{2\beta}{\lambda}\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\\ &\geq\inf_{Sym^{2}_{\geq 0}\setminus Sym^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)-\lambda_{1}\frac{2\beta}{\lambda}\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\\ &\geq\mathcal{J}(\Sigma_{0})+2\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\lambda_{1}\frac{2\beta}{\lambda}\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}],\end{split} Thus as long as λ 1 ≤ Λ − 1 ≤ λ ​ ϵ 2 ​ β ​ ‖ 𝒦 ′ ‖ C 0 \lambda_{1}\leq\Lambda^{-1}\leq\frac{\lambda\epsilon}{2\beta\left\lVert\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0}}} , we have 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ ) ≥ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ 0 ) + ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] \mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\geq\mathcal{J}(\Sigma_{0})+\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}] . On the other hand, by ( ) and the essential dependence assumption, as long as λ d ≥ Λ ≥ ( C 𝒦 ​ p 0 ) 2 ​ ( λ ​ ϵ ) − 4 \lambda_{d}\geq\Lambda\geq(C_{\mathcal{K}}p_{0})^{2}(\lambda\epsilon)^{-4} , we also have 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) ≥ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ≥ 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ 0 ) + ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]\geq\mathcal{J}(\Sigma_{0})+\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]. Consequently, at Σ = Σ 0 \Sigma=\Sigma_{0} , we have λ 1 ≥ Λ − 1 \lambda_{1}\geq\Lambda^{-1} and λ d ≤ Λ \lambda_{d}\leq\Lambda , so Σ ∈ 𝒩 ( | ⋅ | V 2 , Λ ) \Sigma\in\mathcal{N}(|\cdot|_{V}^{2},\Lambda) . Moreover, any Σ ∈ ∂ 𝒩 ⁡ ( Σ 0 , Λ 2 ) \Sigma\in\partial\mathcal{N}(\Sigma_{0},\Lambda^{2}) lies outside of 𝒩 ( | ⋅ | V 2 , Λ ) \mathcal{N}(|\cdot|_{V}^{2},\Lambda) , so either λ 1 ≤ Λ − 1 \lambda_{1}\leq\Lambda^{-1} or λ d ≥ Λ \lambda_{d}\geq\Lambda , hence the strict gap property for the ( ϵ , Λ 2 ) (\epsilon,\Lambda^{2}) -scale detector Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} follows. ∎ Remark 23 . We do not claim that the global minimizing vacuum is unique, nor that the interior scale detectors are unique. On the other hand, in the context of the above theorem all these are uniformly equivalent to each other with controllable constants, so from the viewpoint of the scale detection philosophy, their roles are more or less interchangeable. Remark 24 . The localization estimates for scale detectors in Lemmas – and Theorem deteriorate as λ → 0 + \lambda\to 0^{+} . They do not preclude whether the scale detectors remain bounded, approach the boundary, or escape to infinity as λ → 0 + \lambda\to 0^{+} . Understanding the vacua’s limiting behavior as λ → 0 + \lambda\to 0^{+} would require a separate analysis and lies beyond the scope of the paper. 7.3 Multiple vacua in dimension one A significant phenomenon is that when Y Y is the superposition of several functions of X X , with very different sets of inherent scale parameters, one can sometimes observe multiple vacua , each sensitive to one set of scale parameters. As a corollary, the vacua that are not necessarily the global minimizer of 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) , can carry important information which the global minimizer does not detect. Our one-dimensional examples should be viewed as a proof of concept, which illustrates how the vacua detects the scale parameters. One can hope to explore the higher dimensional examples using the tools developed in this paper, such as the dimensional reduction near the boundary of S ​ y ​ m ≥ 0 2 Sym^{2}_{\geq 0} , and the asymptotic behavior for large Σ \Sigma , but the full picture remains an interesting question. 7.3.1 Setup We work in a special case of Section ; a typical example to keep in mind is Example . Let X 0 X_{0} be a discrete random variable valued in { 1 , 2 , … ​ m } \{1,2,\ldots m\} , which is not directly accessible to the observer, and let p i = ℙ ⁡ ( X 0 = i ) > 0 p_{i}=\mathbb{P}(X_{0}=i)>0 . Let Z 1 , … ​ Z m Z_{1},\ldots Z_{m} be continuous random variables on ℝ \mathbb{R} , such that 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Z i | 2 ] \mathbb{E}[|Z_{i}|^{2}] is finite, 𝔼 ⁡ [ Z i ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[Z_{i}]=0 , and all the Z i Z_{i} and X 0 X_{0} are independent. Let σ 1 , … ​ σ m ∈ ℝ \sigma_{1},\ldots\sigma_{m}\in\mathbb{R} , and a 1 , … ​ a m ∈ ℝ a_{1},\ldots a_{m}\in\mathbb{R} , such that X = ∑ 1 m χ i ​ ( a i + σ i ​ Z i ) , χ i = 𝟙 X 0 = i . X=\sum_{1}^{m}\chi_{i}(a_{i}+\sigma_{i}Z_{i}),\quad\chi_{i}=\mathbbm{1}_{X_{0}=i}. Thus conditional on X 0 = i X_{0}=i , then X X has the same law as a i + σ i ​ Z i a_{i}+\sigma_{i}Z_{i} . One should view σ i \sigma_{i} as specifying the scale parameters of the clusters, the a i a_{i} as specifying the cluster centres, while Z i Z_{i} gives the rescaled version of the conditional distribution of X X (e.g. Z i Z_{i} could be a standard Gaussian). Let Y = ∑ 1 m g i ​ ( X − a i σ i ) , Y=\sum_{1}^{m}g_{i}(\frac{X-a_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}), such that 𝔼 ⁡ [ | Y | 2 ] < + ∞ \mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]<+\infty , and 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ | Y | 2 ] > 0 \mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|Y|^{2}]>0 . Here g i g_{i} are fixed functions independent of the choice of a i , σ i a_{i},\sigma_{i} , such that Y Y is a nonzero function of X X conditional on X 0 = i X_{0}=i . We want to study the multi-scale phenomenon in these examples, so we view p i , Z i , g i p_{i},Z_{i},g_{i} as fixed choices, while σ i \sigma_{i} are scale parameters that we shall vary. We shall focus on the case 0 < σ 1 ≪ σ 2 ≪ … ​ σ m , min i ≠ j ⁡ | a i − a j | ≳ σ m . 0<\sigma_{1}\ll\sigma_{2}\ll\ldots\\ \sigma_{m},\quad\min_{i\neq j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|\gtrsim\sigma_{m}. Qualitatively, we wish to understand the vacua of 𝒥 \mathcal{J} as σ i / σ i + 1 → 0 \sigma_{i}/\sigma_{i+1}\to 0 . In the following arguments, the constants are allowed to depend on p i , Z i , g i p_{i},Z_{i},g_{i} , and we will use o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) to denote quantities that converges to zero in the limit σ i / σ i + 1 → 0 \sigma_{i}/\sigma_{i+1}\to 0 . The explicit dependence on σ i / σ i + 1 \sigma_{i}/\sigma_{i+1} is computable in principle, but we will mostly suppress this for simplicity. We shall make the following additional assumption: • (Average value in each cluster) 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y | X 0 = i ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[Y|X_{0}=i]=0 for each value of X 0 X_{0} . Remark 25 . Here the conditional expectation of Y Y can be viewed as the zero dimensional reduction of the subproblem of learning Y Y conditional on X 0 = i X_{0}=i . If the average zero condition is not satisfied on the nose, then one should first try to learn these average numbers and then subtract them off, as we remarked in Section , item 4. As usual, we write the kernel function as K ⁡ ( x ) = 𝒦 ⁡ ( | x | 2 ) K(x)=\mathcal{K}(|x|^{2}) . 7.3.2 Multiple vacua Under the above setting, we shall prove the existence of many vacua which reflect the scale parameters in the distribution of X X . Theorem 7.8 . (Multiple vacua existence) There are a small constant ϵ \epsilon and a large constant Λ \Lambda , such that if σ i / σ i + 1 \sigma_{i}/\sigma_{i+1} is sufficiently small for all i i , and min i ≠ j ⁡ | a i − a j | ≥ C − 1 ​ σ m \min_{i\neq j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|\geq C^{-1}\sigma_{m} , then the following statements hold: 1. Any ( ϵ , Λ ) (\epsilon,\Lambda) -scale detector Σ \Sigma satisfies Λ − 1 ​ σ i − 2 < Σ = U 2 < Λ ​ σ i − 2 \Lambda^{-1}\sigma_{i}^{-2}<\Sigma=U^{2}<\Lambda\sigma_{i}^{-2} for some i ∈ { 1 , … ​ m } i\in\{1,\ldots m\} . 2. For each i = 1 , … ​ m i=1,\ldots m , there is an ( ϵ , Λ 2 ) (\epsilon,\Lambda^{2}) -scale detector Σ \Sigma with Λ − 1 ​ σ i − 2 < Σ = U 2 < Λ ​ σ i − 2 . \Lambda^{-1}\sigma_{i}^{-2}<\Sigma=U^{2}<\Lambda\sigma_{i}^{-2}. Proof. We shall focus on the cases σ i + 1 − 1 ​ σ i − 1 ≤ Σ ≤ σ i − 1 ​ σ i − 1 − 1 \sigma_{i+1}^{-1}\sigma_{i}^{-1}\leq\Sigma\leq\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{i-1}^{-1} for some 2 ≤ i ≤ m − 1 2\leq i\leq m-1 ; the other cases Σ ≤ σ m − 1 ​ σ m − 1 − 1 \Sigma\leq\sigma_{m}^{-1}\sigma_{m-1}^{-1} and Σ ≥ σ 1 − 1 ​ σ 2 − 1 \Sigma\geq\sigma_{1}^{-1}\sigma_{2}^{-1} can be treated similarly. The main difficulty is that we are dealing with a multi-parameter family of random variables ( X , Y ) (X,Y) depending on σ 1 , … ​ σ m \sigma_{1},\ldots\sigma_{m} . Our strategy is to simplify the problem by a number of reductions, to arrive at a problem with no extra scale parameters. • Step 1: Replacing the small scale clusters X 0 < i X_{0}<i by atoms. First, conditional on X 0 = j X_{0}=j for j ≤ i − 1 j\leq i-1 , we replace χ j ​ Y \chi_{j}Y with its conditional mean (which by assumption is zero). The deviation between the minimizer of the original loss function I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) versus the new loss function, is the quantity f U 1 f_{U}^{1} in Lemma , which is bounded by λ ‖ f U 1 ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 [ | f U 1 ( U X ) | 2 ] ≤ 1 λ 𝔼 [ | Y 1 | 2 ] × 𝔼 ​ [ | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) − 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X ~ − X ′ | Σ 2 ) − 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ ~ | Σ 2 ) + 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X ~ − X ′ ~ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ] 1 / 2 , \begin{split}&\lambda\left\lVert f_{U}^{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[|f_{U}^{1}(UX)|^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}[|Y^{1}|^{2}]\times\\ &\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})-\mathcal{K}(|\tilde{X}-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})-\mathcal{K}(|X-\tilde{X^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma}^{2})+\mathcal{K}(|\tilde{X}-\tilde{X^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma}^{2})|^{2}]^{1/2},\end{split} where X ~ = X \tilde{X}=X conditional on X 0 ∈ { i , … ​ m } X_{0}\in\{i,\ldots m\} , and X ~ = 𝔼 ⁡ [ X | X 0 = j ] = a j \tilde{X}=\mathbb{E}[X|X_{0}=j]=a_{j} conditional on X 0 = j X_{0}=j for j < i j<i . We claim that as σ i − 1 ​ U → 0 \sigma_{i-1}U\to 0 , the quantity 𝔼 ⁡ [ | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) − 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X ~ − X ′ | Σ 2 ) − 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ ~ | Σ 2 ) + 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X ~ − X ′ ~ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ] \mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})-\mathcal{K}(|\tilde{X}-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})-\mathcal{K}(|X-\tilde{X^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma}^{2})+\mathcal{K}(|\tilde{X}-\tilde{X^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma}^{2})|^{2}] converges to zero. To see the claim, recall that 𝒦 ⁡ ( r ) → 0 \mathcal{K}(r)\to 0 as r → + ∞ r\to+\infty by Riemann-Lebesgue, so we only need to consider the case where the distances | X − X ′ | Σ |X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma} etc are all bounded. Now conditional on X 0 = j X_{0}=j for some j < i j<i , we have | X − X ~ | Σ = σ j ​ U ​ | Z j − 𝔼 ⁡ [ Z j ] | ≤ σ i − 1 ​ U ​ | Z j − 𝔼 ⁡ [ Z j ] | , |X-\tilde{X}|_{\Sigma}=\sigma_{j}U|Z_{j}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{j}]|\leq\sigma_{i-1}U|Z_{j}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{j}]|, so | X − X ′ | Σ 2 − | ​ X ~ − X ′ | Σ 2 |X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2}-|\tilde{X}-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2} is small when σ i − 1 ​ U ≪ 1 \sigma_{i-1}U\ll 1 . The claim then follows from the uniform modulus of continuity on the kernel function. The upshot is that up to o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) error in the σ k / σ k + 1 → 0 \sigma_{k}/\sigma_{k+1}\to 0 limit, we can compute 𝒥 \mathcal{J} by the simpler problem where χ j ​ Y \chi_{j}Y is replaced by its conditional expectation value zero, for all j < i j<i . Likewise, by the arguments of Section , for the purpose of finding 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda) and the minimizing function f U f_{U} of I ⁡ ( f , U , λ ) I(f,U,\lambda) up to o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) error, we may replace the conditional distribution of χ j ​ X \chi_{j}X by the atomic measure at the average value of χ j ​ X \chi_{j}X , which is a j a_{j} . • Step 2: Decouple the bigger scale clusters. In Section we analyzed the deviation between the minimizing function f U f_{U} versus the sum f ~ U = ∑ f k \tilde{f}_{U}=\sum f_{k} of the minimizing functions f k f_{k} of the decoupled problems associated to individual clusters. By Corollary , the deviation error is bounded by λ ​ ‖ f U − f ~ U ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ | ( f U − f ~ U ) ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ≤ 1 λ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ ( ∑ 1 ≤ l ≠ k ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ l ​ χ k ′ ​ | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ] ) . \lambda\left\lVert f_{U}-\tilde{f}_{U}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[|(f_{U}-\tilde{f}_{U})(UX)|^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}](\sum_{1\leq l\neq k\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{l}\chi_{k}^{\prime}|\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})|^{2}]). (54) By Theorem , { 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) − ∑ 1 m J j ≤ 1 λ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ ( ∑ 1 ≤ l ≠ k ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ l ​ χ k ′ ​ | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ] ) . 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) − ∑ 1 m J i ≥ − 2 λ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] ​ ( ∑ 1 ≤ l ≠ k ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ l ​ χ k ′ ​ | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ] ) . \begin{cases}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)-\sum_{1}^{m}J_{j}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}](\sum_{1\leq l\neq k\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{l}\chi_{k}^{\prime}|\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})|^{2}]).\\ \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)-\sum_{1}^{m}J_{i}\geq-\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}](\sum_{1\leq l\neq k\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{l}\chi_{k}^{\prime}|\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})|^{2}]).\end{cases} (55) Here J i J_{i} stands for the minimum value of the decoupled problems. We now analyze the key quantity ∑ 1 ≤ l ≠ k ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ l ′ ​ χ k ​ | K ⁡ ( U ​ X , U ​ X ′ ) | 2 ] \sum_{1\leq l\neq k\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{l}^{\prime}\chi_{k}|K(UX,UX^{\prime})|^{2}] . Conditional on X 0 = l X_{0}=l and X 0 ′ = k X_{0}^{\prime}=k , then X X has average a l a_{l} with variance σ l 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Z l | 2 ] \sigma_{l}^{2}\mathbb{E}[|Z_{l}|^{2}] , while X ′ X^{\prime} has average a k a_{k} with variance σ k 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Z k | 2 ] \sigma_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}[|Z_{k}|^{2}] . By assumption | a l − a k | ≳ σ m ≫ σ m − 1 ≫ … ≫ σ 1 . |a_{l}-a_{k}|\gtrsim\sigma_{m}\gg\sigma_{m-1}\gg\ldots\gg\sigma_{1}. Let R ≫ 1 R\gg 1 be any given parameter. In the σ k / σ k + 1 → 0 \sigma_{k}/\sigma_{k+1}\to 0 limit, using the assumption that Σ ≥ ( σ i ​ σ i + 1 ) − 1 \Sigma\geq(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1})^{-1} , outside the exceptional event | X − X ′ | < R − 1 ​ min ⁡ { | a l − a k | , σ m } , |X-X^{\prime}|<R^{-1}\min\{|a_{l}-a_{k}|,\sigma_{m}\}, the separation distance | X − X ′ | Σ 2 |X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2} is bounded below by | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ≥ R − 2 ​ min ⁡ { | a l − a k | , σ m } 2 ​ ( σ i ​ σ i + 1 ) − 1 ≥ C − 1 ​ R − 2 ​ σ m 2 ​ ( σ i ​ σ i + 1 ) − 1 |X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2}\geq R^{-2}\min\{|a_{l}-a_{k}|,\sigma_{m}\}^{2}(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1})^{-1}\geq C^{-1}R^{-2}\sigma_{m}^{2}(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1})^{-1} which tends to infinity for any fixed R R . We now show that the exceptional event has arbitrarily small probability in the limit when R R is sufficiently large. When l , k ≤ m − 1 l,k\leq m-1 , ℙ ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | < R − 1 ​ | a l − a k | ∣ X 0 = l , X 0 ′ = k ) = ℙ ⁡ ( | a l + σ l ​ Z l − a k − σ k ​ Z k | < R − 1 ​ | a l − a k | ) ≤ ℙ ⁡ ( | σ l ​ Z l | ≥ 1 4 ​ | a l − a k | ) + ℙ ⁡ ( | σ k ​ Z k | ≥ 1 4 ​ | a l − a k | ) ≤ 4 ​ σ l | a l − a k | ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Z l | 2 ] 1 / 2 + 4 ​ σ k | a l − a k | ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Z k | 2 ] 1 / 2 ≤ C ⁡ ( σ l + σ k ) ​ σ m − 1 → 0 , \begin{split}&\mathbb{P}(|X-X^{\prime}|<R^{-1}|a_{l}-a_{k}|\mid X_{0}=l,X_{0}^{\prime}=k)\\ =&\mathbb{P}(|a_{l}+\sigma_{l}Z_{l}-a_{k}-\sigma_{k}Z_{k}|<R^{-1}|a_{l}-a_{k}|)\\ \leq&\mathbb{P}(|\sigma_{l}Z_{l}|\geq\frac{1}{4}|a_{l}-a_{k}|)+\mathbb{P}(|\sigma_{k}Z_{k}|\geq\frac{1}{4}|a_{l}-a_{k}|)\\ \leq&\frac{4\sigma_{l}}{|a_{l}-a_{k}|}\mathbb{E}[|Z_{l}|^{2}]^{1/2}+\frac{4\sigma_{k}}{|a_{l}-a_{k}|}\mathbb{E}[|Z_{k}|^{2}]^{1/2}\\ \leq&C(\sigma_{l}+\sigma_{k})\sigma_{m}^{-1}\to 0,\end{split} where we used | a l − a k | ≳ σ m |a_{l}-a_{k}|\gtrsim\sigma_{m} and ( σ k + σ l ) ​ σ m − 1 = o ⁡ ( 1 ) (\sigma_{k}+\sigma_{l})\sigma_{m}^{-1}=o(1) , while when l l or k k is m m , the probability ℙ ⁡ ( | a l + σ l ​ Z l − a k − σ k ​ Z k | < R − 1 ​ σ m ) \mathbb{P}(|a_{l}+\sigma_{l}Z_{l}-a_{k}-\sigma_{k}Z_{k}|<R^{-1}\sigma_{m}) can be made arbitrarily small by taking R R sufficiently large, thanks to the assumption that Z m Z_{m} is a continuous random variable and hence sup c ∈ ℝ ℙ ⁡ ( | Z m − c | < R − 1 ) → 0 \sup_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}(|Z_{m}-c|<R^{-1})\to 0 as R → ∞ R\to\infty . Since the kernel function 𝒦 ⁡ ( r ) \mathcal{K}(r) tends to zero as r → ∞ r\to\infty , this shows that in the σ k / σ k + 1 → 0 \sigma_{k}/\sigma_{k+1}\to 0 limit, ∑ 1 ≤ l ≠ k ≤ m 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ l ​ χ k ′ ​ | 𝒦 ⁡ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) | 2 ] → 0 . \sum_{1\leq l\neq k\leq m}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{l}\chi_{k}^{\prime}|\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})|^{2}]\to 0. The conclusion is that up to o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) error, we may replace the minimizing function by the sum of the minimizing functions for the decoupled problems. Moreover, for each cases X 0 = i + 1 , … ​ X 0 = m X_{0}=i+1,\ldots X_{0}=m , the norms of the minimizing functions for the decoupled problems can be bounded by Corollary , { λ ​ ‖ f k ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ | f k ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] ≤ 1 λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ k ​ χ k ′ ​ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ] , 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) ≥ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ k ​ | Y | 2 ] − ( 2 ​ λ ) − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ k ​ χ k ′ ​ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ] . \begin{cases}\lambda\left\lVert f_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[|f_{k}(UX)|^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k}\chi_{k}^{\prime}Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}K(U(X-X^{\prime}))],\\ \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k}|Y|^{2}]-(2\lambda)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k}\chi_{k}^{\prime}Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}K(U(X-X^{\prime}))].\end{cases} By Cauchy-Schwarz, and the fact that conditional on X 0 = k X_{0}=k then X = a k + σ k ​ Z k X=a_{k}+\sigma_{k}Z_{k} , 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ k ​ χ k ′ ​ Y ​ Y ¯ ′ ​ K ​ ( U ⁡ ( X − X ′ ) ) ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ k ​ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ k ​ χ k ′ ​ 𝒦 ​ ( | X − X ′ | Σ 2 ) ] 1 / 2 = 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ k ​ | Y | 2 ] ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ k ​ χ k ′ ​ 𝒦 ​ ( σ k 2 ​ Σ ​ | Z k − Z k ′ | 2 ) ] 1 / 2 \begin{split}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k}\chi_{k}^{\prime}Y\bar{Y}^{\prime}K(U(X-X^{\prime}))]\leq&\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k}|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k}\chi_{k}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}(|X-X^{\prime}|_{\Sigma}^{2})]^{1/2}\\ =&\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k}|Y|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k}\chi_{k}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}(\sigma_{k}^{2}\Sigma|Z_{k}-Z_{k}^{\prime}|^{2})]^{1/2}\end{split} Since for k > i k>i we have σ k 2 ​ Σ ≥ σ k 2 ​ ( σ i ​ σ i + 1 ) − 1 → + ∞ \sigma_{k}^{2}\Sigma\geq\sigma_{k}^{2}(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1})^{-1}\to+\infty in the limit, and since Z k Z_{k} is a continuous variable, 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ k ​ χ k ′ ​ 𝒦 ​ ( σ k 2 ​ Σ ​ | Z k − Z k ′ | 2 ) ] → 0 . \mathbb{E}[\chi_{k}\chi_{k}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}(\sigma_{k}^{2}\Sigma|Z_{k}-Z_{k}^{\prime}|^{2})]\to 0. The upshot is that the contributions from the decoupled problems for X 0 = i + 1 , … ​ X 0 = m X_{0}=i+1,\ldots X_{0}=m (the ‘bigger scale clusters’) to the sum has norm o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) in the limit. It is worth noting that the o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -estimate here uses only the information on the distribution of X X (ultimately due to the separation of the cluster centres in X X as σ k 2 ​ Σ → + ∞ \sigma_{k}^{2}\Sigma\to+\infty ), but does not require decay property of the functions g i g_{i} that appears in Y Y . On the other hand, conditional on X 0 = 1 , 2 , … ​ i − 1 X_{0}=1,2,\ldots i-1 , since we have replaced Y Y by zero, the corresponding decoupled problem becomes trivial. • Step 3: Analyzing the simpler kernel ridge regression problem. We have reduced to the decoupled minimization problem 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ | Y − f ⁡ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ | Y − f ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( a i + σ i ​ Z i ) ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 . \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|Y-f(UX)|^{2}]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|Y-f(U(a_{i}+\sigma_{i}Z_{i}))|^{2}]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}. By the above discussion, the deviation between the minimizer f U f_{U} of the original kernel ridge regression problem, versus the minimizer f i f_{i} of this decoupled problem, converges to zero in norm. By the translation invariance of the RKHS, the minimum value of the functional does not depend on a i a_{i} . Setting aside a i a_{i} , and viewing U ​ σ i U\sigma_{i} as the scaling parameter, we have thereby removed the dependence of the distribution ( X , Y ) (X,Y) on the extra parameters σ 1 , … ​ σ m \sigma_{1},\ldots\sigma_{m} . We recognize the two limits: 1. As U ​ σ i → + ∞ U\sigma_{i}\to+\infty , namely Σ ​ σ i 2 → + ∞ \Sigma\sigma_{i}^{2}\to+\infty , the minimizing function f i f_{i} satisfies λ ​ ‖ f i ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ | f i ​ ( U ⁡ ( a i + σ i ​ Z i ) ) | 2 ] → 0 . \lambda\left\lVert f_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|f_{i}(U(a_{i}+\sigma_{i}Z_{i}))|^{2}]\to 0. This follows from Corollary and the assumption that Z i Z_{i} is a continuous random variable. 2. As U ​ σ i → 0 U\sigma_{i}\to 0 , namely Σ ​ σ i 2 → 0 \Sigma\sigma_{i}^{2}\to 0 , then again the minimizing function satisfies λ ​ ‖ f i ‖ ℋ 2 + 𝔼 ⁡ [ χ i ​ | f i ​ ( U ⁡ ( a i + σ i ​ Z i ) ) | 2 ] → 0 . \lambda\left\lVert f_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|f_{i}(U(a_{i}+\sigma_{i}Z_{i}))|^{2}]\to 0. To see this, by Corollary and Lemma , this limit of the minimizing function is given by the minimizing function for U = 0 U=0 . But when U = 0 U=0 the minimization depends on Y Y only though its conditional expectation value, which is zero by assumption. Moreover, for U = σ i − 1 U=\sigma_{i}^{-1} ( i.e. OPEN Σ = σ i − 2 ) \Sigma=\sigma_{i}^{-2}) , the decoupled functional becomes 1 2 𝔼 [ χ i | Y − f ( U a i + Z i ) ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 . \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|Y-f(Ua_{i}+Z_{i}))|^{2}]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}. By translation invariance, this minimum coincides with the minimum of 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ | Y − f ⁡ ( Z i ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ f ‖ ℋ 2 , \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\chi_{i}|Y-f(Z_{i})|^{2}\right]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}, which is manifestly independent of the scale parameter σ i \sigma_{i} . Since Y Y is a nonzero function of X X conditional on X 0 = i X_{0}=i , the minimizer is nontrivial. Thus there exists some ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 independent of σ i \sigma_{i} , such that the min value of this decoupled functional has a quantitative gap from its trivial upper bound: J i = min f i ⁡ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ | Y − f i ​ ( U ​ X ) | 2 ] + λ 2 ​ ‖ f i ‖ ℋ 2 < 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ i ​ | Y | 2 ] − 2 ​ ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] . J_{i}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\min_{f_{i}}}}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|Y-f_{i}(UX)|^{2}]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\lVert f_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}<\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|Y|^{2}]-2\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]. • Step 4: Quantitative version. As always we fix Z i Z_{i} , g i g_{i} , λ \lambda , and the choice of kernel, and assume min i ≠ j ⁡ | a i − a j | / σ m \min_{i\neq j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|/\sigma_{m} is bounded uniformly from below. By Steps 1-2, the norm of the deviation between f i f_{i} and the minimizer f U f_{U} of the original kernel ridge regression, converges to zero in the limit. As σ k / σ k + 1 → 0 \sigma_{k}/\sigma_{k+1}\to 0 , 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) = ∑ 1 m J j + o ⁡ ( 1 ) = J i + ∑ j ≠ i 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ χ j ​ | Y | 2 ] + o ⁡ ( 1 ) . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)=\sum_{1}^{m}J_{j}+o(1)=J_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{j}|Y|^{2}]+o(1). Using Step 3, we infer that there exists some η > 0 \eta>0 small enough, such that the following holds when σ i / σ i + 1 < η \sigma_{i}/\sigma_{i+1}<\eta for all i i , and σ i + 1 − 1 ​ σ i − 1 ≤ Σ ≤ σ i − 1 ​ σ i − 1 − 1 \sigma_{i+1}^{-1}\sigma_{i}^{-1}\leq\Sigma\leq\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{i-1}^{-1} , 1. There is a strict gap 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ = σ i − 2 , λ ) ≤ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − 2 ​ ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma=\sigma_{i}^{-2};\lambda)\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-2\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]. 2. There is some Λ \Lambda large enough depending on ϵ \epsilon but not on σ i \sigma_{i} , such that whenever Σ ≤ Λ − 1 ​ σ i − 2 \Sigma\leq\Lambda^{-1}\sigma_{i}^{-2} or Σ ≥ Λ ​ σ i − 2 \Sigma\geq\Lambda\sigma_{i}^{-2} , then 𝒥 ⁡ ( Σ , λ ) ≥ 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] − ϵ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ | Y | 2 ] . \mathcal{J}(\Sigma;\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]-\epsilon\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2}]. By the second point, any ( ϵ , Λ ) (\epsilon,\Lambda) -scale detector within the range σ i + 1 − 1 ​ σ i − 1 ≤ Σ ≤ σ i − 1 ​ σ i − 1 − 1 \sigma_{i+1}^{-1}\sigma_{i}^{-1}\leq\Sigma\leq\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{i-1}^{-1} must be contained in the smaller range Λ − 1 ​ σ i − 2 < Σ < Λ ​ σ i − 2 \Lambda^{-1}\sigma_{i}^{-2}<\Sigma<\Lambda\sigma_{i}^{-2} . The other two situations 0 ≤ Σ ≤ σ m − 1 ​ σ m − 1 − 1 0\leq\Sigma\leq\sigma_{m}^{-1}\sigma_{m-1}^{-1} and Σ ≥ σ 1 − 1 ​ σ 2 − 1 \Sigma\geq\sigma_{1}^{-1}\sigma_{2}^{-1} can be treated similarly (using Step 1, resp. Step 2) , and the Theorem is proved. ∎ Appendix A Discussions: Representation Learning as a Variational Phenomenon In machine learning, representation learning seeks to understand how a learning system can automatically discover meaningful structure in data. This question is often raised in the context of neural networks, whose empirical success is frequently attributed to their ability to learn useful internal representations of the data [ 6 ] . Yet the nature of such representations and the mechanisms responsible for their emergence remain difficult to formulate and analyze rigorously. The purpose of this section is not to develop new mathematical results, but rather to reinterpret the variational analysis developed in this paper through a representation-learning lens. To place the present work in this context, we first recall the standard two-layer neural network model, draw connections to the kernel learning problem studied in this paper, and discuss what the mathematical results we established may suggest from a representation-learning perspective. A standard two-layer neural network model takes the form x ↦ ∑ 1 m a i ​ σ ​ ( ⟨ w i , x ⟩ ) , a i ∈ ℂ , w i ∈ V ∗ x\mapsto\sum_{1}^{m}a_{i}\sigma(\langle w_{i},x\rangle),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ a_{i}\in\mathbb{C},w_{i}\in V^{*} where both the first-layer weights ( w i ) 1 m (w_{i})_{1}^{m} and the second-layer weights ( a i ) 1 m (a_{i})_{1}^{m} are learned from the data. Here σ : ℝ → ℝ \sigma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R} is a prescribed nonlinear activation function, say ReLU ( σ ⁡ ( x ) = max ⁡ ( x , 0 ) \sigma(x)=\max(x,0) ). A central question in the theory of neural networks, and one of the motivating questions behind representation learning, is whether the learned first-layer weights ( w i ) 1 m (w_{i})_{1}^{m} encode meaningful ‘structural information’ about the underlying data distribution. To date, even formulating this question precisely remains subtle. Rigorous evidence has recently been obtained that specific training dynamics can drive learned representations toward the underlying low-dimensional structure in certain highly structured multi-index models [ 2 , 7 , 27 ] . Nevertheless, these works focus on particular optimization dynamics , and a general understanding of which representations are intrinsically preferred by the corresponding population variational problem remains elusive. The difficulty is compounded by the fact that the population variational problem associated with neural network models is typically highly nonlinear, overparameterized, and nonconvex, making its landscape difficult to characterize directly. The kernel learning problem studied in this paper may be viewed as a tractable analogue of a two-layer neural network. Specifically, the predictor takes the form x ↦ f ⁡ ( U ​ x ) , f ∈ ℋ , U ∈ End ⁡ ( V ) x\mapsto f(Ux),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ f\in\mathcal{H},U\in\End(V) where both f ∈ ℋ f\in\mathcal{H} and U ∈ End ⁡ ( V ) U\in\End(V) are learned from the data. Here U U plays a role analogous to the collection of learned first-layer weights ( w i ) 1 m (w_{i})_{1}^{m} in a two-layer neural network, while f f plays the role of a learned second layer. Similar to neural networks, the variational problem associated with the kernel learning model f ⁡ ( U ​ x ) f(Ux) studied in this paper remains nonlinear, overparameterized, and nonconvex. Nevertheless, the two models are mathematically quite different. A principal advantage of the f ⁡ ( U ​ x ) f(Ux) model is that, despite these challenges, its population objective admits a rigorous variational analysis. This makes it possible to study representation-learning phenomena directly through the geometry of the population landscape, rather than through the behavior of a particular optimization algorithm. From this perspective, the present work provides a concrete example in which favorable local minimizers of a two-layer model induce representations encoding meaningful ‘structural information’ of the data, including intrinsic scales, predictive low-dimensional structure, and clustering structure in the underlying data distribution—phenomena that are rigorously characterized in the present paper. These phenomena arise as properties of the population objective itself. In this sense, the analysis addresses a more foundational question: which representations are intrinsically favored by the learning objective, rather than by a particular optimization algorithm. This contrasts with much of the existing neural-network literature, e.g., [ 22 , 26 , 2 , 7 , 27 ] , where representation-learning phenomena are often analyzed through the behavior of specific algorithmic schemes. While we do not claim analogous results for neural networks, these findings suggest that certain forms of representation learning may arise from the geometry of the population landscape itself, and suggest investigating whether similar mechanisms arise in neural networks. It is important to emphasize that the present paper focuses exclusively on the static, variational properties of the kernel learning problem. The dynamical aspects of the model, including the behavior of optimization algorithms and the evolution of learned representations during training, are not addressed here. These questions are investigated from a gradient-flow perspective in the companion paper Gradient Flow in the Kernel Learning Problem [ 28 ] . Readers interested in the broader connection between the present variational analysis and representation learning in two-layer neural networks are strongly encouraged to refer to the postscript of that work, where these connections and their implications for optimization dynamics are discussed in considerably greater detail.
